INTRODUCTION 
         I, the Chairperson of the Committee on External Affairs (2025-26), having been 
authorized by the Committee to submit the Report on their behalf, present this 
Seventeenth Report (18 th Lok Sabha) on the subject ‘India’s role in Global Tech 
Diplomacy, Digital governance, Outer Space Diplomacy, AI, and emerging innovations.’ 
2.  The Committee selected the subject ‘India’s role in Global Tech Diplomacy, Digital 
governance, Outer Space Diplomacy, AI, and emerging innovations’ for detailed 
examination during 2025-26. The representatives of the Ministry of External Affairs briefed 
the Committee on the subject on 17 November 2025. Thereafter, the Committee took 
evidence from the representatives of the Ministry of External Affairs, the Ministry of 
Electronics and Information Technology, and the National Association of Software and 
Services Companies (Nasscom) on 2 February 2026. Following the AI Impact Summit held 
in Delhi from 16 February 2026 to 21 February 2026, the Ministry of External Affairs and 
the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology briefed the Committee on the 
outcomes of the AI Summit on 18 March 2026. Further, in accordance with Rule 331(L) of 
the Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in Lok Sabha, the Committee heard the 
views of experts on the subject, namely Ms. Sangeeta Gupta, Senior Vice President, and 
Shri Shivendra Singh, Vice President, NASSCOM; Shri Chetan Chopra, Head of Legal & 
Public Policy, Ola Krutrim Group; and Professor B. Ravindran, Head, Wadhwani School of 
Data Science and AI, IIT Madras. 
 
3.   The Report was considered and adopted by the Committee at their Sitting held on 22 
September 2026. The Minutes of the Sittings of the Committee are appended to the 
Report. 
4.   The Committee wish to express their gratitude to the Ministry of External Affairs 
Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology and the experts/non-official witnesses 
for placing material information as well as tendering evidence and views before the 
Committee. 
5. The Committee place on record their appreciation for the assistance 
rendered to them by the officials of the Lok Sabha Secretariat attached to the Committee 
6.  For facility of reference, the Observations/Recommendations of the Committee have 
been printed in bold letters in Part-II of the Report. 
 
New Delhi                                         Dr. Shashi Tharoor,                    
22_ September, 2026                                                                  Chairperson,

1 | P a g e 
 
CHAPTER 1 
INTRODUCTION 
In the contemporary world, technology has emerged as a central 
determinant of geopolitics, economic competitiveness, and national 
security. Issues relating to artificial intelligence, data governance, 
semiconductors, cybersecurity, digital trade, and the mobility of skilled 
talent are now integral components of foreign policy. The Ministry of 
External Affairs, entrusted with the primary responsibility for the conduct 
of India's external relations, is increasingly required to negotiate, 
coordinate, and advocate on technology issues that were, until recently, 
regarded as purely technical or domestic regulatory matters. 
1.2 The Committee on External Affairs has accordingly selected the 
subject ‘India's Role in Global Tech Diplomacy, Digital Governance, Outer 
Space Diplomacy, AI and Emerging Innovations’ for detailed 
examination, recognising that technology diplomacy has emerged as a 
distinct and increasingly consequential dimension of India's foreign 
policy. India's own trajectory makes this subject timely and relevant for 
detailed examination and Report. India has built globally recognised 
Digital Public Infrastructure, sustained a cost-effective and 
internationally engaged space programme, and positioned itself as a 
leading voice in shaping global norms for Artificial Intelligence, including 
through the hosting of AI Impact summit and its role in plurilateral 
initiatives. At the same time, India is a party to an expanding 
architecture of bilateral technology partnerships and is an active 
participant in the deliberations of the United Nations, the G20, and other 
groupings on questions of digital governance and emerging technology. 
Scope of the Report 
1.3 At the outset, it is clarified that these aspects of technology cut 
across all Ministries and the Report does not purport to be a 
comprehensive or exhaustive treatment of every aspect of India's 
technology policy across the subjects named in its title. The Report 
confines itself to the foreign policy and diplomatic dimensions of India's 
engagement with technology. It does not undertake a detailed 

2 | P a g e 
 
examination of domestic regulatory or legislative frameworks except to 
the extent that these bear upon India's external commitments, 
negotiating positions or international obligations. The Committee's focus, 
in other words, is on how India engages the world on questions of 
technology rather than on the internal administration of technology 
policy, which falls within the remit of other Departmentally Related 
Standing Committees. Within this broad framework, the Report 
examines issues impinging on four principal themes: 
(i) India Technology Diplomacy and strategy, including the 
institutional architecture, bilateral and plurilateral partnerships 
(through which India advances its technology interests abroad. 
(ii) Use of India’s Digital Public Infrastructure as an instrument of 
diplomacy and development partnership, particularly the Global 
South.  
(iii) India’s Outer Space Diplomacy which includes India's bilateral and 
multilateral space cooperation, its engagement with frameworks 
such as the Artemis Accords and the UN Committee on the 
Peaceful Uses of Outer Space (COPUOS), and the use of space 
capability as an instrument of regional and global diplomacy; and 
(iv) India's role in shaping international AI governance norms, its 
participation in bodies such as the Global Partnership on Artificial 
Intelligence (GPAI), and its diplomatic engagement on frontier 
technologies including semiconductors, quantum computing and 
critical minerals. 
1.5 Although the Report examines global technology diplomacy, digital 
governance, outer space diplomacy, and artificial intelligence as distinct 
chapters, the Committee wish to emphasise that these four pillars are 
closely interlinked and are increasingly treated as such in India's actual 
diplomatic practice. Partnerships originally framed around one theme 
routinely extend into the others. The diagram below illustrates this 
interlinkage.

1.6 For facility of reference, the Report has been organised in two 
parts: Part I (Narration) and Part II (Recommendations/ Observations by 
the Committee on External Affairs). Part-I contains six chapters. 
Following this introductory chapter , Chapters 2 to 5 examine, in turn, 
India's global technology diplomacy, digital governance diplomacy, outer 
space diplomacy and artificial intelligence diplomacy. Part II presents the 
Committee's consolidated observations and recommendations on the 
subject.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

4 | P a g e 
 
CHAPTER 2 
INDIA’S TECH DIPLOMACY 
 
Foreign policy has traditionally been organised around the 
instruments of diplomacy, trade and security. Over the past decade, 
technology has established itself as another crucial instrument, woven 
directly into how states negotiate, compete and align with one another. 
For India, a country whose economy is among the most digitally 
advanced in scale and whose technology services sector reaches every 
major market in the world, this evolution carries particular significance: 
India's ability to safeguard its strategic autonomy, sustain its economic 
growth, and secure a voice in the norms that will govern these 
technologies globally depends substantially on how effectively it 
conducts this newer strand of diplomacy. 
 
I. DEFINING TECH DIPLOMACY AND STRATEGY 
2.1 Broadly speaking, tech diplomacy refers to the conduct of a 
country's external relations, bilateral, minilateral, and multilateral, with 
particular reference to technology as either the subject of negotiation 
(as in semiconductor or AI partnerships), the instrument of diplomacy 
(as in DPI export or space-based development cooperation), or the 
terrain on which strategic competition and cooperation between States is 
increasingly conducted (as in standard-setting bodies and technology-
related export control regimes). Global tech diplomacy is described as 
the art and practice of shaping international cooperation, competition, 
and rule-making around technology in order to advance national, 
economic, and societal interests. When asked to define Tech Diplomacy, 
the Ministry of External Affairs in a written reply stated that it refers to 
pursuing and harnessing international cooperation/partnership and 
diplomatic efforts to guide how emerging and critical technologies are 
developed, governed and used for strategic interests and development 
goals. 
2.2 The Committee were keen to know the manner in which India is 
integrating technology into its foreign policy objectives and how the 

5 | P a g e 
 
ministry conceptualizes India’s role in global tech diplomacy. To this the 
Ministry responded that India is integrating technology into its foreign 
policy priorities by giving it a central place in its engagements with other 
countries. The Government has been laying particular emphasis on the 
three Ts framework - Trade, Technology, and Tourism, in its foreign 
policy approach and engagement. For example, initiatives like the India–
US TRUST, the India-UK Technology Security Initiative, the India-EU 
Trade and Technology Council, show how technology is becoming a key 
pillar of cooperation. In line with a series of domestic initiatives, the 
Ministry is actively pursuing engagements and partnerships in the 
technology domain, as a force for larger good and a strong voice for the 
Global South in the spirit of ‘Vasudhaiva Kutumbakam’. 
2.3 Highlighting the significance of technology as an instrument of 
Foreign Policy, the representatives of the Ministry of External Affairs 
during briefing held on 17 November 2025 submitted as under: 
“…technology is not merely a sectoral concern. It is a central pillar 
of diplomacy, governance, national security, and economic growth. 
As you mentioned, the global technology landscape remains very 
competitive. The pace of technological advances that we are 
witnessing is unprecedented. Recognising these shifts, India has 
consciously integrated technology into its foreign policy priorities. 
Our approach is customized, and our key objectives include 
identifying the specific technologies that India needs, and from 
where and which countries; identifying technologies India has 
developed, like DPI, and that we can share with the members of 
the Global South; facilitating and strengthening linkages not just 
between the Governments, but also between industry and the 
academia; and finally, effective participation in technology, 
governance, debate, and dialogue that is happening today in both 
bilateral and multilateral format, and ensuring that India is 
participating in the discussion on norms that may emerge in the 
technology domain in the future.  
India has endeavoured to strategically position itself as a 
trusted global partner, committed to harnessing transformative 

6 | P a g e 
 
technologies for inclusive development. Our approach remains 
anchored in ensuring democratization of technology, capacity 
building for the Global South, and safeguarding strategic interest 
through robust regulatory frameworks and partnership. India has 
rolled out open-source platforms at a billion-user scale that not 
only empowers millions domestically, but also serve as a replicable 
template for the Global South, seeking to build resilient digital 
ecosystems.  
2.4 India's strategy in global technology domain is defined by a 
commitment to  harness strategic technologies while simultaneously 
building its role as a trusted  global partner. This approach is 
institutionalized through bilateral, plurilateral, and  multilateral 
frameworks focusing on the interface of technology, trade and security.  
Furthermore, India's diplomatic posture includes working together with 
partners in the  Global South, including through capacity building 
initiatives and sharing of best practices and digital solutions. 
2.5 When the Committee desired to know if there is a clearly 
articulated strategy that integrates digital governance, AI, cyber issues, 
and space diplomacy within India’s broader foreign policy framework, 
the Ministry in their written submission replied that India is progressively 
developing an integrated approach that brings together digital 
governance, artificial intelligence, cyber issues and outer space within its 
broader foreign policy and national development vision, including the 
objectives of Viksit Bharat 2047. While there is no single, stand-alone 
policy document that consolidates all these strands, the strategic 
direction is reflected across sectoral missions, inter-ministerial 
coordination mechanisms and India’s positions in key bilateral, 
plurilateral and multilateral engagements. 
2.6 In addition, domestically, flagship initiatives such as Digital India, 
the IndiaAI Mission, the Indian Space Policy 2023 etc., provide the policy 
backbone for India’s external engagements in these domains. 
Internationally, India has established dedicated mechanisms such as the 
India-US TRUST initiative, the India–EU Trade and Technology Council 
and the India–UK Technology and Security Initiative which explicitly 

7 | P a g e 
 
integrate cooperation on critical and emerging technologies, digital 
public infrastructure, cyber security, AI, semiconductors and space. 
These arrangements ensure that technology, security and development 
considerations are addressed in a mutually reinforcing manner. 
 
2.7 When asked about the manner in which India ensures that its 
partnerships in the technology domain align with its national security 
interests, especially with regard to sensitive technologies like AI, 
semiconductors, and quantum computing, the Ministry stated that  India 
ensures that its national security interests are protected and pursued 
while engaging with foreign partners in sensitive technology areas such 
as AI, semiconductors and quantum computing. These partnerships are, 
inter-alia, guided by trust and security considerations. Relevant 
Ministries are involved from the initial stages of discussions to ensure 
there are no gaps. Some of the bilateral dialogues, given the importance 
of tech diplomacy, are steered at the level of the National Security 
Adviser (NSA). For instance, NSA leads the dialogue mechanism with US 
& UK (India–US TRUST, India–UK Technology Security Initiative).  
 
2.8 Regarding the ways in which India's tech diplomacy acts as a tool 
for achieving its ‘Viksit Bharat 2047’ vision, the Ministry of External 
Affairs stated that India’s technology diplomacy supports the Viksit 
Bharat 2047 vision by linking domestic technology missions with 
international partnerships. Major initiatives such as the India AI Mission, 
Green Hydrogen Mission, Quantum Mission, Semiconductor Mission, 
Super Computing Mission etc., have strong international components. 
MEA facilitates these engagements and coordinates global cooperation. 
For instance, NEST Division is the nodal Division for the BioE3 policy and 
National Green Hydrogen Mission to anchor its international 
coordination. Through such efforts, India leverages global partnerships, 
investments, and knowledge sharing to strengthen domestic capabilities 
and achieve the goals of Viksit Bharat. 
2.9 As regards reforms undertaken within India’s domestic R&D, 
regulatory and innovation ecosystem so that our diplomatic gains 
translate into concrete technological outcomes, the Ministry of External 

8 | P a g e 
 
Affairs stated that India is undertaking a suite of structural reforms in 
R&D, regulation, and innovation governance to overcome domestic 
bottlenecks, such as slow approvals, fragmented funding, weak industry- 
academia links, talent attrition, and uncertain regulation and to ensure 
that high-level technology diplomacy, including iCET, India-EU Trade and 
Technology Council (TTC), and QUAD tech cooperation to facilitate 
industrial and technological engagements on the ground.  
2.10  To address systemic constraints like bureaucratic delays in 
clearances, low R&D intensity and poor IP/commercialisation support, 
India is pursuing a liberalisation of research through the Anusandhan 
National Research Foundation (ANRF), which aims to professionalise 
R&D funding and raise public and private R&D spending to 2% of GDP 
by 2030, while an ‘ease of doing research’ package is slashing delays by 
devolving procurement and financial powers to institutions and fast-
tracking project approvals. India is also revitalising the Technology 
Development Board (TDB), which now plays a key role in funding 
technology demonstration, scaling up deep-tech startups, and de-risking 
industry participation in cutting-edge fields like semiconductors, AI, 
quantum, and green hydrogen, thereby bridging the gap between 
laboratory R&D and commercial deployment. 
2.11 Alongside this, reforms are strengthening research autonomy, 
long-term research grants to attract and retain talent, and synchronising 
domestic regulatory frameworks with global tech partnerships. National 
AI regulatory guidelines are being developed to enable rules; standards 
and certification in telecom, electronics, and critical tech are being 
harmonised with partners; and Startup India 2.0, along with robust data 
governance and common data platforms like India AI’s dataset hub, is 
being used to strengthen the domestic innovation ecosystem and 
accelerate cross-border R&D.  
2.12 India is leveraging flagship missions, such as the India 
Semiconductor Mission (ISM) with ₹26,000 crore in support for fabs, 
OSAT, and domestic supply chains, and the India AI Mission (₹10,372 
crore, 2024-28) with a seven-pillar architecture for compute, data, skills, 
and sovereign AI stacks, so that technologies co-developed in TRUST 

9 | P a g e 
 
and other tech dialogues are absorbed into Indian industry, startups, 
and governance. 
2.13 On the question of alignment of major domestic initiatives such as 
India AI Mission, Digital India, and semiconductor policies with India’s 
international technology diplomacy, the Ministry stated that India’s major 
domestic initiatives are aligned with its international technology 
diplomacy through global engagement and partnerships. For example, 
India hosted the India AI Impact Summit in 2026, and under Digital 
India, it promoted Digital Public Infrastructure during its G20 Presidency. 
India is also working with partners like the Japan, Netherlands, 
Singapore, South Korea and UK in related areas. These examples show 
that India’s domestic policies and missions are closely linked with its 
international technology engagements. 
2.14 On being asked about the measures taken by the Government to 
build India as a trusted global partner in tech diplomacy, the Ministry 
stated that India is emerging as a trusted global partner in technology 
diplomacy through several initiatives. It hosted the India AI Impact 
Summit 2026, reflecting its leadership in responsible AI. India has 
actively contributed to global discussions such as the UN Global Digital 
Compact. It is also sharing its Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI) with 
partner countries, positioning it as a global public good. India also leads 
the Coalition for Disaster Resilient Infrastructure (CDRI), the Global 
Biofuel Alliance (GBA) and the International Solar Alliance (ISA). In 
addition, capacity-building initiatives such as MEA's ITEC programme 
help strengthen partnerships and build goodwill. These efforts 
demonstrate India's role as a trusted and reliable technology partner 
globally. 
2.15 Regarding the key challenges faced in advancing India’s 
technology diplomacy and the mechanisms put in place to address these 
challenges, the Ministry stated that India faces challenges in technology 
diplomacy due to global uncertainties including geopolitical tensions, 
conflict situations, export controls, and restrictions on dual-use 
technologies, alongside complex patent regimes and varying legal 
frameworks. To ensure coherence, the Government is strengthening 

10 | P a g e 
 
Inter-Ministerial coordination and moving from a Whole-of-Government 
to a Whole-of-Society approach by involving industry, academia, and 
think tanks. India is also taking steps to better understand global trends, 
by developing capacities in horizon scanning. These measures help in 
managing challenges and strengthening India’s technology diplomacy. 
 
II. INSTITUTIONAL ARCHITECTURE 
2.16 As can be seen from the definition, tech diplomacy is inherently a 
multi-stakeholder domain within the Government. Several Ministries and 
Departments play a direct and continuing role. Ministry of External 
Affairs (MEA) is the nodal ministry and leads on global tech diplomacy, 
other ministries/departments play supporting or sector-specific roles in 
international tech cooperation. Within the Ministry of External Affairs, 
the New, Emerging and Strategic Technologies (NEST) and Cyber 
Diplomacy Divisions were set up to contribute to the task of India’s tech 
diplomacy. They are supported by other relevant Divisions of the 
Ministry, including Territorial Divisions. 
2.17 Elaborating the ways in which Indian Diaspora can contribute to 
our efforts in tech diplomacy, the representative of MEA during 
testimony on 17 November 2026 submitted: 
“…in terms of engaging first of all with the Indian diaspora, we 
have a very large diaspora, as you know very well, and one of the 
priorities for Government of India and MEA is to engage the Indian 
diaspora who has expertise, who has knowledge. Not that he has 
to move to India to contribute to India's development, but how 
can he, sitting in Stanford or Massachusetts or Michigan or in 
London or Canada, can contribute to India's growth and 
development journey. In the domain of technology, we have 
created a platform called PRABHAS, Pravasi Bhartiya Academic and 
Scientific Sampark. This is a platform developed by DST and the 
science ministries, CSIR and DST, and MEA is also involved in that. 
Since I have served in USA, so I know that we used to engage 
with the diaspora, which is over five-million strong in the US itself, 
to engage with this platform, submit their ideas, information, 

11 | P a g e 
 
register themselves, and we try to tap into those resources for our 
education programmes, our science and technology programmes, 
or their participation in CSIR activities. So, it is a very vibrant 
platform, that we have set up, and we are very happy that there is 
an interest from Indian diaspora in that.” 
2.18 On the question of cooperation with State Governments, the 
representative of MEA on 17 November 2025 stated: 
“We have a very focussed attention on how we, as MEA, work with 
the State Governments. We have opened up a new division for 
some years now called states. I know for the fact that a lot of 
activity in investment or technologies is happening in the States. 
So, through our Branch Secretariat Offices/RPOs, we have some 
officers who are now on lien to State Governments. So, we are 
working very closely with the State Governments. “ 
 
2.19 The Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY) has 
been at the forefront of driving digital transformation in governance and 
public service delivery. Through strategic policymaking, creation of 
enabling digital infrastructure, and implementation of mission-mode 
projects, MeitY has institutionalized digital governance across all tiers of 
government, from the Centre to the States and Union Territories. MeitY’s 
approach emphasises “whole-of-government” digital transformation, 
interoperability, and citizen-centricity, while ensuring trust, transparency, 
and accountability. India’s digital governance ecosystem has been 
strengthened through a range of flagship programmes and platforms 
that have transformed service delivery across sectors.  
2.20 Although MeitY is not formally mandated to undertake Global Tech 
Diplomacy, in alignment with the Government’s emphasis on digital 
diplomacy, the digital economy, and the launch of the Digital India 
Programme, MeitY has proactively synergised its efforts to expand 
India’s IT and electronics ecosystem globally. These efforts focus on 
diversification of geographies, strengthening domain expertise, and 
leveraging a highly skilled workforce to enhance international business 
opportunities. MeitY has been actively working to build strategic 

12 | P a g e 
 
alliances with foreign partners, especially in the emerging areas of 
Information and communications technology (ICT), through bilateral and 
multilateral cooperation. MeitY regularly engages with various foreign 
governments, academic institutions, and industry bodies to develop 
partnerships that are mutually beneficial, promoting the exchange of 
expertise, experience sharing and fostering innovation.  
2.21 During 2025-26, the International Cooperation Division (ICD) of 
MeitY has taken the lead in driving these initiatives, focusing on key 
areas such as: 
● Supporting National Goals: Ensuring that all international 
collaborations align with key government initiatives like 'Digital 
India' and 'Make in India,' which are central to India’s long-term 
digital transformation and manufacturing objectives. Engaging in 
Global Forums:  MeitY has strengthened India’s presence and 
leadership across key international platforms to advance national 
interests and contribute to global digital discourse. It has been 
actively engaging in global forums to address priorities such as 
digital transformation, bridging digital and gender divides, and 
strengthening digital governance and Digital Public Infrastructure. 
These engagements involve collaboration across forums and 
organisations including the G20, SCO, BRICS, ASEAN, IPEF, QUAD, 
WTO, United Nations, World Bank, G7, ADB, BIMSTEC, 
Commonwealth, IPU, ITU, SAARC, OECD, and other relevant 
international platforms. 
● Through sustained participation in these forums, India has 
showcased its digital achievements, fostered strategic partnerships 
with global stakeholders, and enhanced its role in shaping the 
global digital economy. 
● Facilitating International Industry Collaboration: Creating an 
environment that encourages cooperation between Indian 
industries and their international counterparts to foster innovation 
and joint development in cutting-edge ICT solutions. 

13 | P a g e 
 
● Encouraging R&D Initiatives: Supporting international research and 
development partnerships to accelerate advancements in ICT 
applications, thereby contributing to the development of 
groundbreaking digital technologies. 
● Coordinating with Global Institutions: Collaborating with 
international organizations such as the UN agencies (including 
UNCSTD, UNCITRAL, UNESCO, UNCTAD, UNDP, ECOSOC, and 
ESCAP), WSIS, World Bank, WTO, ADB, and the World Economic 
Forum (WEF). These partnerships help safeguard India’s interests 
and ensure that the country’s priorities are represented in global 
ICT policies and discussions. 
● Spearheading Collaborative Projects: Launching joint projects with 
global partners, including establishing IT institutes, software 
development parks, digital solutions and initiating joint R&D 
ventures. 
● Promoting India’s ICT Expertise: Showcasing India’s ICT strength 
across various international forums by organising, sponsoring and 
participating in trade fairs, symposiums, exhibitions etc. 
● Aligning foreign collaboration activities in India’s ‘Digital India 
program’ and ‘Make in India’ initiatives of the Government of India.  
● Creating a conducive environment for international cooperation to 
help industries to cooperate with the industries of other countries.  
● Fostering, encouraging and promoting research and development 
in the application of information technology related facilities.  
● Initiating joint projects like IT institutes, software parks, 
programmes for joint R&D and facilitating IT Advisers etc. 
● Showcasing India’s ICT strength across the globe by organising, 
sponsoring and participating in trade fairs, symposiums, 
exhibitions etc. 
2.22 To achieve these objectives, the ICD, MeitY engages through 
various mechanisms, such as Memorandums of Understanding (MoUs), 
Joint Working Group (JWG) meetings, Free Trade Agreement (FTA) and 

14 | P a g e 
 
multilateral deliberations/negotiations in the ICT, digital economy and 
digital trade areas. The ICD also leads India’s involvement in key 
international events to highlight the potential of Indian IT and software 
industries. Moreover, the division actively works to address challenges 
related to the export of Indian IT services and the mobility of IT 
professionals, representing these issues at global forums. Through these 
efforts, MeitY aims to strengthen India’s position as a key player in the 
global digital economy, promoting its ICT capabilities while ensuring that 
the country’s interests and expertise are well-reflected in international 
collaborations. 
2.23 When asked about the Ministries involved in India’s tech diplomacy 
and how inter-Ministerial coordination function in these domains, the 
Ministry stated that all Ministries/Departments that deal with specific 
technology sectors or are nodal for such technologies are involved in 
India’s technology diplomacy. These Ministries engage internationally in 
their respective domains, with MEA participating, wherever required. 
Inter-Ministerial coordination is carried out through existing mechanisms 
within the Government, with each Ministry having a sui generis process 
for coordination and consultation. For example, the Ministry is 
represented on the Advisory Group on Border Management. NEST 
Division is also designated as the nodal Division for international 
engagements in BioE3 Policy (By Department of Biotechnology) and 
Green Hydrogen Mission (by Ministry of New and Renewable Energy). 
 
2.24 The Committee further enquired about capacity gaps within the 
diplomatic corps with respect to AI and emerging technologies along 
with measures taken to bridge these gaps. In a written reply the Ministry 
submitted that given the pace of technological change and the growing 
centrality of digital technologies, AI and other emerging technologies in 
foreign policy, India, like most countries, faces an ongoing requirement 
to continuously upgrade domain knowledge and skills within its 
diplomatic corps. The challenge is less of a static gap and more of a 
dynamic need to build, update and retain specialized expertise across 
multiple fast-evolving technology domains. 

15 | P a g e 
 
2.25 To address this, the Ministry of External Affairs has taken several 
steps including the creation of specialized divisions such as the New, 
Emerging and Strategic Technologies (NEST) Division and the Cyber 
Diplomacy (CD) Division, which has enabled the Ministry to develop 
diplomatic expertise on AI, cyber issues, critical and emerging 
technologies and digital governance, and to serve as a focal point for all 
aspects of international capacity building, policy formulation and inter-
ministerial coordination. It may be noted that a few Indian Missions in 
the USA, UK etc., also have dedicated officers looking after technology 
related issues and developments. 
2.26 In addition, the Sushma Swaraj Institute of Foreign Service 
(SSIFS) regularly incorporates modules on technology diplomacy, AI, 
cyber issues and digital public infrastructure into its foundational and 
mid-career training programmes. Officers are also nominated for 
specialized courses and workshops conducted in collaboration with 
domestic institutions (such as IITs, IISc and policy think tanks) as well 
as foreign training institutions and multilateral platforms focusing on AI, 
cyber and digital governance. 
2.27 MEA also works closely with line Ministries/Departments such as 
MeitY, Department of Telecommunications, Department of Space, 
Department of Science and Technology, Office of Principal Scientific 
Adviser among others to draw on their technical expertise for 
negotiations and dialogues. Regular consultations with industry, 
start-ups, academia, think tanks and civil society are also used as a 
means of updating officers on latest technological trends, applications 
and regulatory debates, thereby bridging the knowledge gap between 
diplomatic practice and frontier technologies. 
2.28 Further, active involvement in forums such as G20, BRICS, GPAI, 
the UN Global Digital Compact process, Global mechanism on ICTs, 
multilateral export-control and relevant standards-setting bodies also 
provide Indian diplomats with continuous exposure to global best 
practices and evolving normative frameworks on AI and emerging 
technologies. 
 

16 | P a g e 
 
Role of Technology Services Sector 
2.29 The technology services sector has played a pivotal role in 
reshaping India’s positioning globally – a hub for high skilled talent, 
magnet for global companies to set up their technology and operations 
centres in India, Indian origin leaders as CEOs of global technology 
companies and strong partnership with Fortune 500 companies that 
embed India as critical part of their tech supply chain. India’s technology 
sector in FY2025 was estimated at $283 billion, almost 7% relative to 
India’s GDP and largest service exporter for the country. The sector 
employs almost 5.8 million people directly and has supported indirect 
employment for over 10-12 million. 
2.30 India’s technology industry is uniquely positioned for global tech 
diplomacy due to its scale and deep integration into global digital value 
chains. Indian IT services, engineering, and digital platforms are 
embedded in the operations of governments, enterprises, and critical 
infrastructure across major economies. This long-standing presence has 
built trust, operational credibility, and institutional familiarity with global 
regulatory environments. As a result, Indian technology firms are seen 
not merely as vendors but as long-term partner, enabling India to 
engage credibly in international discussions on technology governance 
and digital trade. This embeddedness allows industry perspectives to 
inform diplomatic engagements with practical, implementation-oriented 
insights, an increasingly important requirement in global tech 
negotiations. 
2.31 Recognising the significant role played by the technology services 
sector in the tech space, the Committee on External Affairs took 
evidence of two industry stakeholders: NASSCOM and Krutrim. 
NASSCOM is an active player in converting this diplomatic engagement 
into market access, innovation partnerships, and global influence. In 
practice, NASSCOM’s global tech diplomacy includes:  
 Hosting international platforms where governments, regulators, 
and companies deliberate on tech issues and trade.  
 Building bilateral dialogue forums that complement traditional 
government diplomacy  

17 | P a g e 
 
 Long-term business partnerships and market access initiatives for 
Indian tech firms.  
  Engaging in policy and standards discussions that affect global 
digital economies.  
 Establishing Institutionalized Track-1.5 mechanisms to advance 
technology diplomacy through formation of high-profile country 
forums that enable structured, principal-level engagement 
between industry leaders, governments, academia, and other 
ecosystem partners. These platforms translate strategic, trade, and 
foreign policy objectives into actionable collaboration, much on the 
lines of the goals of FTAs, and position the Indian technology 
industry as a trusted strategic partner with proven capabilities in 
scaling AI adoption, digital infrastructure, and mission-critical 
initiatives aligned with national priorities 
2.32 Elaborating on the role played by this sector in India’s tech space, 
the representative of NASSCOM submitted during evidence on 2 
February 2026 as under: 
“If you look at our industry, we are roughly about a 280-290 billion 
sector, which is very export focused. So, almost 70-75 per cent of 
our business is export-led. The U.S. has always been our largest 
export market. About 60 per cent of our industry is exporting to 
the U.S. I think our focus in the U.S. market, not just under the 
current administration but even in the past, has been to build a 
platform where you can have almost a Track 1.5 diplomacy. This 
involves bringing together different groups of people, such as 
Government, industry, policymakers, think tanks, and industry 
professionals on a common platform to create and discuss many of 
these issues. 
Recently, in the last year, we created the NASSCOM U.S. CEO 
Forum. Secretary Christian spoke at this platform a few months 
back, and I think it is creating an engagement where it is not seen 
as being only about the H1B worker issue, which a lot of our 
industry gets tagged with, but a much broader platform where you 

18 | P a g e 
 
can discuss what is needed to grow tech collaboration between 
both markets, particularly in areas of emerging technology... 
The second focus for us has been to do more evidence-based 
advocacy in the U.S. In particular , Indian IT is often seen as taking 
away jobs from the U.S., so we have partnered with U.S. market 
analyst firms to showcase how Indian IT has actually been creating 
jobs in the U.S. Our last report, which was published a few years 
back, showed that we have created 200,000 net new jobs in the 
U.S. and added to the GDP of the U.S. market. It is a positive win-
win relationship on both sides, and it is not a one-way trade, as is 
often perceived. 
You are also seeing many of our member companies actively 
involved in CSR activities, local skilling, local community projects, 
and train-the-teacher programmes. The idea is really to make us 
more local in those markets, and not just be perceived as an 
exporter of services. We are also driving a whole India-U.S. startup 
partnership, because we believe that startups are the engine of 
innovation. Whether it is taking delegations from India to the U.S., 
working with missions to bring together different stakeholders who 
can engage with these startups, or the other way around when U.S. 
companies want to understand what innovation is happening in 
India, we are creating platforms to support this engagement.” 
 
III. BILATERAL TECHNOLOGY ENGAGEMENTS 
2.33 India's technology diplomacy rests upon a widening set of bilateral 
and multilateral engagements. At the bilateral level, India-US TRUST 
Initiative (Transforming the Relationship Utilizing Strategic Technology) 
facilitates government-to-government, academic, and private sector 
cooperation across areas like AI, semiconductors, quantum, energy, 
critical minerals, and space, with a central focus on ensuring trusted 
vendors and supply-chain security. Complementing this, the India-EU 
Trade & Technology Council (TTC), established in 2022, provides 
guidance through working groups dedicated to strategic technologies, 
digital governance, clean energy, critical minerals and resilient supply 

19 | P a g e 
 
chains. The India-UK Technology & Security Initiative (TSI) similarly 
promotes joint work streams on future telecoms, co-investment, AI, 
critical minerals and securing supply chains. India has expanded its 
engagement on technology matters with several other partners 
countries, including on cyber, AI, critical minerals, defence, space, and 
clean energy.  
 
2.34 Regarding India’s bilateral level engagements, the representative 
of MEA on 17 November 2025 briefed the Committee as under: 
“At the bilateral level, sir, let me outline some of the major 
initiatives that define government tech diplomacy and tech 
engagement. You mentioned in your opening remarks about our 
engagement with U.S. Certainly, with U.S. we have a very robust 
tech partnership and initiative, namely Critical and Emerging 
Technology. This establishes a very high-level framework led by 
two NSAs to accelerate cooperation in AI, semiconductors, 
quantum, biotechnology, defence innovation, advanced 
telecommunications, space, through co-production, co-
development, and joint R&D. Building on this momentum of the 
ICET Initiative, the Transforming Relation Utilizing Strategic 
Technology Initiative was launched only this year, and now anchors 
our shared focus on trusted supply chains, technology protection, 
and export control reforms. 
Cooperation in strategic technology is an important 
component of our bilateral collaboration with the European Union 
and European countries. The India-EU Trade and Technology 
Council and India-UK Technology Security Initiative serve as a key 
platform to address challenges at the confluence of trade, trusted 
technology, and security, including semiconductors, artificial 
intelligence, digital governance, green and clean energy 
technologies, biotechnology, and resilient value chains. 
India has expanded its engagement on technology matters 
with a number of countries such as Russia, Japan, Germany, 
Netherlands, the Republic of Korea, Australia, and Israel, in several 

20 | P a g e 
 
domains such as cyber, clean technology, AI, defence, space, 
critical minerals, biotechnology, and the whole domain of 
sustainability. These partnerships are not isolated. They are 
complementary parts of India's broader vision to position itself as 
a trusted global innovation and tech hub. They also underscore the 
strength that India brings to the global table, a combination of 
technological capability, a large talent base, democratic 
governance, upward-looking economic growth trajectory, and a 
proven record in building digital systems that empower citizens.” 
 
A. India and United States 
2.35 Regarding India-US bilateral ties in this domain, the Ministry of 
External Affairs in a written submission stated that US accounts for over 
60% of India’s technology service exports.  
2.36 U.S.- India TRUST Initiative: During the Prime Minister’s visit 
to the United States in February 2025, both sides formally announced 
the launch of the U.S.-India TRUST (Transforming the Relationship 
Utilizing Strategic Technology) initiative. TRUST aims to catalyse 
collaboration among governments, industry, academia, and research 
institutions to advance cooperation in defence, artificial intelligence, 
semiconductors, quantum technologies, biotechnology, energy, and 
space, while promoting the use of trusted and verified technology 
vendors and ensuring the protection of sensitive technologies. TRUST 
replaced the India-U.S. Initiative on Critical and Emerging Technology 
(iCET), which was launched in May 2022 by PM Modi and then United 
States President Joe Biden.  
2.37 Under iCET, the NSAs of India and the US engaged regularly in a 
high-level dialogue through extensive discussions on a broad bilateral, 
regional and global agenda. The focus areas under iCET included AI, 
Quantum Computing, Semiconductors, Advanced Telecommunications, 
Biotechnology, Defence and Space. Under iCET, there have been two 
meetings at the NSA level. The first meeting was held in Washington 
D.C. in 2023 and the second meeting took place in New Delhi in 2024.  
Strategic cooperation across key technology sectors including space, 

21 | P a g e 
 
semiconductors, advanced telecommunications, artificial intelligence, 
quantum, STEM, biotechnology, critical minerals and clean energy was 
the focus of discussion in these meetings.  
2.38 TRUST initiative is expected to catalyse government-to-
government, academia and private sector collaboration to promote the 
application of critical and emerging technologies in areas like defense, 
artificial intelligence, semiconductors, quantum, biotechnology, energy 
and space, while encouraging the use of verified technology vendors and 
ensuring sensitive technologies are protected.  
  
2.39 Key initiatives proposed within TRUST include: 
(i) Roadmap for Accelerating AI Infrastructure: Agreements with the 
private sector to accelerate India’s AI buildout, powered by 
increased bilateral energy trade, and relying on U.S. origin 
technologies. 
(ii) Pharmaceutical Supply Chains/API Manufacturing: Secure a private 
sector commitment to expand Indian manufacturing of Active 
Pharmaceutical Ingredients (API) in the United States to create 
jobs, bolster supply chain resilience, and improve health security 
for India and the United States. 
(iii) Supply Chain Resilience : Secure private sector investments and 
build new government and industry partnerships to diversity and 
strengthen semiconductor and critical mineral supply chains. 
(iv) INDUS-X: Leveraging the India-U.S. Defense Acceleration 
Ecosystem (INDUS-X), forge additional partnerships to produce 
critical military capability between U.S. and Indian defense firms, 
investors, and universities. 
2.40 Lines of Effort under TRUST: 
(i) Strategic Mineral Recovery Initiative (SMRI): India and US will 
intensify efforts to deepen cooperation in the exploration, 
beneficiation, and processing as well as recycling technologies of 
critical minerals as part of the SMRI, a new U.S.-India program to 
recover and process critical minerals (including lithium, cobalt, and 

22 | P a g e 
 
rare earths) from heavy industries like aluminium, coal mining and 
oil and gas. 
(ii) Commercial Space : India and US will collaborate on space 
exploration, including long duration human spaceflight missions, 
spaceflight safety and sharing of expertise and professional 
exchanges in emerging areas, including planetary protection. 
Commercial space collaboration will also be pursued through 
industry engagements in conventional and emerging areas, such 
as connectivity, advanced spaceflight, satellite and space launch 
systems, space sustainability, space tourism and advanced space 
manufacturing. 
(iii) U.S. NSF – Indian Anusandhan National Research Foundation 
(ANRF) partnership:  This partnership builds on ongoing 
collaboration between the U.S. National Science Foundation and 
several Indian science agencies to enable joint research in the 
areas of semiconductors, connected vehicles, machine learning, 
next-generation telecommunications, intelligent transportation 
systems, and future biomanufacturing. 
(iv) Advanced Materials cooperation : The 2nd India-US iCET meeting 
held in June 2024 launched US-India Advanced Materials R&D 
Forum (AMF) with focus on 4 areas: co-development of Advanced 
Materials (AM), access to technology transfer on AM, address 
regulatory challenges, and bridging the gap between industry, 
academia in this sector. A Track 1.5 dialogue of the US-India 
Advanced Materials R&D Forum (AMF) was held in New Delhi on 
14 November 2024, focusing on AM in clean energy. These 
discussions are expected to be taken forward under TRUST. 
(v) Quantum:  Under iCET, the India-US Quantum Coordination 
Meeting was held on 27 August 2024 in Washington DC. The two 
sides have been discussing how India can leverage quantum 
testing facilities in the USA, and potential co-operations between 
National labs, Industries, and startups of both the countries and 
the mobility of relevant experts. These discussions are expected to 
be taken forward under TRUST. 

23 | P a g e 
 
2.41 Meetings held under TRUST: 
(i) During the visit of the Foreign Secretary and Deputy National 
Security Advisor to Washington D.C. on 28–29 May 2025, 
discussions were held focused on advancing the TRUST initiative. 
(ii) A delegation led by Senior Advisor at the Cyber and Digital Policy 
Bureau at the US Department of State Alka Patel visited India on 8 
September 2025 for discussions on artificial intelligence, advanced 
materials, rare earths, and pharmaceuticals, under TRUST . 
(iii) Senior Bureau Official for the Bureau of Cyberspace and Digital 
Policy Russell C. Headlee visited India on 10 December 2025, to 
discuss the TRUST initiative, preparations for the upcoming AI 
Impact Summit and broader issues related to cyberspace and 
digital policy. 
2.42 In their written submission, Nasscom highlighted a significant 
aspect of India’s technology engagement with the United States, 
reinforcing that India-US Tech Partnership has been mutually beneficial 
for both markets: US industry has been a key contributor to India’s tech 
services sector growth, and these facts are well documented. However, 
there is much less awareness on Indian tech sector’s contribution to the 
US, and the media narrative is mostly focussed on low-cost jobs being 
shipped to India or H-1B workers taking away American jobs. Nasscom 
partnered with HIS Markit, an independent research company now part 
of the S&P group to build a data backed analysis of India’s contribution 
to the US economy. The report published in 2021-22 showcased that in 
2021 the Indian technology industry directly generated $103 billion of 
revenue (32% growth since 2017) and directly employed 207,000 people 
in the US (22% growth since 2017). The direct activity of both 
operational and investment spending, as well as supporting their client 
base, rippled through the economy and generated $198 billion in 
contribution to US GDP, and supported 1.6 million jobs for the US 
economy in 2021. This GDP contribution is larger than the economies of 
20 US states in 2021. 
 

24 | P a g e 
 
India-US Startup Partnership 
2.43 Nasscom submitted before the Committee that US has been a key 
hub for Indian technology startups and across chambers and startup 
groups, there is a plethora of programmes to connect Indian startups 
with enterprises, VCs and accelerators in the US. Y Combinator which is 
one of the most sought-after programs in the US has graduated over 
150 Indian tech founders that have built and scaled businesses in India 
and the US. Nasscom has been hosting an Innotrek delegation every 
year wherein selected startups are taken for a two-week immersion 
program helping them to understand the US market, meet with key 
leaders and build an innovation engagement across the two markets. 
 
B. Europe 
2.44 Nasscom stated that Europe including the UK is India’s second 
largest export destination and accounts for almost 25-28% of overall 
exports with 17-18% being contributed from UK. A bilateral meeting 
between Hon’ble Minister of Electronics and Information Technology, and 
Executive Vice-President for Technological Sovereignty, Security, and 
Democracy, European Commission, was held on February 27, 2025 in 
New Delhi. Discussions focused on strengthening India-EU cooperation 
in key areas such as microelectronics and supply chain resilience, AI, 
DPI, high-performance computing and quantum technology, digital skills, 
and telecom and IT standardization. 
 
India- EU Trade and Technology Council 
2.45 The India-EU Trade and Technology Council (TTC) was established 
in April 2022 by PM and the President of the European Commission, Ms. 
Ursula von der Leyen during the latter’s visit to India. TTC is an 
important platform for India for anchoring discussions on issues of 
strategic and contemporary importance like Semiconductors, quantum 
computing, AI, Green energy and resilient supply chains.  
2.46 The TTC was established as a high-level Strategic platform chaired 
by EAM, CIM and Minister of MEITY from the Indian side and Executive 
Vice President/Commissioner(s) dealing with Trade; and Digital Affairs 

25 | P a g e 
 
from the EU side. Under the new European Commission (which assumed 
charge in December 2024), EU side has expanded the co-chairs from 
their side to include their Commissioner handling Startups, Research and 
Innovation. This was the first such mechanism for India with any partner 
and was the second for the EU after its TTC with the US.  
2.47 Three Working Groups have been established under the TTC; 
Strategic technologies, Digital Connectivity and Digital Governance 
anchored by MEITY; Green and Clean Energy Technologies by Office of 
Principal Scientific Advisor; and Trade, Investments and Resilient supply 
chains by Department of Commerce chaired by respective Secretaries of 
the Departments. 
2.48 Three meetings of the TTC have been held so far, in May 2023 (1st 
Ministerial Meeting), in November 2023 (virtual review meeting), and in 
February 2025 (2nd Ministerial Meeting). Under Working Group 1, in the 
field of semiconductors, the two sides have signed an MoU in November 
2023 aimed to share experiences, best practices and information on 
their respective semiconductors ecosystems; identify areas for 
collaboration in research, development and innovation among 
universities, research organisations and businesses; promote skills, 
talent and workforce development for the semiconductors industry. 
Under the MoU, joint workshops on skilling and advanced semiconductor 
R&D have already been organised.  
2.49 On High Performance Computing, under the Intent of Cooperation 
(IoC) signed in 2022, India-EU joint initiative GANANA has been 
launched focused on a joint initiative focused on computational research 
in Natural Hazards, Climate Change, and Bioinformatics. 
2.50 On AI, India AI Mission the European AI Office are working 
together to deepen cooperation, encourage an ecosystem of innovation 
and foster information exchange on common open research questions 
for developing trustworthy AI.  
2.51 Further, Bharat 6G Alliance and the EU 6G Smart Networks and 
Services Industry Association have signed an MoU in June 2024 aimed 

26 | P a g e 
 
towards aligning research and development priorities and creating 
secured and trusted telecommunications and resilient supply chains. 
 
2.52 An administrative arrangement on mutual recognition of e-
signature has been signed in January 2026. Working Group 2 has been 
active in promoting cooperation in the areas of battery technologies, 
waste to energy, marine plastic litter management, waste-water 
management, long-term research and standards.  
2.53 On clean and green technologies, joint research initiatives on EV 
battery recycling, waste-to-hydrogen, and marine plastic litter 
underscore the TTC’s role in addressing pressing environmental 
challenges focusing on scalability and industry adoption was announced 
in February 2026 with a €60 million joint commitment.  
2.54 Workshops, ideathons, matchmaking events, immersion visits on 
recycling of EV batteries, EV interoperability and combating marine 
plastic pollution have been held. Working Group 3 is focused on market 
access issues, resilient value chains, and WTO and other multilateral 
issues. On trade and investment, India and the EU are working together 
to resolve each other’s priority market access issues which has resulted 
in EU resolving market access for Indian marine products and agriculture 
related issues from the EU side. The discussions also cover investment 
protection and cooperation on resilient value chains in consultation with 
their industry stakeholders.  
 
C. United Kingdom 
2.55 UK is the 2nd largest market for India IT and India is the 2nd 
largest source of FDI in the UK, with technology sector being a key 
contributor. 
India-UK Technology and Security Initiative 
2.56 The 'Technology Security Initiative' (TSI) was launched in July 
2024 during the visit of UK Foreign Secretary Mr. David Lammy. The TSI 
brings into sharper focus collaboration in critical and emerging 
technologies (CET) across priority sectors such as telecoms, critical 

27 | P a g e 
 
minerals, artificial intelligence (AI), quantum, health/bio technology, 
advanced materials and semiconductors. It reinforces existing 
collaborative efforts in various technologies, broaden the mandates of 
existing mechanism, and establish new mechanisms for cooperation. TSI 
is reviewed annually at the level of NSAs.   
2.57 An anniversary Statement was issued during PM's visit to the UK in 
July 2025 to mark the achievements of TSI in last one year and to 
underscore the transformative potential of the TSI to deliver cutting-
edge innovations and generate investment across the entire technology 
value chain. 
2.58 During the October 2025 visit of UK Prime Minister Keir Starmer to 
India, both sides announced establishment of India – UK Connectivity 
and Innovation Centre; establishment of India – UK Joint Centre for AI; 
Launch of Phase II of UK–India Critical Minerals Supply Chain 
Observatory and establishment of a new satellite campus at IIT – ISM 
Dhanbad and establishment of the Critical Minerals Industry Guild to 
secure resilient supply chains and promote green technologies. 
 
D. Germany  
2.59 Germany has been a key growth market for Indian tech 
engineering services as well as many German companies have set up 
extensive R&D centres and IT operations in India. A bilateral meeting 
between Secretary, MeitY, and State Secretary, Federal Ministry for 
Digital and Transport (BMDV), Germany, was held on April 9, 2025, in 
MeitY. Discussions focused on advancing strategic cooperation in resilient 
and diversified supply chains with emphasis on collaboration in AI, 
research and development, digital skilling, and Digital Public 
Infrastructure. 
Other bilateral engagements 
2.60 India has, in parallel, expanded and deepened its bilateral 
technology engagement with a wider set of partners over 2025-26, 

28 | P a g e 
 
illustrated by the following recent engagements placed before the 
Committee: 
 A bilateral meeting between Group Coordinator (R&D), MeitY, and 
a delegation from the Department of Science, Innovation and 
Technology, UK, was held on March 13, 2025, in MeitY. 
Discussions were held regarding compound semiconductors, 
microelectronics and supply chain resilience, research and 
development, and skilling. 
 The Second Meeting of the India–South Africa Joint Working 
Group (JWG) on Information, Communication and Technology 
(ICT), co-chaired Joint Secretary, MeitY, from the Indian side, and 
Acting Deputy Director General, ICT International Relations and

South Africa, from March 26-28, 2025. Discussions were held 
regarding cooperation in the areas of Digital Public Infrastructure, 
digital payments ecosystem, artificial intelligence, startup 
ecosystem, digital skills, and IT/ITeS. 
 A bilateral meeting between Secretary, MeitY, and State Secretary, 
Federal Ministry for Digital and Transport (BMDV), Germany, was

skilling, and Digital Public Infrastructure. 
  A delegation led by Secretary, MeitY, visited the Netherlands from

 The first meeting of the Malaysia–India Digital Council (MIDC), co-

 A meeting between Secretary, MeitY, and Hon’ble Vice Minister for 
the Development of Information and Communication 
Technologies, Venezuela, was held on September 16, 2025, in 
MeitY. Discussions were held regarding DigiLocker proof of 
concept and further cooperation under the signed MoU on Sharing 
Successful Digital Solutions between the Ministry of Electronics 
and Information Technology, India, and the Ministry of People’s 
Power for Science and Technology, Venezuela. 
 The Working Group-1 of the India-EU Trade and Technology 
Council (TTC) on Strategic Technologies, Digital Governance and 
Digital Connectivity met on October 31, 2025 to discuss digital

quantum computing, and IT and telecom standardization. The 
meeting was co-chaired by the Secretary, MeitY and the Director-
General, DG CNECT. 

30 | P a g e 
 
 A delegation, led by the Secretary, MeitY, visited the United Arab 
Emirates to participate as a speaker at the Abu Dhabi Global AI 
Summit during 2–3 November 2025 
  The inaugural meeting of the India–Mauritius Joint Working

 The 3 rd meeting of the India - Uzbekistan Joint Working Group 
(JWG) on Digital Technologies was held in virtual mode on

2.61 At present, MeitY has a total of 48 active MoUs in the areas of IT ,

framework for cooperation on strategic technologies, green and clean 

31 | P a g e 
 
technologies, and trade and resilient value chains, while the India-UK 
Technology and Security Initiative provides a focused mechanism for 
collaboration on technology with security implications.

2.65 When asked how India can leverage its strategic tech partnerships 
at bilateral level on the above initiatives to advance its national interests,

cornerstone of the strategic partnership between India and the US. As 
part of this growing partnership, the TRUST initiative aims to build 
resilient supply chains, accelerate AI infrastructure development, and 
foster innovation ecosystems that strengthen the technological 
capabilities and economic competitiveness of India and the US. The 
TRUST framework is designed to ensure strategic technology 
collaboration is secure, results-driven, and aligned with national security 
and economic interests of both countries. 

32 | P a g e

platforms to secure access to critical technologies, deepen supply-chain 
resilience, and expand cooperation in strategic sectors such as 
semiconductors, artificial intelligence, quantum technologies, cyber

2.68 Under the India-UK Technology and Security Initiative, we have a 
focused framework to advance cooperation in technology areas, 
including telecommunications, critical minerals, semiconductors, AI, 
quantum, biotechnology, and advanced materials. The initiative provides 
a channel for innovation cooperation, and for advancing collaboration 
among governments, industry, academia, and start-ups. These initiatives 
support India’s objectives of technology access, industrial upgrading, 
supply-chain diversification, and stronger participation in trusted 
international technology ecosystems.

international forums highlights its commitment to driving digital progress 
and fostering partnerships with global stakeholders. Through these 
forums, India showcases its achievements, explores opportunities for 

33 | P a g e 
 
collaboration, and strengthens its influence in shaping the global digital

incorporating the proposed priority areas- Universal and equitable 
digital inclusion through connectivity, usage, and enabling conditions; 
Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI) as a key element for digital 
transformation; digital innovation ecosystems unleashing the potential 
of Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs); and equitable, 
inclusive, trustworthy, and sustainable Artificial Intelligence (AI) for 
good and for all. The AITF meetings focused on deliberations and 
negotiations of the AITF Ministerial Declaration, which emphasized 
inclusive, ethical, and sustainable AI development, addressing themes 
such as human rights–centred AI, digital sovereignty, bias mitigation, 
open access, information integrity, responsible use of AI, public sector 
capacity building, and linking AI with sustainability, with data 
governance as the foundation.  
MeitY negotiated the key issues and put forth the DPI and AI 
agenda agreed under the Indian Presidency in 2023. The Chair’s 
Statement of the Digital Economy Working Group and the Chair’s 
Statement of the Task Force on Artificial Intelligence, Data

United Nations Commission on International Trade Law

Asian and Pacific Training Centre for Information and 
Communication Technology for Development (APCICT): United

countries of the region with 53 members including China, Indonesia,

use the ICT in their socio-economic development.

United Nations Educational, Scientific, and Cultural 
Organization (UNESCO): 43 rd General Conference (GC): 
UNESCO is a specialized agency of the United Nations that works to 
build peace and sustainable development through international 
cooperation in education, science, culture, and communication. It 
supports Member States by setting global norms, promoting 
knowledge sharing, protecting cultural and natural heritage, and 
strengthening national capacities through policy guidance and 
programmes. 
The UNESCO General Conference is the organisation’s highest 
decision-making body, comprising all Member States, which meets 
biennially to set overall policies and priorities, approve the programme 
and budget, adopt conventions, and elect the Executive Board and 
Director-General

During our G20 presidency, India placed digital public

38 | P a g e
