CHAPTER 3 
DIGITAL GOVERNANCE AND DIGITAL PUBLIC 
INFRASTRUCTURE 
Digital governance has emerged as a cornerstone of India’s 
domestic success in building scalable, interoperable digital platforms and 
has inspired a model of governance that combines efficiency, inclusivity, 
and accountability. These initiatives include creating a unified digital 
identity (Aadhaar), a robust payments system (UPI), and paperless 
service delivery (e.g. DigiLocker, CoWIN), harnessing technology. India is 
sharing its DPI model as a replicable framework for other developing 
nations. Through the India Stack Global, India is helping partner 
countries build their own digital infrastructure using open APIs and 
interoperable standards. For instance, India has collaborated with 
countries such as Sri Lanka, Mauritius, and the Philippines to implement 
digital identity and payment systems inspired by Indian models. 
I. INDIA’S DIGITAL GOVERNANCE MODEL 
3.2 The Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY), 
Government of India launched the ‘Digital India’ programme on July 1, 
2015 with the vision to transforming India into a digitally empowered 
society and knowledge-based economy by ensuring digital access, digital 
inclusion, digital empowerment and bridging the digital divide. The 
programme is centred on three key vision areas, namely digital 
infrastructure as a core utility to every citizen, governance and services 
on demand, and digital empowerment of citizens. The overall goal is to 
ensure that digital technologies improve the life of every citizen, expand 
India’s digital economy, and create investment and employment 
opportunities and digital technological capabilities in the country.   
3.3 Digital India has considerably bridged the distance between 
Government and citizens and enhanced trust on government and 
governance. It has also helped in the delivery of services directly to 
beneficiaries in a transparent manner. In the process, India has emerged 
as one of the pre-eminent nations of the world to use technology to 
transform the lives of its citizens.  

43 | P a g e 
 
3.4 Digital India represents a shift from traditional paper-based 
administration to a platform-based, citizen-centric model. Over the last 
decade, India has developed a suite of foundational digital systems - 
such as Aadhaar, Unified Payments Interface (UPI), DigiLocker, MeghRaj 
Cloud, and Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) - which together form the 
backbone of digital governance in the country. These systems enable 
government services to reach citizens seamlessly, across geographical 
and socio-economic divides, transforming how welfare, education, 
healthcare, and financial inclusion are delivered. 
 
3.5 The Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY) has 
informed that it has been at the forefront of driving digital 
transformation in governance and public service delivery. Through 
strategic policymaking, creation of enabling digital infrastructure, and 
implementation of mission-mode projects, MeitY has institutionalized 
digital governance across all tiers of government-from the Centre to the 
States and Union Territories. MeitY’s approach emphasises “whole-of-
government” digital transformation, interoperability, and citizen-
centricity, while ensuring trust, transparency, and accountability. India’s 
digital governance ecosystem has been strengthened through a range of 
flagship programs and platforms that have transformed service delivery 
across sectors. These initiatives enable paperless, presence-less, and 
cashless governance, fostering efficiency, transparency, and inclusivity. 
The principal such flagship programmes are set out below. 
II. KEY DIGITAL GOVERNANCE INITIATIVES ENABLING 
PUBLIC SERVICE DELIVERY 
3.6 Some of the key digital governance initiatives enabling public 
service delivery are enumerated below: 
(i) DigiLocker: Revolutionizing Paperless Governance: 
DigiLocker is a flagship initiative under the Digital India programme 
aimed at providing citizens with a secure, cloud-based platform to 
store, access, and share digital documents. It enables paperless 
governance by allowing issuance, verification, and storage of 
authentic digital documents and certificates directly from the source 

44 | P a g e 
 
issuers. Integrated with Aadhaar for authentication, it eliminates the 
need for physical copies, thereby promoting efficiency and trust in 
service delivery. DigiLocker has, as of the latest figures placed before 
the Committee, 62-plus crore registered users, has issued more than 
950 crore documents, and has 2,409 registered issuers and 3,275 
requestors 
(ii) Entity Locker: Empowering Institutions and 
Organisations: To extend the benefits of paperless governance to 
the institutional domain, DigiLocker launched Entity Locker — a 
dedicated digital locker for organisations, institutions, and government 
entities. Entity Locker enables departments, corporations, universities, 
banks, and statutory bodies to securely store, issue, and verify 
organisational documents digitally, ensuring authenticity, 
accountability, and operational efficiency.  It has emerged as a 
foundational component for e-Governance workflows, empowering 
government bodies and regulated entities to transition towards trusted 
digital repositories that comply with national standards of 
authentication and auditability. 
As of 31 December 2025, Entity Locker had onboarded 40,739 
entities and 374 issuers. It has been integrated with the Indian 
Railway e-Procurement System (IREPS), marking one of its earliest 
requestor-side implementations, and with the National Single Window 
System (NSWS), enabling digital document exchange across 26 
Ministries and 35 Departments. Integration with the CAG and State AG 
Offices for electronic pension orders, GPF statements, and audit 
certificates is continuing, reinforcing institutional adoption of 
DigiLocker for administrative record management. These integrations 
collectively signify DigiLocker's evolution into a national digital 
backbone for trusted document exchange, verification and 
interoperability. 
(iii)  Unified Mobile App for New-Age Governance (UMANG) : UMANG 
has been conceptualized with an objective to improve ease-of-living 
by putting the power in the hands of citizens for availing major 
government services anytime, anywhere with just few clicks on a 

45 | P a g e 
 
single unified mobile application integrated with DigiLocker, 
myScheme, National Single Sign-On (NSSO), etc. It enables the 
citizens of India to access e-Government services of various sectors 
such as Agriculture, Education, Health, Housing, Employees, 
Pensioners, Students, Ration Card, Railways, and many more services 
from the Central and State Governments. UMANG is available in 23 
multi-lingual languages (for top 100 services), including English & 
Hindi and has been hosted on cloud. UMANG aims to bring power to 
the fingertips of citizens. As of the latest figures, UMANG offers 2,390 
services (872 Central, 1,518 State) across 240 departments and 
entities, has more than 10.15 crore user registrations, and has 
processed over 722.37 crore transactions, with key services including 
Aadhaar, the Delhi Metro Rail Corporation, Bhashini, EPFO, the 
Ayushman Bharat Health Account, and the National Highways 
Authority of India. 
(iv)  My Scheme: My Scheme is a National Platform that aims to offer 
one-stop search and discovery of the Government schemes. It 
provides an innovative, technology- based solution to discover scheme 
information based upon the eligibility of the citizen. The platform 
helps the citizen to find the right Government schemes for them. It 
also guides on how to apply for different Government schemes. Its 
portal may be accessed at https://www.myscheme.gov.in/ and the 
same is also accessible through the UMANG platform 
(https://web.umang.gov.in/). The platform currently supports English 
and Hindi. The platform offers search and discovery for more than 
4,500 schemes (590-plus Central, 3,970-plus State/UT), of which 
approximately 3,700 have an updated Check Eligibility Questionnaire. 
(v) National Data Highway (NDH)/ Application Programming Interface 
(API) Setu: API Setu is a platform that plays a vital role in building a 
connected digital governance ecosystem. The platform aims to enable 
secure, interoperable, and scalable service delivery by fostering 
seamless data exchange across diverse e-Governance applications and 
government departments. With a vision to empower innovation and 
efficiency in public service delivery, API Setu facilitates the safe and 
reliable sharing of information across systems, promoting 

46 | P a g e 
 
transparency and improving citizen-centric service design. More than 
7,850 APIs have been published on the platform, with over 610 crore 
transactions processed, 2,468-plus publishers (including PAN, Driving 
Licence, Vehicle Registration, COVID Vaccination Certificate and 
CBSE), and 6,150-plus consumers (including eSanad, NABARD, and 
various State departments). 
(vi)  MeriPehchaan [National Single Sign-On (NSSO) platform]:  
National Single Sign-On (MeriPehchaan) is a platform that 
authenticates citizens easily and securely. MeriPehchaan is a user 
authentication service in which a single set of credentials provide 
access to multiple online applications or services. It aims at 
eliminating the need to repeatedly prove user identity to different 
applications and hold different credentials for each application. It is an 
extensive collaboration of the three mainstream Single Sign-On (SSO) 
platforms e-Pramaan, Jan Parichay and DigiLocker. MeriPehchaan 
enables standardized registration which means users need to provide 
information once for accessing different services. It authenticates the 
user based on multiple authentication parameters like username, 
mobile number, Aadhaar, PAN, etc. It presently integrates 15,089-plus 
services, has processed over 200 crore transactions, and has 59.17 
crore users, with key services including Rajasthan SSO, Assam Sewa 
setu, CoWIN, DigiLocker, eShram and MyGov, among others. 
(vii) Online e-Sign (e-Hastakshar): e-Hastakshar, a cutting-edge eSign 
service that allows citizens to digitally sign documents online in real-
time, providing a legally acceptable form and convenient alternative to 
physical signatures. Over the past year, Centre for Development of 
Advanced Computing (C-DAC) integrated this service with various 
departments, ministries, and agencies at the Central and State 
Government levels, as well as Union Territories. eSign service and 
Aadhaar service are now accessible through the portal for status 
tracking and monitoring purposes. In addition, eSign service 
notifications have been implemented via our WhatsApp Business 
account to ensure that organisations receive timely updates regarding 
the service. Currently, more than 300 agencies are utilizing the eSign 

47 | P a g e 
 
service. Since its inception, over 34.56 crore e-Sign digital signatures 
have been issued by C-DAC. 
(viii) Common Services Centres (CSCs) : CSCs have been set up under 
an initiative of the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology. 
CSC e-Governance Services India Limited is the implementing agency. 
The CSC initiative aims to deliver e-services to rural citizens through 
CSCs and expand the CSC network till the Gram Panchayat level. Over 
800 services are being delivered through CSCs, including government 
services, financial services and services related to Aadhaar, various 
social welfare schemes, education, tele-medicine, travel bookings, 
utility payments. CSC 2.0 project has been completed as on Mar 2024. 
It operates on a self-sustainable entrepreneurship model. It is a PAN 
India project. As of December 2025, 5.72 lakh CSCs are functional 
across the country, of which 4.45 lakh are functional at the Gram 
Panchayat level. 
(ix)  National Scholarship Portal (NSP): The National Scholarship Portal 
(NSP) was launched under the Digital India Programme to provide a 
unified platform for processing, sanctioning, and disbursing various 
scholarship schemes offered by both Central and State Governments. 
The portal aims to create a system that is Simplified, Mission-oriented, 
Accountable, Responsive, and Transparent (SMART) for the effective 
distribution of scholarship funds directly into the beneficiaries' bank 
accounts through Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT). The portal has 
cumulatively benefited approximately 7.33 crore beneficiaries, 
disbursed approximately ₹44,500 crore, and onboarded 153 
scholarship schemes, with key integrations including DigiLocker, API 
Setu, the Aadhaar Payment Bridge, CPGRAMS, the Unique Disability 
ID and Bhashini. 
(x) GI Cloud (MeghRaj):  Government of India has a sharp focus on 
digitalizing governance under the “Digital India” flagship program, and 
it led to an increase in computing resource requirements for numerous 
projects. To realize the Digital India vision, and harness the benefits of 
Cloud Computing, the Government of India has embarked upon an 
ambitious initiative – ‘GI Cloud’, which has been named as 

48 | P a g e 
 
‘MeghRaj’.The objective of initiative is to deliver ICT services over 
Cloud to all the Departments/Ministries at the Centre and States/UTs 
to ensure proliferation of Cloud ecosystem in the country. Presently, 
32,205 virtual machines run on GI Cloud, which is used by more than 
2,188 Government Departments. MeitY has empanelled the service 
offerings of 26 domestic and international Cloud Service Providers 
across various deployment and service models, with all such cloud 
services listed, and procurable, on the Government e-Marketplace 
(GeM) platform. 
(xi)  Integrated Citizen Engagement Platform (INCEP) : MyGov is a 
Government of India’s Citizen Engagement Platform which 
collaborates with multiple Government bodies/ Ministries to engage 
people in policy formulation and seeks the opinion of people on 
issues/ topics of public interest and welfare. Since its inception in 
2014, MyGov has engaged more than 6 crore citizens as MyGov 
Sathis, hosting over 1,878 tasks with 18.84-plus lakh submissions, 
1,051 discussions with 56.11-plus lakh submissions, and 1,378 quizzes 
with 3.17-plus crore participations. The platform now operates across 
28 States and Union Territories. 
(xii) Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI):  Over the past decade, India 
has redefined how technology can empower societies. Through the 
development of Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI)-such as Aadhaar for 
identity, Unified Payments Interface (UPI) for payments, DigiLocker for 
document exchange, etc - it is shown how digital systems, when built 
as open, interoperable, and inclusive platforms, can transform 
governance and improve lives. Aadhaar has provided digital identity to 
more than 1.43 billion persons; UPI, with more than 49 crore users 
and 675 participating banks, is the world's largest digital payment 
system; DigiLocker has issued 950-plus crore documents to 62-plus 
crore users; and API Setu has enabled data exchange through 7,850-
plus APIs. India's DPIs — Aadhaar, UPI, UMANG, DigiLocker, CoWIN, 
FASTag and others — are now studied and adopted globally, and India 
has signed MoUs with 22 countries to share its DPI to enhance digital 
transformation, improve public service delivery, and foster innovation. 

49 | P a g e 
 
(xiii) India Stack Local : In addition, India Stack Local portal has been 
developed which aims to showcase the successes of States and Union 
Territories in rolling out digital transformational solutions at State 
level. It is envisaged that the differentiated solution, deep value, 
inclusive nature, feature richness and the impact of these solutions 
may inspire national level adoption and/ or replication in another 
States. The idea is to encourage States and UTs to share their 
expertise of digital solutions so that it fosters competition and 
collaboration to do better for good governance. The portal will provide 
opportunity to share, learn, replicate and contribute towards 
equitable, economical and sustainable growth of our country. 794 
Digital Transformative solutions have already been listed on the stack 
which can be accessed at https://indiastacklocal.in/. 
(xiv) Bhaskaracharya National Institute for Space Applications and 
Geo-informatics (BISAG-N):  BISAG-N, through its various national-
level projects, has been playing a pivotal role in advancing digital 
governance to improve public service delivery across sectors. 
Initiatives such as the PM GatiShakti National Master Plan, Pradhan 
Mantri Dakshta Aur Kushalta Sampann Hitgrahi (PM DAKSH), Pradhan 
Mantri Unauthorised Colonies in Delhi Awas Adhikar Yojana (PM 
UDAY), Pradhan Mantri e-Vidya (PM e-Vidya) and the development of 
the PM Internship Portal for the Ministry of Corporate Affairs 
demonstrate how digital technologies are enabling transparent, 
efficient, and data-driven governance. By leveraging geospatial 
platforms, Information Technology (IT) systems, and secure digital 
infrastructure, BISAG-N supports evidence-based decision-making, 
accelerates infrastructure planning, and enhances citizen-centric 
service delivery. 
3.7 In less than a decade, the Digital India programme has 
fundamentally transformed the delivery of public services by embedding 
technology at the core of governance. The three pillars of building digital 
infrastructure, delivering services on demand, and empowering citizens 
have converged to create a comprehensive ecosystem of DPI that 
enables scale, speed, and transparency across sectors such as 
education, health, finance, and welfare. Flagship platforms like UMANG, 

50 | P a g e 
 
DigiLocker , e-Sign, API Setu, and the Common Service Centres have 
institutionalized citizen-centric, paperless, and interoperable service 
delivery models, while initiatives like Unified Payments Interface (UPI), 
Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) and Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission 
(ABDM) have ensured that benefits reach citizens directly and efficiently.  
 
III.  IMPACT OF DIGITAL GOVERNANCE ON PUBLIC SERVICE 
DELIVERY 
3.8 Digital Governance has become the backbone of India’s 
transformation towards effective public service delivery. MeitY, through 
its Digital Governance Division, continues to drive the vision of an open, 
inclusive, transparent, and responsive digital government, aligned with 
the goal of improving ease of living and ease of doing business across 
the country in a sustainable manner . As India advances toward Vision 
2047, digital governance will remain central to promoting transparency, 
efficiency, and accountability in public service delivery, while enhancing 
the overall ease of living for every citizen. 
3.9 Digital Governance has redefined public service delivery across 
multiple dimensions: 
 Citizen-Centricity: Delivering services tailored to citizen needs 
through anytime, anywhere accessibility. 
 Efficiency and Cost Savings:  Paperless transactions, data 
interoperability, and automation have reduced administrative 
overheads and duplication. 
 Transparency and Accountability: Real-time dashboards and 
digital workflows have increased accountability, making government 
performance measurable and visible. Tracking service delivery metrics 
through dashboards, analytics, and grievance redressal systems. 
 Interoperability: Enabling seamless data exchange and integration 
across departments through digital platforms and APIs. 
 Innovation Ecosystem:  Open APIs and data standards have 
catalysed public–private collaboration, creating new markets and 
services built atop public infrastructure. 

51 | P a g e 
 
 Inclusivity: Digital platforms have enabled access to services even 
in remote and marginalized communities, narrowing rural-urban and 
gender divides. Ensuring last-mile connectivity to rural and remote 
areas using digital and mobile platforms.  
 Accessibility: Citizens can avail services 24x7 from any location 
through digital platforms. 
 Empowerment: Citizens are empowered to access entitlements 
without intermediaries. 
 Resilience: Continuity of service delivery during crises such as the 
COVID-19 pandemic. 
 Digital governance also enhances evidence-based policymaking by 
generating large-scale, anonymized datasets on service usage, 
enabling better targeting of interventions and evaluation of outcomes. 
 
3.10 When asked about how  digital technology is playing a 
transformative role in improving governance, public service delivery 
system, investment, citizens welfare, etc. in the country, MEItY replied 
that digital technology has emerged as a key enabler of transformative 
governance in India, significantly enhancing transparency, efficiency, 
accountability, and inclusivity across sectors. The Government has 
leveraged digital public infrastructure (DPIs) and platforms to improve 
governance, public service delivery, and citizen welfare.  Digital Public 
Infrastructure (DPI), including Aadhaar, DigiLocker, and Unified 
Payments Interface (UPI), has enabled seamless, faceless, and cashless 
service delivery.  
 
3.11 Digital India program has brought fundamental change in the 
people’s lives: 
(i) More than 144 crore Aadhaar numbers have been issued providing 
digital identity to Indian residents 
(ii) India's UPI is now the world's largest digital payment system with 
more than 46 Cr users and 685 banks. UPI powers 81% of India’s 
digital payments and nearly 49% of global real-time digital 
payments 

52 | P a g e 
 
(iii) Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) mechanisms ensure targeted delivery 
of subsidies and welfare benefits, reducing leakages and improving 
efficiency. Using Jan Dhan, Aadhaar and Mobile (JAM) Trinity, 
subsidies and benefits worth Rs. 49.82 lakh crore have been 
transferred directly to the citizens 
(iv) DigiLocker enables citizens to access and share digitally issued 
documents anytime and anywhere, eliminating the need to carry 
physical copies and facilitating seamless verification by authorities. 
DigiLocker has provided digital access to authentic digital documents 
from original issuer for the common citizen. Currently, 62+ crore 
users are registered on the platform. More than 950+ crore 
documents have been issued using DigiLocker. 
(v) Unified Mobile Application for New-age Governance (UMANG) 
provides a single unified interface to access multiple Government 
services, enabling citizens to avail services from their homes, 
thereby saving time and travel costs. UMANG mobile application for 
all government services is operational and offers more than 2,446 
services. It has more than 10.88 Cr registered users. 
(vi) Initiatives like e-Office & e-Sign, MeriPehchaan/National Single Sign-
On (NSSO), have improved efficiency and accountability in public 
administration. 
(vii) Sectoral Digital Public Infrastructures (DPIs) and platforms in Health 
(Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM)), Education (DIKSHA & 
SWAYAM), Agriculture (AgriStack), and skilling (Skill India Digital 
Hub (SIDH)) are significantly expanding equitable access to 
essential services, particularly in rural and remote areas.  
(viii) Common Services Centres (CSCs) are established under the Digital 
India Programme, throughout the country to provide citizen-centric 
services. These centres are operated by Village Level Entrepreneurs 
(VLE). Over 800 services are being delivered through CSCs. 
Currently, there are 5.41 Lakh CSCs operational throughout the 
country. Citizens access a wide range of services through CSCs, 
including electricity bill payments, e-District services, municipal 

53 | P a g e 
 
services, Update Client Lite (UCL) demographic services (Aadhaar 
Demographic update), e-recharge, PAN services, etc. All the services 
provided by CSCs can be seen at  https://csc.gov.in/. District wise 
details of CSCs, are available at https://csc.gov.in/. 
(ix) Promoting Digital Literacy: The Government of India has, through 
iGOT Karmayogi and capacity-building initiatives under the Digital 
India programme, substantially expanded digital skilling access 
across central and state levels. Further, the Government has taken 
several initiatives to provide skilling/upskilling to youth. These 
initiatives aim to develop industry-ready manpower in emerging and 
advanced technology domains across the country. 
 PMGDISHA: Pradhan Mantri Gramin Digital Saksharta Abhiyan 
(PMGDISHA) was launched in 2017 to ensure digital literacy. The 
scheme concluded on 31 st March 2024. The scheme was one of 
the world’s largest digital literacy initiatives. Against the target of 
training 6 Cr people, more than 6.39 Crore persons were trained 
nationwide. 
 FutureSkills Prime: FutureSkills PRIME program is a collaborative 
initiative of MeitY and National Association of Software and 
Service Companies (NASSCOM) aimed at making India a cutting-
edge digital talent nation. Under the programme, so far, more 
than 32+ lakh candidates have registered on the portal, out of 
which there have been 21+ lakh candidates enrolled/trained in 
various courses. 
 National Institute of Electronics and Information Technology 
(NIELIT): NIELIT provides digital literacy courses such as 
Awareness in Computer Concept (ACC), Course on Computer 
Concepts (CCC) etc. NIELIT has trained 43 lakh+ candidates 
under various courses, including digital literacy and cyber security 
awareness. Training is delivered through a wide network of 56 
NIELIT Centres and 9000+ Accredited Training 
Partners/Facilitation Centres. 
(x)  Additionally, under the BharatNet Programme, being implemented 
by the Department of Telecommunications, optical fibre connectivity 

54 | P a g e 
 
is being provided to the Gram Panchayats to enable high-speed 
broadband services, including Fiber-To-The-Home (FTTH) 
connections. As per the information available on the BharatNet 
dashboard, 2.18 lakh Gram Panchayats (GPs) have been made 
service-ready.  
3.12 India’s digital economy has expanded rapidly into a globally 
distinctive model defined by scale, affordability, and inclusivity, 
underpinned by robust public digital infrastructure. It operates among 
the world’s largest digital identity and payment ecosystems, with 
platforms such as Unified Payments Interface (UPI) surpassing many 
developed countries in transaction volumes. The use of open, 
interoperable architectures has enabled delivery of digital services at 
significantly lower cost while accelerating financial inclusion at an 
unprecedented scale. Unlike the largely proprietary systems in 
developed economies, India’s Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI) 
approach fosters innovation through an open and scalable framework 
that actively enables both public and private sector participation. India 
has demonstrated faster and broader adoption across payments, 
governance, and citizen-centric services, positioning itself as a leading 
force in the global digital landscape. 
IV. LEGAL AND REGULATORY FRAMEWORK 
3.13 The domestic success of India's digital governance model is closely 
linked to its use as a diplomatic instrument. The representative of the 
Ministry of External Affairs submitted before the Committee as under: 
“As part of diplomacy, India is endeavoring to actively shape 
conversations on digital governance, keeping in focus our guiding 
principle that technology must advance the welfare of our people 
and empower everyone. To strengthen the technology ecosystem 
in the country, relevant line Ministries have launched several 
national missions, such as the India AI Mission, Digital India, the 
National Supercomputing Mission, the PLI scheme for 
semiconductors, and many more. MEA is working very closely 

55 | P a g e 
 
with all the line Ministries in pursuit of our overall interest in the 
technology domain.” 
3.14 Building on this, the Committee enquired about India's regulatory 
framework governing digital governance. In a written reply, MeitY stated 
that India's digital governance is guided by a comprehensive and 
evolving regulatory framework that combines statutory laws, policy 
instruments and institutional mechanisms to ensure secure, inclusive 
and accountable use of digital technologies. At the core is the 
Information Technology Act, 2000, which provides the legal basis for 
electronic governance, digital transactions, cybersecurity and 
intermediary liability. This is complemented by the Digital Personal Data 
Protection Act, 2023, which establishes a rights-based framework for 
personal data protection, emphasising consent, purpose limitation and 
the accountability of data fiduciaries. 
3.15 MeitY acts as the nodal Ministry for digital policy, supported by 
guidelines such as the Intermediary Rules, cybersecurity directions 
issued by the Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In), 
and e-Governance standards for interoperability and security. Digital 
platforms and services are also governed by policies under the Digital 
India programme, which promotes digital infrastructure, services and 
digital literacy. Overall, India's regulatory framework emphasises 
openness, interoperability, user consent, data protection and innovation, 
while ensuring accountability, security and trust in digital governance 
systems. 
V. CYBERSECURITY AND DATA PROTECTION IN DIGITAL 
GOVERNANCE 
3.15 The Committee also enquired how India is addressing challenges 
relating to cybersecurity in its digital governance framework, particularly 
in relation to cross-border data flows and privacy protection. In reply, 
MeitY stated that the policies of the Government of India are aimed at 
ensuring an open, safe, trusted and accountable cyberspace for users in 
the country, and that the Government is cognizant of the risks and 
harms arising from the misuse of digital technologies, including digital 

56 | P a g e 
 
platforms. India addresses these challenges through a robust blend of 
legal safeguards, regulatory oversight and institutional mechanisms. 
3.16 The Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023, the Information 
Technology Act, 2000, and the Information Technology (Intermediary 
Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021, together provide 
a comprehensive framework for user safety and responsible digital 
participation, supplemented by sector-specific guidelines from regulators 
such as the UIDAI and the RBI. Complaints relating to unauthorised 
access to personal data, fraudulent financial transactions, and data 
scraping are handled by sector regulators (such as those for banking 
and telecom), the National Cybercrime Reporting Portal, CERT-In, and 
platform-specific grievance systems. Penalties available include financial 
penalties for data misuse, suspension of API access, revocation of 
platform licences, and, in severe cases, criminal penalties; the DPDP Act, 
in particular, introduces significant financial penalties for data breaches 
or non-compliance. The underlying safeguards consist of mandatory 
user consent, purpose limitation, data minimisation, security safeguards 
and encryption, and independent audits. 
 
VI. DIGITAL PUBLIC INFRASTRUCTURE: INDIA’S APPROACH 
3.17 The Digital India programme focuses on expanding digital access, 
internet affordability, digital public infrastructure and digital literacy. 
India has been fostering its digital transformation through Digital Public 
Infrastructure (DPI) approach, built on the core principles of open 
access and interoperability through open standards and specifications. 
This architecture enables regulators to embed legal and compliance 
requirements directly into system design, ensuring adherence through 
participation while upholding trust and user consent. Further, the DPI 
model actively enables private sector participation, fostering innovation, 
scalability, and widespread adoption.  
3.18 The DPI approach is a key pillar of this programme. It enables the 
delivery of digital services at population scale through open, 
interoperable and secure platforms. Platforms such as Aadhaar, UPI, 
DigiLocker, etc are shining examples of the same. The Government 

57 | P a g e 
 
remains committed to strengthening DPI as a driver of inclusive growth, 
innovation and efficient service delivery. DPIs enables delivery of digital 
services at population scale through open, interoperable and scalable 
platforms. This is in line with India’s leadership during the G-20 Summit 
and the adoption of the G20 Framework for Systems of Digital Public 
Infrastructure. The Global DPI Repository (https://www.dpi.global), 
launched during India’s G20 Presidency (2023), serves as a global 
knowledge platform, with India contributing the highest number of DPI 
solutions. 
3.19 The Government of India has signed Memorandum of 
understanding (MoU)/ agreements with 24 countries for sharing or 
cooperation on India Stack/Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI), primarily 
for replication and adoption of India’s digital governance 
platforms.  These MoUs focus on cooperation in areas such as digital 
identity, digital payments, data exchange and service delivery platforms, 
aligned with India’s broader DPI diplomacy under the India Stack 
framework. Apart from this, the Government of India has taken 
measures to share the success of India’s Digital Public Infrastructure 
(DPI) globally.  India Stack Global (https://www.indiastack.global/) 
showcases India’s DPI and facilitates its adoption by friendly countries. 
The portal provides access to 18 key digital platforms. 
3.20 Different DPIs are implemented and governed by the respective 
Ministries/Departments and their associated organisations in 
consultation with relevant stakeholders. For instance, key Digital Public 
Infrastructures such as digital identity (Aadhaar), digital payments (UPI), 
data exchange platforms (DigiLocker and API Setu), and service delivery 
platforms like e-Sanjeevani and the Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission 
(ABDM) are managed by the respective Ministries/Departments and 
implementing agencies. These are governed with appropriate regulatory 
oversight by the concerned Ministries. 
India Stack Global and DPI Diplomacy 
3.21 India Stack is a pioneering initiative comprising open APIs, e-gov 
solutions and digital public goods and infrastructure. It empowers 
governments, businesses, startups, and developers to innovate at scale. 

58 | P a g e 
 
Presently, India Stack encompasses 18  digital transformation solutions, 
including AADHAAR, UPI, CoWIN, API Setu, DigiLocker, Aarogya Setu, 
Government eMarketplace (GeM), UMANG, Diksha, eSanjeevani, 
eHospital, eOffice, eCourt, POSHAN Tracker, Skill India Digital Hub 
(SIDH), the NCD Platform, PM Gatishakti and Public Financial 
Management System (PFMS). Information about solutions is available on 
the India Stack Global portal in all six UN languages 
https://www.indiastack.global/ 
3.22 A key aspect of India’s global contribution is its leadership in 
deploying digital technologies at population scale. India’s Digital Public 
Infrastructure (DPI) is anchored in three foundational layers that enable 
secure, reliable, scalable, and inclusive service delivery: a verifiable 
identity layer through Aadhaar; a real-time payments layer through the 
Unified Payments Interface (UPI); and a consent-based data exchange 
layer enabled by DigiLocker for secure storage and sharing of official 
documents. This architecture has been catalysed by the JAM trinity - 
Aadhaar, Jan Dhan bank accounts, and mobile connectivity - which has 
driven financial inclusion and digital access. As a result, DPI has become 
a key enabler of economic activity across sectors and a scalable model 
that can be shared globally. 
 
3.23 When asked about India’s principal positions in the ongoing 
negotiations on global digital governance frameworks, the Ministry of 
Electronics and Information Technology stated that India’s strategic 
vision includes semiconductors, Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI), AI 
and Emerging Technologies. Some guiding principles that shape India's 
position at bilateral and multilateral negotiations include: 
 Developing self-reliance through domestic production and design 
capabilities 
 Capacity building and innovation to strengthen India's 
technology base 
 Strengthening global supply chains to reduce concentration risks 
 Partnerships with trusted geographies to ensure secure 
technology flows 

59 | P a g e 
 
 Democratisation of compute resources and other advanced 
technological capacities, so that the benefits of emerging 
technologies are not confined to a few nations or actors 
 Multistakeholder consultations with governments, industry and 
think-tanks  
3.24 MeitY further stated that India has built a strong domestic 
technology ecosystem through flagship schemes including Digital India, 
the IndiaAI Mission, the India Semiconductor Mission, and various 
Production and Design-Linked Incentive schemes. Critically, these are 
not siloed domestic programmes; they are being actively aligned with 
and reinforced by India's international diplomacy. India's DPI framework 
is perhaps the clearest example of this coherence. India championed the 
inclusion of DPI as a priority agenda item during its G20 Presidency, and 
consistently pushed for the democratisation of AI throughout its tenure 
in the Troika and beyond. These positions were also advanced at the UN 
Global Digital Compact negotiations and continue to be advocated at the 
UN Commission on Science and Technology for Development (UNCSTD). 
3.25 On Multilateral and Bilateral Engagements, MeitY stated India is 
actively engaged with key partners, including the United States, the 
European Union, Japan, Singapore, and the Netherlands, to build trusted 
technology partnerships that serve both strategic and economic 
objectives. At the multilateral level, India is a committed member of the 
Indo-Pacific Economic Framework (IPEF). Under Pillar II on Supply Chain 
Resilience, India serves as Vice Chair of the Supply Chain Council 
alongside the United States and is also a member of the Semiconductor 
Action Plan Team, underscoring India's centrality to efforts to build 
diversified and secure semiconductor supply chains. India has also 
recently signed the Pax Silica Declaration, reaffirming its commitment to 
ensuring diversified and resilient supply chains for technologies 
foundational to the AI era, particularly silicon and critical minerals that 
underpin chips, advanced computing, and other high-technology 
systems. 
3.26 In conclusion, India's technology diplomacy is grounded in a 
coherent strategic vision, underpinned by strong domestic initiatives, 

60 | P a g e 
 
and actively pursued through bilateral partnerships and multilateral 
engagements. India is not merely a participant in the global technology 
order; it is actively working to shape one that is open, inclusive, and 
resilient. 
3.27 On being asked about specific measures taken by the Government 
to strengthen DPI at the international levels, MEITY stated that guided 
by Vasudhaiva Kutumbakam, India is sharing its DPI as a global public 
good. 
● India Stack MoUs: As of February 2026, India has signed 24 MoUs 
with countries for cooperation on India Stack and DPI. These 
partnerships aim to strengthen digital infrastructure and service 
delivery models.  
● The Global DPI Repository (GDPIR): India proposed and the G20 
endorsed the establishment of GDPIR to facilitate knowledge 
sharing and mutual learning in DPI development. Currently, the 
GDPIR features 54 DPIs from 16 countries, and it can be accessed 
at https://www.dpi.global. 
● UPI is live in eight countries - UAE, Singapore, Bhutan, Nepal, Sri 
Lanka, France, Mauritius, Qatar. 
● During the Virtual G20 Leaders’ Summit the Hon’ble Prime Minister 
of India announced the creation of Social Impact Fund (SIF) for 
DPI acceleration in Global South nations, committing to an initial 
contribution by India of USD 25 million. 
3.28 As of February 2026, the Government has signed Memoranda of 
Understanding (MoUs) and agreements with 24 countries to cooperate 
on India Stack and Digital Public Infrastructure. These partnerships 
focus on sharing technical knowledge and supporting the replication of 
digital governance platforms. The MoUs cover digital identity, digital 
payments, data exchange, and service delivery platforms, aligned with 
India's broader DPI diplomacy under the India Stack framework. MoUs 
have been signed with countries from five regions of the world, with 
Africa having the highest number at six, followed by Asia, the Caribbean, 
Latin America, and Oceania. The full list of partner countries is: Antigua 
and Barbuda, Armenia, Papua New Guinea, Sierra Leone, Suriname, 

61 | P a g e 
 
Trinidad and Tobago, Tanzania, Kenya, Cuba, Colombia, the Lao People's 
Democratic Republic, Saint Kitts and Nevis, Ethiopia, Jamaica, the 
Gambia, Fiji, Guyana, Venezuela, Sri Lanka, Brazil, Lesotho, the 
Maldives, Mongolia and Seychelles. Separately, UPI is now operational in 
eight countries, including the UAE, Singapore, Bhutan, Nepal, Sri Lanka, 
France, Mauritius and Qatar, while four countries — Cuba, Kenya, the 
UAE, and the Lao People's Democratic Republic — have agreements in 
place regarding the DigiLocker data exchange platform. 
 
3.29 The scope of Cooperation under the signed MoUs includes: 
a) Capacity building of officials in Digital transformation (viz. INDIA 
STACK) through joint training programs 
b) Exchange of materials and information on current developments in 
Digital transformation, exchange of best practices, publications and 
documentation. 
c) Organising of Joint workshops, conferences 
d) Feasibility study and e-readiness assessment and implementation 
of Digital transformation solutions 
e) Enabling consultation of public officials and subject matter experts 
f) Cooperation in development of Digital transformation solutions 
(viz. INDIA STACK) through pilot or demo solutions of projects, 
which are already proven and successfully implemented at 
population scale. 
g) Scaling up of pilot Digital transformation solutions. 
h) Exploring possibilities of third country capacity building programs 
on the mutually agreed basis between the parties. 
 
3.30 To enhance our collaboration with all the recipient countries under 
the signed MoUs, number of virtual meetings and technical workshops 
have been convened. The objective of these virtual sessions is to 
sensitize the recipient countries, enabling them to understand and 
recognise how various Digital Public Infrastructure (DPIs) and digital 
solutions developed in India have played a pivotal role in driving digital 
transformation across the country, resulting in greater financial and 
social inclusion.  During the preliminary virtual meetings, India Stack 

62 | P a g e 
 
architecture and various use cases of DPIs/Digital solutions are 
explained to the recipient countries with the objective to narrow down 
their interest. Once a recipient country identifies a DPI/Digital Solution 
of interest, a virtual workshop between MeitY, concerned technical team 
from India, MEA and the recipient country is conducted to discuss the 
technical architecture of that particular DPI/Digital Solution.  Subsequent 
to the meetings, based on the interest of a recipient country for proof of 
concept (PoC) implementation in their country, a feasibility study is 
conducted to evaluate state of digital ecosystem in that very recipient 
country. 
3.31 The implementation of specific India Stack DPIs and Digital 
Solutions is at an advanced stage in the following countries: 
S.No Country 
Name 
Status 
1 Cuba i. Several virtual technical workshops regarding 
DigiLocker have held. The Cuban side expressed 
interest in adopting DigiLocker. A team from MEA 
and NeGD visited Cuba in August 2025 to 
conduct feasibility study. MeitY shared

3.32 These MoUs focus on cooperation in areas such as digital identity, 
digital payments, data exchange and service delivery platforms, aligned 
with India’s broader DPI diplomacy under the India Stack framework. 
UPI is now operational in eight countries, including the UAE, Singapore, 
Bhutan, Nepal, Sri Lanka, France, Mauritius, and Qatar. Four countries - 
Cuba, Kenya, UAE, and the Lao People's Democratic Republic - have 
deals regarding the DigiLocker data exchange platform. 
3.33 On the manner in which these MoUs benefit partner countries and 
India, MeitY responded that the MoUs enable partner countries to 
leverage proven, scalable digital solutions for faster and cost-effective 
digital transformation, strengthening governance and inclusion 
outcomes. Capacity-building and knowledge exchange further enhance 
institutional capabilities and digital readiness. For India, these 
engagements reinforce its role as a trusted digital partner and a global 
leader in DPI innovation, while also deepening its soft power and 
strategic influence, particularly across the Global South. They create 
avenues for sustained technical collaboration and policy learning. 
Overall, the partnership promotes inclusive, secure, and sustainable 
digital development across participating countries. 
 

64 | P a g e 
 
VII. DPI AND INDIA'S ENGAGEMENT WITH THE GLOBAL 
SOUTH 
3.34 When enquired about the way in which DPI developed by India 
can help countries in the Global South to improve governance and public 
service delivery in their countries, MEITY responded that India is actively 
supporting countries of the Global South in advancing digital governance 
and capacity building by sharing its experience of building inclusive, 
scalable, and cost-effective Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI). Through 
its flagship programme Digital India, India has developed interoperable 
platforms such as Aadhaar, Unified Payments Interface (UPI), and 
DigiLocker , which are being shared as global digital public goods. India 
provides technical assistance, advisory support, and capacity-building 
programmes to partner countries for adapting these solutions to local 
contexts, including through training, workshops, and institutional 
partnerships. 
 
3.35 The Global Digital Public Infrastructure Repository-GDPIR 
(https://www.dpi.global/), an initiative of the Indian G20 Presidency, is 
already operational. The GDPIR is designed to be a resource for key 
lessons and knowledge available from G20 members and guest 
countries, enabling easy discoverability. It is aimed at addressing the 
existing knowledge gap around the right practices to design, build, and 
deploy population scale DPI. Each participant, based on whether their 
DPI are built on open platforms, APIs, standards, and protocols, can 
choose to display any information at their discretion, which can help 
others to develop their DPI. Currently, the GDPIR features 54 DPIs from 
16 countries, with India being the largest contributor. It may be 
accessed at https://www.dpi.globalhttps://www.dpi.global/ 
 India Stack Global: India has implemented innovative digital 
projects, population-scale transformations, and ensured digital 
inclusion, contributing to global digital public goods repositories 
and providing affordable access to digital services. Currently 18 
DPIs are included in India Stack 
Global(https://www.indiastack.global/). 

65 | P a g e 
 
 India’s Digital Public Infrastructures (DPIs) such as Aadhaar, UPI, 
UMANG, DigiLocker, CoWIN, FASTag, etc are now studied and 
adopted globally. India has signed Memorandum of Understanding 
(MoU) with 24 countries to share its DPI to enhance digital 
transformation, improve public service delivery, and foster 
innovation. 
 The GoI has active DPI implementation engagements across 
multiple Global South countries including Sri Lanka, Myanmar, 
Siera Leon, Bangladesh, and India government has established the 
Social Impact Fund with an initial commitment of USD 25 million 
specifically to provide "upstream technical and non-technical 
assistance to countries in developing DPI systems"  
3.36 On the same question, the Ministry of External Affairs in a written 
reply stated that the foundational pillars of digital governance- capacity, 
access, and literacy- are essential not only for developing and adopting 
digital platforms but for ensuring that such transformation remains 
inclusive, equitable and citizen-centric. Towards this end, India regularly 
facilitates study visits and technical interactions for foreign delegations, 
enabling them to engage with key institutions like Unique Identification 
Authority of India (UIDAI), National e-Governance Division (NeGD), 
National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI), NPCI International 
Payments Limited (NIPL) etc. These meetings allow partner countries to 
discuss implementation challenges and learn from India’s experience in 
scaling systems. Some of the recent study visits include delegations from 
Sri Lanka, Kenya, Rwanda, Nepal, Indonesia, and South Africa, among 
others. Additionally, India has implemented several grant-based projects 
aimed at promoting digital literacy and bridging digital divide such as 
setting up of Myanmar Institute of Information Technology, supply of e-
Tablets to Mauritus, e-Vidyabharati and e-Aarogyabharati (e-VBAB) 
Network project for African students, supply of laptops to Trinidad & 
Tobago and up gradation of  India-Myanmar Center for Enhancement of 
IT skills in Myanmar, among others. 
 
3.37 The Committee also asked about the role India sees for initiatives 
such as UPI in establishing global norms for digital payments and 

66 | P a g e 
 
enhancing its soft power. To this the Ministry replied that the spread of 
India’s DPI would increasingly shape global norms in digital governance 
through multilateral institutions by embedding its DPI principles into 
global discussions (e.g. AI Impact Summit 2026) and institutional 
frameworks. India has actively advanced its digital governance 
principles- openness, inclusion, safety, interoperability- through forums 
such as the G20, where it helped build consensus on DPI as a global 
public good during its Presidency. The global spread of India’s DPI also 
serves as a tool of developmental diplomacy, especially in the Global 
South. By sharing technology along with legal, regulatory, and 
governance frameworks, India positions itself as a partner in capacity 
building than a technology exporter. This enhances its credibility and 
trust, key components of soft power. 
 
3.38 When asked about the challenges faced in successful adaptation in 
these countries, the Ministry stated that the major challenges  primarily 
are first, limited institutional capacity, as sustained digital transformation 
requires strong programme management and cross-ministry 
coordination that many countries are still developing. Second, evolving 
legal frameworks, with data protection, digital identity, and payment 
regulations needing to keep pace with technological adoption. Third, 
infrastructure gaps, as reliable connectivity, electricity, and device access 
remain uneven across the Global South. Fourth, and most 
fundamentally, trust and inclusion - DPI must be safe, inclusive, and 
interoperable, as emphasised by the G20 DEWG framework, since 
exclusion of marginalised groups can deepen existing inequalities rather 
3.39 Complementing the replies of Meity, MEA in a written submission 
stated that sharing India's developmental learnings and implementation 
methodology through capacity building ITEC courses is a regular and on-
going feature of our development cooperation with countries in the 
Global South. These courses are structured as 2-week immersive 
residential programmes which provide middle-to-senior level officials of 
foreign governments exposure to both models of digital governance and 
India Stack/ India's DPIs in various sectors like Identity, payments and 
data exchange. These courses are conducted both in the multi-country 

67 | P a g e 
 
and single-country/on-request formats. Apart from such courses, India 
has also set up close to 50 Centres of Excellence in IT (in 48 countries of 
the Global south) which provide graduate-level IT skills to young 
professionals in educational institutions of those countries. It is 
estimated that more than 57000 graduates have benefitted from these 
centres. 
 
3.40 The Committee also desired to know how India is leveraging DPI 
diplomatically to build long-term strategic partnerships with Global South 
nations, and how concerns about data security, digital sovereignty and 
standards alignment are addressed in the course of adoption. To this, 
MeitY stated that the Indian Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI) model 
follows a balanced approach, combining government-led, secure, open-
source DPI with private sector innovation. It is neither highly centralized 
and tightly state controlled- that risks a trust deficit and raises 
surveillance concerns nor predominantly market-driven, where 
proprietary and profit-led systems can undermine citizen-centricity, 
inclusion and affordability. India’s approach to building DPI is reflected in 
the G20 New Delhi Declaration which recognised that safe, secure, 
trusted, accountable and inclusive digital public infrastructure, respectful 
of human rights, personal data, privacy and intellectual property rights 
can foster resilience, and enable service delivery and innovation. 
3.41 India’s value-driven approach- coupled with demonstrable 
development outcomes enabled by the population-scale development of 
its DPI- has attracted significant interest across the Global South. This is 
reflected in the signing of twenty-three (23) MOUs on ‘Cooperation in 
the Field of Sharing Successful Digital Solutions Implemented at 
Population Scale for Digital Transformation’ with diverse countries 
helping them build their own robust digital ecosystems using proven 
Indian models. Addressing concerns related to data security, digital 
sovereignty, and standards alignment in partner countries is an 
extension of our strong domestic emphasis on these principles-applied 
with an even stronger diligence in overseas contexts. This is done 
through customizing our digital solutions to adhere to local cyber, data 
protection and privacy laws; zero-trust or minimal-trust architecture 

68 | P a g e 
 
design; open-source technology; in-built consent-based data sharing; 
adherence to minimal data collection and storage principles and regular 
third-party auditing. Further, upon completion, the partner country 
retains full ownership of the project. 
3.42 The developmental gains realized by partner countries through 
Indian DPI are not limited to delivering local outcomes- they create 
lasting synergies between digital ecosystems and strengthen confidence 
in our solutions, unlocking new and untapped opportunities for our 
industry. As partner countries’ needs evolve, the innovations developed 
to meet them make Indian solutions more agile and future ready. This 
mutually reinforcing cycle of value creation deepens strategic alignment 
and lays the foundation for enduring strategic partnerships.  
 
3.43 The Committee further were keen to know about the lessons 
learnt by India in terms of digital inclusion and data privacy and way in 
which it can be shared globally. The Ministry of Electronics and 
Information Technology in a written reply stated that India’s DPI journey 
shows that inclusion must be deliberately designed and anchored in 
trust. Platforms like Aadhaar and Unified Payments Interface (UPI) 
demonstrate that scale comes from simplicity, interoperability, and 
strong last-mile enablement supported by assisted access, multilingual 
design, and low-cost infrastructure. At the same time, the Digital 
Personal Data Protection Act, 2023 underscores that sustainable 
adoption depends on privacy-by-design, consent-based data use, and 
institutional accountability. The digital systems succeed only when they 
are inclusive by architecture and trusted by citizens. Globally, India can 
translate these lessons by positioning DPI as a public good sharing 
open, interoperable digital building block, strengthening capacity in 
partner countries, and advancing cooperation through multilateral 
platforms.  
(a) The Global Digital Public Infrastructure Repository-GDPIR 
(https://www.dpi.global/), an initiative of the Indian G20 
Presidency, is already operational. The GDPIR is designed to be a 
resource for key lessons and knowledge available from G20 
members and guest countries, enabling easy discoverability. It is 

69 | P a g e 
 
aimed at addressing the existing knowledge gap around the right 
practices to design, build, and deploy population scale DPI. Each 
participant, based on whether their DPI are built on open 
platforms, APIs, standards, and protocols, can choose to display 
any information at their discretion, which can help others to 
develop their DPI. Currently, the GDPIR features 54 DPIs from 16 
countries, with India being the largest contributor. It may be 
accessed at https://www.dpi.globalhttps://www.dpi.global/ 
 
(b) India Stack Global: India has implemented innovative digital 
projects, population-scale transformations, and ensured digital 
inclusion, contributing to global digital public goods repositories 
and providing affordable access to digital services. Currently 18 
DPIs are included in India Stack Global 
(https://www.indiastack.global/). 
 
(c) India’s Digital Public Infrastructures (DPIs) such as Aadhaar, 
UPI, UMANG, DigiLocker, CoWIN, FASTag, etc are now studied and 
adopted globally. India has signed Memorandum of Understanding 
(MoU) with 24 countries to share its DPI to enhance digital 
transformation, improve public service delivery, and foster 
innovation. 
 
3.44 Asked as to how India views its role in shaping the global 
conversation on digital sovereignty, and whether a policy framework 
addresses the sovereignty of nations over their data and technology 
infrastructure, MeitY stated that India advocates for a balanced digital 
sovereignty model that enables nations to leverage global innovation 
while maintaining strict sovereign oversight over their data and critical 
digital infrastructure. This approach is reflected in policy frameworks 
such as the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023, which establishes 
safeguards for lawful data processing while regulating its cross-border 
flow. Alongside this, India promotes open, interoperable Digital Public 
Infrastructure (DPI) anchored in principles of security, inclusivity, and 
trust while supporting nations in building their own sovereign digital 
capabilities through partnerships and capacity-building initiatives. During 

70 | P a g e 
 
the G20 Presidency and through the "Global Digital Public Infrastructure 
Repository," India actively promotes a decentralized digital order that 
respects the right of every nation to govern its data and digital destiny. 
 
3.45 During the testimony on 17 November 2025, the representative of 
the Ministry of External Affairs situated partnership with the Global 
South by stating as under: 
“Finally, turning to our partnership with the Global South, which is 
a very important work that we do in the Ministry, I would like to 
submit that India's platform like Aadhaar, UPI, DigiLocker, CoWIN, 
demonstrate that technology - when designed for scale and 
inclusion - can deliver both efficiency and equity. Internationally, 
through the India Stack Global Initiative, countries are partnering 
with India to implement similar frameworks showcasing how our 
digital transformation can inspire global cooperation.  
You asked me about how we are taking our DPI model to our 
partners in Global South. I am very happy to mention to you that 
we have discussed with a number of countries as to how we can 
support them and partner with them. We already have 
partnership, cooperative MOU with 22 members. We are already 
working in Sri Lanka for developing their unique identity system 
like Aadhaar. At the population level, we have a programme with 
Kenya where we are piloting a DigiLocker model that we have in 
India. Kenya has shown interest there. Similarly, we are working 
with other countries also, like Namibia, Peru, who have shown 
interest in our DPI infrastructure… 
There is a tremendous interest in both of our African partners to 
learn from our experience of rolling out DPI at a one-billion-plus 
scale. India's engagement in technology diplomacy is also 
underpinned by established expertise in IT and cyber domains, 
leading numerous development partners to seek assistance 
through primarily two structured verticals. One is Centre for 
Excellence in IT Model and institution-based training under the 
Indian Technical and Economic Cooperation Scheme, or ITEC, as 

71 | P a g e 
 
we call it. Under the first framework of CEIT model, we started our 
journey with a hub and spoke project in Ghana, way back in 2002, 
about 20 years back. And that model has grown considerably since 
then. We have done several projects. We have done 50 projects in 
47 countries, which have in turn trained nearly two lakh individuals 
across subjects like big data, AI, machine language, and IT skills in 
general. It is a very successful programme of India and developed 
a partnership with other countries. Similarly, as I mentioned, under 
the ITEC programme, we do train a number of people every year 
on various programmes in the area of technology. At least 500 to 
600 people are coming every year for advanced courses in IIIT, in 
CDAC, and other institutions of excellence in India. In the space 
domain also, we are engaging with the Global South by providing 
capacity building programmes through initiatives such as launch of 
the South Asia satellite, India-Bhutan satellite launched in 2022, 
and also supporting countries in building satellites or providing 
geospatial information through dedicated platforms.” 
IX. MULTILATERAL ENGAGEMENT: G20 AND BRICS 
3.46 The Committee also enquired how India's role in platforms such as 
the G20 and BRICS is influencing the global governance of digital 
technologies. MeitY replied that, at the global level, India has played a 
proactive role in advancing inclusive, secure and development-oriented 
digital governance through both platforms. 
G20 Contributions: Under its 2023 Presidency, India spearheaded the 
first-ever multilateral consensus on DPI through the Digital Economy 
Working Group (DEWG) led by MeitY. 
● New Delhi Leaders' Declaration (NDLD): The Digital Economy 
Ministers' Meeting (DEMM) unanimously endorsed three DPI 
deliverables — a Framework for building DPI, mobilising finances 
for DPI development in Low- and Middle-Income Countries, and 
the creation of a Global DPI Repository (GDPIR). 

72 | P a g e 
 
● Global DPI Repository (GDPIR):  to facilitate knowledge 
sharing and mutual learning in DPI development GDPIR portal 
established. Currently, the GDPIR features 54 DPIs from 16 
countries, and it can be accessed at https://www.dpi.global. 
● Social Impact Fund (SIF):  During the Virtual Leaders’ Summit 
2023 held in November, 2023, the Hon’ble Prime Minister of India 
announced the creation of the Social Impact Fund (SIF) for DPI 
implementation in global south nations. India has also committed 
to contribute an initial USD25 million to the fund. 
● G20 Toolkit on Cyber Education and Cyber Awareness of 
Children and Youth : The toolkit provides a concise, practice-
oriented framework for policymakers to design effective online 
safety initiatives. Based on global inputs, it highlights key priorities 
such as age-specific risk classification, stronger response and 
support systems, a multi-stakeholder approach, enhanced 
international cooperation, and a central role for industry and digital 
platforms in ensuring child online safety. 
● Data for Development: G20 High-Level Principles on Harnessing 
Data for Development (D4D) provides a framework to leverage 
data as an enabler for achieving Sustainable Development Goals 
(SDGs). They emphasise improving data availability/quality, 
fostering responsible data sharing, and strengthening governance 
frameworks for inclusive evidence-based policymaking. 
● Troika Continuity: Through the India–Brazil–South Africa Troika 
Joint Communiqué on DPI, AI and Data for Governance, India 
ensured the agenda's continuation across successive presidencies. 
BRICS Contributions: 
● BRICS Leaders' Statement on Global Governance of AI 
(Rio Summit, July 2025):  India actively shaped this landmark 
declaration. Honourable Prime Minister Sri Narendra Modi 
welcomed the Statement as a positive step and announced that 
India would organise the "AI Impact Summit" in 2026, articulating 

73 | P a g e 
 
India's "AI for All" approach. The Statement endorsed UN-led, 
sovereignty-respecting AI governance prioritising the Global South. 
● UPI Internationalisation:  Honourable Prime Minister Sri 
Narendra Modi has pitched for UPI's expansion into the BRICS 
grouping to enhance cross-border remittances. 
● Capacity Building:  India hosted a Virtual Capacity Building 
Session on Digital Transformation in BRICS through the National 
Communications Academy – Technology, focusing on mobile 
security, cyber resilience, and digital infrastructure. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

74 | P a g e 
 
CHAPTER 4 
OUTER SPACE DIPLOMACY 
Space applications are now central to daily socio-economic 
activities, including communications, navigation, climate monitoring, and 
disaster management. In international law and diplomacy, the term 
“outer space” carries a specific meaning. It refers to the region beyond 
the atmosphere above States territory where it exercises sovereign 
control over the airspace and in which no State may claim sovereignty 
and all States enjoy freedom of exploration and use. 
4.1 The growing number of actors involved in space exploration, 
particularly on the Moon and potentially on Mars and other celestial 
bodies, increases the possibility of disputes and conflict. To mitigate the 
risk of confrontation in outer space, the United Nations has established 
five major international treaties governing space activities: the Outer 
Space Treaty (1967), the Rescue Agreement (1968), the Liability 
Convention (1972), the Registration Convention (1976), and the Moon 
Agreement (1979). These instruments seek to promote peaceful 
exploration, regulate state responsibility, and prevent conflicts arising 
from activities in outer space. India is a party to four and a signatory to 
the Moon Agreement.  
 
I. SPACE DIPLOMACY ARCHITECTURE 
4.2 India's space diplomacy is conducted through a distinctive 
institutional arrangement in which the Department of Space including 
the Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) and the Indian National 
Space Promotion and Authorisation Centre (IN-SPACe) exercise a degree 
of direct international engagement uncommon among technical 
departments of Government, working in close coordination with the 
Ministry of External Affairs. The Department of Space (DoS) international 
cooperation functions, together with the commercial and international 
marketing activities historically undertaken through Antrix Corporation 
and now substantially through NewSpace India Limited (NSIL), give the 
space establishment a standing capacity to negotiate technical and 

75 | P a g e 
 
commercial space agreements that is then situated within the broader 
framework of India's foreign policy. 
4.3 The Ministry of External Affairs undertakes space diplomacy and 
space cooperation through engagement at the bilateral and multilateral 
levels including on matters relating to space safety and space security. 
To this end, it works closely with a range of stakeholders, including 
Department of Space, ISRO and IN-SPACE. The salience of space 
domain due to its applications in daily life has given an impetus to 
requests to strengthen space cooperation from partner countries. The 
Ministry of External Affairs plays an active role in the negotiation of 
Agreements/Memorandums of Understanding for cooperation in the 
peaceful uses of outer space.  On the multilateral front, India remains 
committed to a legally-binding instrument on the prevention of an arms 
race in outer space, which is universally acceptable, verifiable and 
multilaterally negotiated in the Conference on Disarmament.  
 
4.4 On the Committee’s query about existence of a specific Division 
within MEA to deal with India’s Outer Space diplomacy, the Ministry 
stated that the Disarmament and International Security Affairs (DISA) 
Division works closely with a range of stakeholders, including 
Department of Space, ISRO and IN-SPACe. Headed by the Joint 
Secretary, two officers are dealing with space matters and are assisted 
by the Section. 
 
4.5 The Ministry further stated that Indian Missions/Posts abroad 
being the first point of contact for foreign partners, requests for space 
cooperation are often received through diplomatic channels and 
conveyed to ISRO/ IN-SPACe, Department of Space. The DISA Division 
plays a facilitating role in setting up the interactions between the two 
sides and further , in the negotiation of Agreements/ MoU for 
cooperation. It also conveys the Ministry’s clearance and its Legal and 
Treaties Division’s clearance from international law angle in respect of 
the text before the conclusion of such a document. The DISA Division 
participates in space dialogues for exchange of views on matters 
pertaining to outer space on bilateral and multilateral fronts, including 

76 | P a g e 
 
review of existing space cooperation and potential areas for further 
collaboration. It also raises specific issues of importance to ISRO, such 
as supply of critical goods and technology, under its dialogue 
mechanisms with different countries, such as for disarmament and non-
proliferation, and through Indian Missions/Posts. 
4.6 On the multilateral front, the Permanent Mission to the Conference 
on Disarmament (CD) in Geneva participates in the discussions and 
negotiations at various forums, such as the First Committee, CD and the 
Open-Ended Working Group (OEWG) for the Prevention of an Arms Race 
in Outer Space (PAROS). 
4.7 When asked about the manner in which India is integrating its 
growing space capabilities and emerging technology strengths into a 
coherent external technology diplomacy framework, the Ministry stated 
that India navigates these developments through a balanced and 
forward-looking approach by advancing national capabilities through 
ISRO while promoting inclusive cooperation with developing countries. 
The 2023 Indian Space Policy enables participation of the private sector 
in the space economy and reinforces global engagement. Projects such 
as the South Asia Satellite and NASA-ISRO’s Synthetic Aperture Radar, 
demonstrate India’s technological strength along with sustained 
diplomatic outreach. 
4.8 As regards the cornerstones or guiding principles of India’s Outer 
Space Diplomacy, the Ministry of External Affairs stated that  as a major 
spacefaring country, India has vital developmental and security interests 
in outer space. Related matters are both technical and political in nature. 
As a founding member of the Committee on Peaceful Uses of Outer 
Space (COPUOS), India has been a consistent advocate for preserving 
the outer space as an ever-expanding frontier for cooperative 
endeavours. It has opposed its weaponisation and remains committed to 
a legally binding instrument on the prevention of an arms race in outer 
space which is universally acceptable, verifiable and multilaterally 
negotiated in the CD. 
 

77 | P a g e 
 
II. BILATERAL AND MULTILATERAL SPACE COOPERATION 
4.9 Given that India has launched more than 400 foreign satellites, the 
Committee wanted to know about the measures that have been taken to 
maintain India’s cost-competitiveness and reliability amid competition 
from private global players. In a written reply, the Ministry of External 
Affairs stated that measures are being taken to ensure the cost 
competitiveness through many developmental activities including 
reusability, standardisation, indigenisation, advance propulsion systems, 
and production through industry. Indian private industries are involved in 
manufacturing of ISRO’s operational launch vehicles viz. PSLV through 
industry consortium and SSLV through technology transfer. With the 
increase in volume, such competitiveness can be ensured. Reliability is 
ensured by adopting ISRO’s Quality Assurance practices. 
4.10 Some other important initiatives that are being implemented to 
promote start-ups in the space sector include a Rs. 1000 cr. Antariksh 
Venture Capital fund to encourage private investment, a Technology 
Adoption Fund of Rs. 500 cr. for enabling technology development and 
commercialization, a seed fund scheme to encourage start-ups and 
NGEs to develop the space technologies and applications, providing 
technical assistance to the industry at no cost basis through resident 
experts engaged by IN-SPACe and providing support to State 
Governments for setting up of Space Manufacturing cluster and financial 
assistance to establish common technical facilities. 
4.11 Regarding the collaborative projects being explored to advance 
India’s role as a leading space player and a partner for the global 
community, the Ministry replied as follows: 
a. India and France have also jointly realized two satellite missions - 
Megha-Tropiques (2011) and SARAL (2013). Both the satellites 
outlived the planned life and provided valuable scientific data. A 
third joint satellite to monitor the impact of the climatic change is 
in the pipeline. The IA for jointly realizing the Thermal Infra-Red 
earth observation satellite mission named as TRISHNA was signed 
in 2023. The realization of the satellite is progressing well, with the 
completion of the Preliminary Design Review for the bus elements 

78 | P a g e 
 
and the payloads. ISRO officials also participated in the 
international science workshop on TRISHNA held in France. 
ISRO and JAXA (Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency) have 
carried out a feasibility study to realize a joint lunar polar 
exploration mission to characterize volatiles and composition in the 
lunar polar region. Both sides signed an Implementing 
Arrangement in August 2025 for conducting the joint mission in 
2028. Subsequently, technical visits have been undertaken and a 
high level LUPEX progress review committee has been constituted 
with senior officials from ISRO and JAXA as co-chairs. 
ISRO will also be accommodating instruments from Sweden and 
Russia in its Venus Orbiter Mission that is envisaged to orbit a 
scientific spacecraft in the orbit of planet Venus for better 
understanding of the Venusian surface and subsurface, 
atmospheric processes and influence of Sun on Venusian 
atmosphere. 
India is also working towards the realizing G20 Satellite for 
environment and climate studies, which will carry instruments from 
many countries including France, UK, Australia, Italy, Mexico and 
South Africa.  
b. The Ministry facilitates bilateral and multilateral space cooperation. 
To this end, it works closely with a range of stakeholders, including 
Department of Space, ISRO and IN-SPACe. The Ministry plays an 
active role in the negotiation of framework agreements/MoUs for 
cooperation in the peaceful uses of outer space. Clearance is 
conveyed before the conclusion of such documents.  
The Ministry regularly includes space cooperation as an agenda in 
dialogues with many countries, thus, ensuring political support. It also 
funds specific joint activities for space cooperation with partner 
countries.  
4.12 Asked to provide a brief note on India's landmark achievements in 
its space programme, and the areas in which India can offer leadership 
to the world, and to developing countries in particular, the Ministry 

79 | P a g e 
 
stated that India's space programme has achieved remarkable 
milestones combining technological excellence with cost efficiency. 
These include the Chandrayaan-1 mission's discovery of water molecules 
on the Moon, the Mars Orbiter Mission that made India the first country 
to reach Mars orbit on its maiden attempt, and the Chandrayaan-3 
mission's soft landing near the lunar south pole, alongside demonstrated 
global launch capability through reliable and economical launch vehicles, 
and ISRO's fleet of satellites for communication, navigation (NavIC) and 
Earth observation that support governance and development. 
4.13 India, the Ministry assessed, is well positioned to play a leadership 
role, especially vis-à-vis developing countries: by emphasising inclusive 
growth, sustainability and the peaceful uses of outer space, India can 
bridge the divide between advanced spacefaring and space-aspiring 
countries and contribute to shaping an inclusive global space ecosystem. 
The democratisation of space technology, through low-cost launch 
services, the sharing of satellite data, regional navigation and 
communication services, spacecraft building, and application-driven 
solutions, holds value for the Global South, with India's capacity-building 
and collaborative missions able to help develop indigenous capabilities in 
satellite building and downstream services in partner countries. 
 
4.14 About the current efforts made by the Ministry to leverage these 
achievements to promote India's outer space diplomacy, the Ministry of 
External Affairs stated that space is increasingly identified as an area of 
cooperation in the discussions and outcome documents of high-level 
engagements with both spacefaring and aspiring countries. Recent 
achievements, such as Chandrayaan-3's soft landing on the lunar south 
polar region, have increased international appreciation of India's space 
capabilities, which support the Ministry’s efforts to further India's 
relations with partner countries, including by ensuring support for, and in 
certain cases funding, joint ISRO/IN-SPACe space cooperation activities 
with friendly partner countries such as Bhutan, Mauritius and the Pacific 
Island Countries. 
 

80 | P a g e 
 
4.15 When asked whether India envisages a leadership role in shaping 
norms relating to space governance, satellite data sharing and dual-use 
technologies, the Ministry of External Affairs stated that India is 
contributing to global space governance through its participation in 
COPUOS, the Inter-Agency Space Debris Coordination Committee, the 
Artemis Accords and other multilateral platforms, and is currently 
chairing the COPUOS Working Group on the Long-Term Sustainability of 
Outer Space Activities. ISRO is a key player in the deliberations of the 
Committee on Earth Observation Satellites and the Group on Earth 
Observations, through which data from Indian satellites is shared with 
the global science community for weather forecasting and natural 
resource assessment, while on dual-use technologies, an inter-
ministerial delegation participates actively in multilateral export control 
regimes such as the Wassenaar Arrangement and the Missile Technology 
Control Regime. 
4.16 On the leadership role India envisages for itself in future 
multilateral negotiations on space and emerging technologies, the 
Ministry of External Affairs stated that India envisions itself as a credible 
voice for the Global South in such negotiations. Its chairmanship of the 
Working Group on the Long-Term Sustainability of Outer Space Activities 
since 2021, its championing of COPUOS as the primary forum for space
