governance, and its signing of the Artemis Accords in 2023 have 
signalled its readiness to help shape future cislunar governance. 
Initiatives such as the G20 Satellite Mission for Environment and Climate 
Observation, capacity-building programmes offered through CSSTEAP 
and IIRS, and projects such as the South Asia Satellite have boosted 
India's soft power, guided throughout by constructive engagement with 
partners of varying capacities and by support for norms that promote 
equal access to space. 
4.17 Further, India envisions this future leadership role as grounded in 
the principles of Vasudhaiva Kutumbakam and Sarvajan Sukhaya, 
Sarvajan Hitaya (happiness for all, welfare for all), positioning itself as a 
Vishwabandhu, or trusted global partner. India promotes the idea of 
technology for all — safe, secure and responsible — and consistently 
raises issues of access, affordability, reliability and safety, particularly 

81 | P a g e 
 
from the perspective of the Global South, strengthening its role in space 
and emerging technologies through relevant partnerships and 
cooperation.  
 
4.18 Elaborating on the key challenges associated with outer space 
governance more broadly, the Ministry of External Affairs stated that 
outer space governance faces mounting challenges, including orbital 
congestion, space debris, the extraction and utilisation of resources from 
celestial bodies, and legal frameworks that predate commercial 
spaceflight, with existing treaties lacking adequate mechanisms to 
address these challenges. 
 
4.19 Apprising the Committee about India’s Outer Space Diplomacy 
during testimony on 17 November 2025, the representatives of the 
Ministry of External Affairs stated as under: 
“Turning to outer space, yet another pillar of India's technological 
ecosystem is its leadership in space technology. From the success 
of Chandrayaan-3 to the space docking experiment which 
demonstrated satellite docking capability in low Earth orbit, our 
achievements have placed India among the world's leading space-
faring nations. The upcoming Gaganyaan human spaceflight 
mission and the plan for a Bharatiya Antariksh Station by 2035 
mark a new frontier in our space journey. As a major space-faring 
country, India has vital developmental and security interests in the 
space domain. India has been a consistent advocate of preserving 
outer space as an ever-expanding frontier for cooperative 
endeavours. 
At the bilateral level, we have expanded our space partnership with 
countries like France, Japan, the US, and of course, Russia. At the 
multilateral front, India remains committed to a legally binding 
instrument on the prevention of an arms race in outer space which 
is universally acceptable, verifiable, and multilaterally negotiated. 
India is a founding member of the Committee on the Peaceful Uses 
of Outer Space and is a very active participant in all its work. The 
Axiom-4 mission featuring Group Captain Shubhanshu Shukla's 

82 | P a g e 
 
journey to the International Space Station, and the NISAR mission, 
a collaboration between NASA and ISRO, represent significant 
milestones in our collaborative programmes. India has also 
committed itself to responsible and sustainable space exploration 
through its Debris-Free Space Mission Initiative, pledging that all 
national missions will be debris-free by 2030.” 
4.20 The Committee were keen to know whether India's low-cost space 
missions have helped position it as an attractive space partner, 
particularly for developing countries, and what steps the Government is 
taking to promote India as a global leader and trusted partner. To this, 
the Ministry of External Affairs replied that Indian space missions are 
known for their cost-effectiveness, which has helped develop relations 
with partners on both commercial and cooperative fronts. More than 30 
countries have used India's cost-effective space launch services to 
launch nearly 400 of their satellites, and space sector reforms have 
enabled the growth of a commercial space ecosystem that will soon 
provide launch services to global customers. The joint satellites 
successfully developed with France (Megha-Tropiques and SARAL) and 
the United States (NISAR) have helped build trust among partners, 
further exemplified by future missions such as TRISHNA with France and 
LUPEX with Japan, collectively helping position India as a global hub for 
space activities. Since the space sector reforms initiated in 2020, the 
Indian private sector has also developed notable business relationship 
with foreign commercial space entities. Additionally foreign companies 
have set up their business operations in India. 
4.21 On being asked about the ways in which the Ministry of External 
Affairs supports ISRO in expanding these global efforts, the Ministry 
stated that its support takes multiple forms: the establishment of 
mechanisms for space dialogue; strengthening bilateral and multilateral 
space cooperation through high-level discussions and joint statements; 
addressing export control issues; facilitating the training of officials from 
other countries on space technology utilisation for various applications; 
and providing funding support for specific space activities with strategic 
partner countries. 

83 | P a g e 
 
The Artemis Accords  
4.22 The Artemis Accords are a practical set of principles, guidelines 
and best practices to enhance the governance of the civil exploration 
and use of outer space with the intention of advancing the international 
cooperation for the Artemis programme. India has signed the Artemis 
Accord on June 21, 2023, becoming the 27 th signatory. However, India 
is not a participant in the NASA led Artemis Programme, which is a 
mission-driven initiative led by NASA to return humans to the Moon and 
beyond. India has outlined its own ambitious vision for space exploration 
under its Space Vision 2047. This includes missions such as 
Chandrayaan series for lunar exploration and Gaganyaan continuation 
programme leading to Bhartiya Antariksha Station, reflecting India’s 
vision for advancements in space exploration. However, ISRO will 
explore the possibilities of participation in the Artemis programme in 
future. 
4.23 India-USA space cooperation is anchored by the interaction 
between the two countries’ space relations through the Civil Space Joint 
Working Group (CSJWG). Both ISRO and NASA have expressed interest 
in continuing the strong cooperation built under NISAR and new 
proposals were discussed during the ninth meeting of the CSJWG held in 
August 2026.  With regard to collaborative missions with other countries, 
ISRO is already engaged with JAXA/Japan for the joint lunar mission 
Chandrayaan-5/ LuPEX and with CNES/France for TRISHNA mission. 
 
India in UN COPUOS and International Space Law 
4.24 India has been a member of the United Nations Committee on the 
Peaceful Uses of Outer Space since its establishment and has been a 
state party to the principal instruments of international space law, 
including the Outer Space Treaty, 1967, the Rescue Agreement, 1968, 
the Liability Convention, 1972, and the Registration Convention, 1975. 
4.25 On COPUOS and India's role within it, the Ministry stated that the 
Committee, established in 1958 by UN General Assembly Resolution 
1348, began as an 18-member body and now counts 104 members, 

84 | P a g e 
 
overseeing the implementation of five foundational space treaties 
through its Scientific and Technical, and Legal, Subcommittees. India is a 
founding member and has made significant contributions to its work: Dr 
Vikram Sarabhai served as Vice-President and Scientific Chairman of the 
first UN Conference on the Exploration and Peaceful Uses of Outer Space 
(UNISPACE-I) in 1968; Professor Yashpal served as Secretary-General of 
UNISPACE-II in 1982; and Professor U.R. Rao served as President of 
UNISPACE-III in 1999 and as Chairman of COPUOS from 1997 to 1999. 
India's insistence on absolute State liability directly shaped the landmark 
Liability Convention of 1972. India also chairs the Working Group on the 
Long-Term Sustainability of Outer Space Activities, championing debris 
mitigation and equal access to outer space for developing countries. 
4.26 India's engagement extends to the wider UN space architecture. 
The Permanent Mission of India to the International Organisations in 
Vienna remains engaged with the UN Office for Outer Space Affairs 
(UNOOSA) as the Secretariat for COPUOS. Indian delegations regularly 
participate in the meetings of COPUOS and its sub-committees, while 
the Department of Telecommunications and the Department of Space 
participate in the meetings of the International Telecommunication 
Union, principally to coordinate orbital resources for space missions and 
to contribute to associated policymaking. 
 
4.27 When enquired about the challenges involved in promoting the 
peaceful uses of outer space, and the areas of convergence and 
divergence among States, the Ministry of External Affairs identified 
orbital congestion, debris accumulation, dual-use technologies, the 
blurring of civilian-military lines, the issue of dark and quiet skies, and 
inadequate governance of private commercial activities as key 
challenges, noting that the Outer Space Treaty of 1967 provides 
foundational principles but struggles to address contemporary 
complexities, and that consensus-based processes within COPUOS are 
time-consuming. While there is universal acceptance of the ‘peaceful 
purposes’ principle and of the 2019 voluntary, non-binding guidelines for 
the Long-Term Sustainability of Outer Space Activities, divergences 
persist in the evolving space environment over preferred negotiating 

85 | P a g e 
 
fora, the nature of instruments, and commitments toward capacity 
building for developing countries. 
 
4.28 Regarding the steps India is taking, in coordination with other 
global actors, to prevent the weaponisation of outer space, the Ministry 
of External Affairs reiterated that India has been a consistent advocate 
of preserving outer space as an ever-expanding frontier for cooperative 
endeavours, has opposed its weaponisation, and remains committed to a 
legally binding instrument on the prevention of an arms race in outer 
space that is universally acceptable, verifiable and multilaterally 
negotiated in the Conference on Disarmament, participating to this end 
in the First Committee, the Conference on Disarmament, and the OEWG 
for PAROS. 
 
4.29 To a query on how India is advancing its advocacy for a legally 
binding instrument to prevent an arms race in outer space, the Ministry 
of External Affairs stated that India supports substantive consideration 
of PAROS in the Conference on Disarmament, where it has been on the 
agenda since 1982, including negotiations in a subsidiary body as part of 
the Programme of Work. While universal and non-discriminatory 
transparency and confidence-building measures can play a useful 
complementary role, the Ministry was clear that they cannot substitute 
for legally binding instruments. India's own commitment 
notwithstanding, the legally binding instrument is not an Indian 
initiative: India participated actively in consultations called by the 
European Union over a decade ago to discuss a draft Code of Conduct 
for Outer Space Activities, while a draft treaty on the prevention of the 
placement of weapons in outer space and the threat or use of force 
against outer space objects was jointly proposed by Russia and China to 
the Conference on Disarmament in 2008, with a revised version 
submitted in 2014. 
 
 
 

86 | P a g e 
 
III. SPACE SECURITY, DEBRIS MITIGATION AND 
SITUATIONAL AWARENESS 
4.30 On being asked about the manner in which India is addressing the 
risks of space debris and the weaponisation of space, the Ministry of 
External Affairs replied that, as a responsible spacefaring nation, India 
upholds its commitment to preserving the long-term sustainability of 
outer space activities through the implementation of mitigation 
guidelines recommended by UN-COPUOS and the Inter-Agency Space 
Debris Coordination Committee (IADC). India engages actively in the 
activities of the International Academy of Astronautics Space Debris 
Working Group, the International Astronautical Federation's Space Traffic 
Management Technical Committee, and the International Organisation 
for Standardisation's Working Group 7, through space-debris-related 
technical contributions, and follows the practice of registering all Indian 
space objects with regular submission to the UN. India has remained 
consistently opposed to the weaponisation of outer space. 
4.31 The Indian Space Policy places significant importance on space 
debris mitigation requirements and space situational awareness (SSA) 
capacity building. The Debris Free Space Mission (DFSM) initiative, 
spearheaded by ISRO, aims to achieve debris-free space missions by all 
Indian space actors, governmental and non-governmental, by 2030, 
aligning with global efforts for space sustainability and positioning India 
as a nation prioritising safety, security and sustainability in outer space 
activities. The ISRO System for Safe and Sustainable Space Operations 
Management (IS4OM) functions as the nodal entity coordinating 
activities to address the growing challenges of operating safely in the 
presence of space debris: regular Collision Avoidance (COLA) 
assessments ensure safe, collision-free lift-off times within designated 
launch windows, and continual close-approach analyses are performed 
for all operational spacecraft to mitigate collision risk with catalogued 
space objects. More proactive efforts underway include the de-orbiting 
of Low Earth Orbit satellites at end-of-life, the de-orbiting of PSLV upper 
stages to very low altitudes after payload injection, and design-level 
measures for newer missions to meet this objective, with mitigation 

87 | P a g e 
 
measures also being implemented, to the extent practicable, for 
missions designed prior to the declaration of the DFSM. As part of space 
situational awareness capacity building, the Network for space object 
Tracking and Analysis (NETRA) project is making steady progress in 
establishing dedicated radar and optical telescope facilities within India. 
The Network for Space Object Tracking and Analysis (NETRA) 
4.32 NETRA is an indigenous capacity-building project envisaging the 
establishment of a network and systems for space situational awareness, 
consisting of three major elements: a Radar Observation System, an 
Optical Telescope Observation System, and an SSA Control Centre. The 
establishment of an optical telescope at Hanle, Ladakh, is underway, and 
efforts to establish an indigenously developed radar in north-eastern 
India are in progress. 
 
4.33 To a query on the extent to which ISRO has been successful in 
space object tracking and analysis, the Ministry stated that IS4OM is 
operational and functions as the nodal entity coordinating efforts 
pertaining to space sustainability, including SSA activities to safeguard 
national space assets against various space environmental hazards. The 
necessary analysis packages to process observation data from 
telescopes and radars have been developed and deployed at the SSA 
Control Centre, and the Multi-Object Tracking Radar (MOTR) at 
Sriharikota currently tracks space objects, including Indian rocket bodies 
and satellites such as POEM-4, during their last few orbits before 
atmospheric re-entry, wherever feasible. 
Perils of Space Debris 
4.34 The Ministry informed that space debris poses serious threats to 
operational assets. Any collision can have catastrophic or mission-ending 
consequences owing to high-velocity impact damage, and improperly 
disposed debris can sometimes self-explode, adding to the debris 
population. Some debris eventually re-enters the atmosphere and 
fragments; these fragments can survive the intense heat of re-entry and 
pose risks to life and property. 

88 | P a g e 
 
4.35 As regards the challenges in achieving debris-free space and the 
progress made so far, the Ministry of External Affairs identified the 
absence of ‘rules of the road’ for space traffic management with regard 
to collision avoidance; the lack of satellite registration with owner details 
that can be contacted to mitigate collision risks; limitations in observing 
small debris, below 10 centimetres in size, that can nonetheless cause 
mission-ending damage; and the absence of commitments or a 
standardised mechanism for sharing orbital data to improve the accuracy 
of collision analysis. Adopting debris mitigation measures at the early 
design stage, rather than as an afterthought, is essential; mission 
extensions beyond a spacecraft's design life compromise post-mission 
disposal capability by depleting fuel and risking the loss of systems 
critical for de-orbiting; and a lack of awareness of the worsening debris 
scenario, together with a failure to adopt internationally accepted 
mitigation guidelines to the maximum extent possible, further 
compounds these challenges. 
4.36 When asked about the role India can continue to play in achieving 
a debris-free space environment, and how it is advancing advocacy for a 
legally binding instrument to this end, the Ministry of External Affairs 
reiterated the DFSM initiative's 2030 target for all Indian space actors, 
governmental and non-governmental, noting that all upper stages of 
Indian launch vehicle missions are passivated, by the removal of excess 
propellant at end-of-mission, to minimise the risk of accidental post-
mission break-up and consequent debris creation. As a responsible 
spacefaring country, India continues to implement mitigation guidelines 
recommended by UN-COPUOS and the IADC, and to engage actively 
with the International Academy of Astronautics, the International 
Astronautical Federation, and the International Organisation for 
Standardisation on debris-related technical contributions, while following 
the practice of registering all Indian objects with the UN. 
 
4.37 The Committee also examined two further dimensions of space 
security. Asked whether the building of a global surveillance network by 
China may have strategic implications for India, including for space 
situational awareness, the Ministry of External Affairs stated that existing 

89 | P a g e 
 
global policies do not restrict satellite imaging by one country of 
another's territory. Separately, on the implications for India's data and 
technology sovereignty in dealing with companies such as SpaceX and 
Starlink, the Ministry stated that SpaceX has expressed interest in 
providing broadband service through the Starlink satellite system in 
India, that IN-SPACe's authorisation had already been issued to Starlink 
following an inter-ministerial process, and that the Department of 
Telecommunications may be better placed to address issues of data and 
technology sovereignty through service licence conditions. 
V. COLLABORATIVE MISSIONS: NISAR AND AXIOM-4 
4.38 India has been engaging in significant collaborative missions, 
including NISAR (the NASA-ISRO Synthetic Aperture Radar), which 
marks the first joint Earth observation collaboration between the two 
agencies, and has also participated in international human spaceflight 
missions such as the Axiom-4 mission in 2025, in which Group Captain 
Shubhanshu Shukla became India's first astronaut to travel to the 
International Space Station. India has also demonstrated its value to the 
global community by pioneering cost-effective launches and delivering 
over 400 foreign satellites into orbit. 
4.39 On being asked about the significance of these two missions, and 
the achievements made so far, the Ministry described NISAR, launched 
on 30 July 2025, as the world's first dual-frequency Synthetic Aperture 
Radar mission, carrying an L-band radar from NASA and an S-band radar 
from ISRO on a single platform, delivering high-resolution, all-weather, 
day-and-night imagery of Earth's land and ice surfaces and revisiting 
each location every 12 days. Over its five-year mission life, NISAR is 
expected to provide invaluable data for global climate science, seismic 
and volcanic monitoring, forest mapping and resource management. The 
mission, estimated to cost over USD 1.5 billion, is funded by both 
agencies in respect of their respective contribution, and its open-access 
data policy, delivered in near real time through ISRO's Bhoonidhi portal 
and NASA's ASF DAAC, is intended to empower scientists and 
researchers and to be useful for developing countries, disaster-response 
agencies and climate-change stakeholders worldwide. Repeat-pass 

90 | P a g e 
 
interferometry, one of NISAR's hallmark techniques, enables the study of 
surface deformations to centimetre-level precision, supporting solid-
earth studies of earthquakes, volcanoes and land subsidence, and the 
mission has also generated significant outreach and educational interest, 
inspiring students and young professionals toward careers in space 
science and earth observation. 
4.40 Under the statement of intent signed between ISRO and NASA, 
Gaganyatri Shubhanshu Shukla travelled to the International Space 
Station through the Axiom-4 mission, launched by a Falcon 9 rocket on 
25 June 2025 aboard the SpaceX Dragon spacecraft. He conducted a 
suite of seven microgravity experiments developed by Indian research 
institutions under the coordination of the Human Space Flight Centre, 
exploring muscle regeneration, algal growth, crop viability, microbial 
survivability, cognitive performance in space, and the behaviour of 
cyanobacteria, each aimed at enhancing the understanding of human 
spaceflight and microgravity science. This flight experience is expected 
to be of considerable benefit to India's own Gaganyaan crewed mission, 
with the procedures and technological compliances developed for these 
experiments expected to inform similar work aboard the future Bharatiya 
Antariksh Station. 
4.41 Regarding the question of equitable data sharing, technology 
access and intellectual property frameworks in international space 
missions such as NISAR, the Ministry stated that ISRO collaborates with 
foreign partners to advance its programmatic priorities, improve the 
quality of its products and services, and contribute to global 
policymaking, and that in such partnerships ISRO ensures equitable data 
sharing and access to technology wherever applicable. Intellectual 
property frameworks are duly addressed in the cooperative documents 
signed for such activities, with due vetting by the relevant Ministries, 
and roles and responsibilities are clearly defined to ensure that 
cooperation remains equitable. 
4.42 On the Committee’s query about the diplomatic advantages India 
gains through collaborations such as NISAR, the Ministry of External 
Affairs stated that the mission demonstrates seamless interoperability 

91 | P a g e 
 
between NASA and ISRO systems, setting new standards for 
international mission integration, and that close relations at the agency 
level during the operational phase have ensured strong professional 
engagements. 
VI. NAVIC AND THE SHARING OF SPACE-RELATED DATA AND 
TECHNOLOGY 
4.43 Navigation with Indian Constellation (NavIC), is India's 
independent navigation satellite system, providing positioning, 
navigation and timing (PNT) services over India and up to 1,500 
kilometres beyond the Indian mainland. Three satellites currently 
provide PNT services. 
 
4.44 When the Committee asked about the status of NavIC's 
implementation and the role it plays in India's space diplomacy, the 
Ministry of External Affairs stated that ISRO has implemented both the 
space and ground segments of the system. In the space segment, three 
satellites currently provide PNT services, with further satellites planned 
to complete a seven-satellite constellation, while the ground segment is 
fully functional. India is one of only six countries in the world with the 
capability of an independent navigation system, alongside the United 
States, Russia, China, the European Union and Japan, and carries out 
close coordination with other navigation systems to ensure compatibility 
and interoperability, playing a leading role in international multilateral 
fora pertaining to PNT. NavIC serves as a tool of soft power, showcasing 
India's indigenous capability and achievement in space. Effective 
implementation of the programme requires the establishment of ground 
stations — IRIMS and IGS — in various countries to improve the quality 
of PNT services, and India is approaching countries where such stations 
could be established, including France, Russia, Italy, South Africa, Japan, 
the Republic of Korea, Australia and Nigeria, among others. 
 
4.45 When asked about how India shares its space-related data and 
technology with other countries to foster goodwill and development, the 
Ministry of External Affairs stated that India is actively involved in 

92 | P a g e 
 
sharing both Earth observation and space science and exploration data 
with the global scientific community, and in ensuring access to data from 
other partners. Data from India's RESOURCESAT series of satellites, and 
from its oceanographic and meteorological satellites, is widely used in 
the United States and Europe for various applications, including weather 
forecasting. India also makes data available from its space science and 
planetary exploration satellites through the Indian Space Science Data 
Centre, with portals such as Bhoonidhi, Bhuvan, MOSDAC and NICES 
making Indian data available to the global community, in many cases 
free and open. 
 
VII. SPACE DIPLOMACY AS AN INSTRUMENT OF REGIONAL 
AND GLOBAL SOUTH ENGAGEMENT 
4.46 India has deliberately used its space capability as an instrument of 
regional diplomacy and South-South cooperation. On India's 
technological collaboration with African countries specifically, the 
Ministry stated that India is actively pursuing its Space Vision 2047, 
comprising a series of Chandrayaan and Gaganyaan follow-on missions, 
while also supporting space-aspiring countries, including African 
countries such as South Africa, Nigeria, Kenya, Tanzania, Mauritius and 
Egypt. ISRO is offering support in the use of remote sensing data 
through training and other capacity-building measures, with IN-SPACe 
also pursuing space cooperation with these countries. 
 
4.47 Asked how India compares with China on space collaboration in 
the Global South, and in Africa in particular, the Ministry stated that the 
space activities of China and India are governed by their respective 
programmatic priorities, and that India collaborates with African and 
Global South countries in pursuit of its own programmatic priorities and 
diplomatic objectives. 
 
4.48 On the Committee’s query about India's cost-wise comparison and 
share of the global satellite launching market, the Ministry of External 
Affairs replied that the cost of launching satellites from India is highly 
competitive against the world market, on both dedicated and ride-share 

93 | P a g e 
 
bases, with India's share aimed to increase to about 8% of the global 
commercial space market over the next decade. Of the more than 400 
foreign satellites India has launched, a total of 13 satellites from 
countries of the Global South were launched by NSIL up to December 
2025, with NSIL in discussion with multiple further customers from 
Global South countries for launching their satellites aboard Indian 
launchers; the launching price is highly competitive and in line with the 
global commercial space market. 
 
4.49 On being asked about the challenges faced by countries of the 
Global South in developing their own space programmes, and the ways 
in which India can help, the Ministry identified limited and inconsistent 
funding as a primary constraint, making it difficult to invest in long-
gestation, high-risk space missions or to maintain continuity of 
programmes, alongside a shortage of advanced technical expertise and 
institutional capacity, including skilled human resources in satellite 
manufacturing, propulsion and mission operations. Inadequate 
infrastructure - launch facilities, tracking networks and testing centres - 
further constrains progress, while restricted access to critical 
technologies due to export controls, fragmented policy frameworks and 
weak industry ecosystems limit private sector participation, and the 
absence of reliable downstream applications and data-utilisation 
frameworks reduces the socio-economic value of space investments. 
India, the Ministry assessed, is well placed to support these countries by 
offering a development-oriented and cost-effective model of space 
capability building, providing affordable launch services and end-to-end 
support in small satellite development, mission planning and operations. 
Capacity-building initiatives such as training programmes and 
institutional partnerships can help create a skilled workforce in partner 
countries, while India can also facilitate access to satellite data for 
applications in agriculture, disaster management, climate monitoring and 
resource mapping, demonstrating tangible societal benefit. 
4.50 On the assistance India is presently providing to Global South 
countries in developing their own space programmes, the Ministry stated 
that, through the training programmes conducted at the Centre for 

94 | P a g e 
 
Space Science and Technology Education for Asia and the Pacific 
(CSSTEAP) and the Indian Institute of Remote Sensing (IIRS), more 
than 3,000 officials from over 100 countries have benefited so far . 
ISRO's UNNATI programme has provided hands-on training on nano-
satellite assembly, satellite building and testing to 90 participants from 
45 countries. The India-Bhutan satellite, the South Asia Satellite, and the 
ongoing establishment of a ground station in Vietnam for providing 
remote sensing data to ASEAN countries from Indian remote sensing 
satellites are further examples of Indian assistance to Global South 
countries. 
4.51 As regards the existing collaboration between India and Global 
South countries across navigation, telecommunication, weather 
forecasting and disaster management, the Ministry stated that India has 
developed end-to-end capabilities across these domains, sharing this 
expertise with other countries through bilateral and multilateral 
engagements. As a member of the International Charter on Space and 
Major Disasters and the Asia-Pacific Regional Space Agency Forum, India 
supports global disaster management activities through the rapid 
acquisition and supply of data from its Remote Sensing Satellites, and 
under the Satellite Aided Search and Rescue (COSPAS-SARSAT) 
programme, ISRO has provided operational services to users in India 
and seven neighbouring countries for more than two decades using GEO 
and LEO ground segments. This engagement with the Global South has 
been undertaken specifically through MoUs on space cooperation with 
about 40 countries, capacity building through the UNNATI programme 
with beneficiaries from about 40 countries, other training programmes 
with beneficiaries from about 35 countries, and the launch of satellites, 
with the provision of transponders from the South Asia Satellite to 
India's neighbouring countries a further example of this cooperation. 
 
 
 
 

95 | P a g e 
 
CHAPTER 5 
ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE 
Artificial Intelligence offers transformative opportunities to 
complement human intelligence and enhance how people live and work. 
As explained by the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology 
(MeitY), artificial intelligence refers to computer systems that can 
perform tasks requiring human intelligence, such as learning from data, 
recognising patterns, making decisions and understanding language, and 
includes technologies such as machine learning, deep learning and 
natural language processing. 
5.1 India's momentum in this field is driven by multiple factors: the 
Stanford AI Index 2025 placed India first globally in AI skill penetration; 
India hosts approximately 16% of the world's AI talent and is recognised 
as one of the fastest-growing AI talent hubs, ranking among the top five 
globally; India ranks second globally in contributions to public generative 
AI projects on GitHub; and India hosts a rapidly growing AI start-up 
ecosystem, with an estimated 1,800-plus AI start-ups, of which over 890 
are in generative AI specifically. 
I. INDIA'S AI ECOSYSTEM AND INFRASTRUCTURE 
5.2 MeitY informed the Committee that AI is being actively deployed 
across sectors in India. 
 Healthcare: AI-based diagnostics (e.g., TB and cancer screening), 
telemedicine platforms like eSanjeevani  
 Agriculture: Crop advisory, weather prediction, and precision 
farming under Digital Agriculture Mission  
 Governance: AI in platforms like Aadhaar, DigiLocker, and GST 
analytics for improved service delivery  
 Finance: Fraud detection, credit scoring, and financial inclusion 
through fintech solutions  
 Education: Adaptive learning platforms and AI-enabled content 
under DIKSHA  

96 | P a g e 
 
 Smart Mobility: Traffic management and logistics optimisation  
Under the IndiaAI Mission, the Government is enabling access to 
compute infrastructure, datasets (AIKosh), and innovation support to 
scale such applications. 
5.3 On being asked about the impact of AI on India’s economic 
growth, the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology stated 
that AI is a key driver of India’s digital economy, with strong potential to 
enhance productivity, innovation, and service delivery. 
 AI is projected to contribute USD 500-600 billion to GDP by 2030, 
with long-term potential of up to USD 1.7 trillion by 2035, driven 
by adoption across sectors such as manufacturing, agriculture, 
healthcare, BFSI, and retail.  
 The IndiaAI Mission (₹10,300+ crore) is strengthening the 
ecosystem through investments in compute infrastructure, 
datasets, innovation, skilling, and startup support.  
 The AIKosh (IndiaAI Datasets Platform) is enabling access to high-
quality datasets, reducing barriers to AI innovation for startups 
and researchers.  
 The IndiaAI Compute pillar is building national AI infrastructure, 
including large-scale GPU capacity, to support development of 
indigenous AI solutions.  
 Initiatives such as Bhashini and BharatGen are expanding AI 
adoption in Indian languages and public services, improving 
inclusion and scalability.  
 AI is enhancing Digital Public Infrastructure and governance 
systems, improving efficiency, targeting of services, and citizen 
engagement at scale.  
 AI is also contributing to job creation, innovation, and expansion of 
the digital economy, supported by India’s strong talent base. 
 

97 | P a g e 
 
5.4 Elaborating the importance of AI for developing counties like India 
and how it can be leveraged as a strategic tool to promote inclusive 
growth and expand access to opportunities, MeitY stated that AI is a 
critical enabler for developing countries like India, with the potential to 
accelerate inclusive growth, improve service delivery, and expand access 
to opportunities at scale, particularly when combined with digital public 
infrastructure. 
 AI is estimated to contribute around USD 500-600 billion to India’s 
GDP by 2030, driven by productivity gains and increased adoption 
across key sector.  
 AI enables inclusion at population scale by improving access to 
services through Digital Public Infrastructure, including applications 
in healthcare, financial services, and governance.  
 In healthcare, AI is being leveraged for early disease detection, 
diagnostics, and telemedicine, including initiatives such as AI-
based cancer detection programmes in collaboration with National 
Cancer Grid.  
 In agriculture, AI supports crop monitoring, weather forecasting, 
and advisory services, helping improve productivity and resilience 
for small and marginal farmers.  
 AI is enabling multilingual access to digital services, including 
through platforms such as Bhashini, thereby bridging language 
barriers and improving access to government and private services.  
 AI-driven solutions are improving efficiency and targeting in 
governance, including in areas such as beneficiary identification, 
service delivery, and grievance redressal.  
 AI is supporting skill development and employment generation, 
including through platforms such as IndiaAI FutureSkills, YUVAi, 
and Skill India Digital Hub, expanding access to digital and AI-
related jobs.  

98 | P a g e 
 
 AI is also enabling innovation and entrepreneurship, with growing 
participation of startups and MSMEs in developing solutions 
tailored to local challenges.  
 Overall, AI serves as a strategic tool for inclusive and sustainable 
development, allowing countries like India to address structural 
challenges, improve productivity, and ensure equitable access to 
opportunities. 
5.5 As regards the measures being taken by the Government to 
harness the transformative potential of AI and in which key sectors can 
AI significantly improve the lives of citizens, MeitY stated that the 
Government is implementing a comprehensive set of measures to 
harness the transformative potential of AI, while enabling its application 
across key sectors to improve citizens’ lives through inclusive, scalable, 
and technology-driven solutions. 
 The IndiaAI Mission (₹10,300+ crore) provides a structured 
framework across compute, datasets (AIKosh), applications, 
skilling, and innovation, enabling development and deployment of 
AI solutions at scale.  
 Under the Application Development Initiative (IADI), AI solutions 
are being developed based on problem statements from ministries 
and departments, with 30 applications shortlisted across sectors 
such as healthcare, agriculture, governance, and climate.  
 The Government is enabling access to datasets and AI models 
through the AIKosh platform, supporting development of sector-
specific solutions.  
 Citizen engagement and participatory governance are being 
promoted through platforms such as MyGov, enabling feedback 
and crowdsourcing of ideas, and the IndiaAI Portal, which provides 
a central repository of AI initiatives and resources.  
 The overall approach focuses on public-private partnerships, open 
platforms, and scalable infrastructure, ensuring wider participation 
and innovation in the AI ecosystem.  

99 | P a g e 
 
 AI is being leveraged in healthcare for early disease detection, 
diagnostics, and telemedicine, including initiatives in collaboration 
with the National Cancer Grid.  
 In agriculture, AI is supporting crop monitoring, weather 
forecasting, and advisory services, improving productivity and 
resilience for farmers.  
 In governance, AI is enhancing service delivery, beneficiary 
targeting, and grievance redressal, leveraging Digital Public 
Infrastructure.  
 In education and skilling, AI is enabling personalised learning and 
digital training platforms, expanding access to quality education.  
 In language and inclusion, platforms such as Bhashini are enabling 
multilingual access to digital services, improving accessibility for 
diverse populations.  
 In sectors such as climate change and disaster management, AI is 
supporting early warning systems, risk assessment, and resource 
optimisation. 
5.6 When asked about the key requirements for building a robust AI 
ecosystem, the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology 
stated that a robust AI ecosystem requires a combination of compute 
infrastructure (GPUs, cloud capacity), high-quality and diverse datasets, 
skilled talent base, research and innovation ecosystem, policy and 
governance frameworks and adoption across sectors.  
 
Current status of India’s AI ecosystem  
5.7 Providing an assessment of the current status of India’s AI 
ecosystem, the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology 
stated as under: 
 India is among the top global AI ecosystems, ranked 3rd globally 
in AI competitiveness.  

100 | P a g e 
 
 The country has a large and growing talent base, with AI skill 
penetration estimated to be 2.5 times the global average.  
 Enterprise adoption is high, with approx. 87% of enterprises 
actively using AI solutions, and key sectors such as BFSI, 
manufacturing, retail, and healthcare driving adoption.  
 India has a strong digital foundation, with Digital Public 
Infrastructure and large-scale datasets enabling AI deployment at 
population scale.  
 The ecosystem includes approx. 6 million professionals in the tech 
and AI workforce, supporting growth across sectors.  
 At the same time, challenges remain in advanced compute 
capacity, frontier research, and private investment, as well as in 
scaling adoption across industry. 
5.8 The measures being undertaken by the Government to strengthen 
India’s AI ecosystem are as follows:  
 The IndiaAI Mission (₹10,300 crore) serves as the central 
programme covering compute infrastructure, datasets (AIKosh), 
innovation, skilling, startup financing, applications, and safe & 
trusted AI.  
 National AI compute capacity is being expanded through 
onboarding of large-scale GPU infrastructure, with affordable 
access enabled for startups, academia, and government entities.  
 The AIKosh - National Datasets Platform provides access to a 
repository of high-quality datasets and AI models, supporting 
research, innovation, and development of AI solutions.  
 The Application Development Initiative (IADI) is supporting 
deployment of AI applications across priority sectors, enabling 
real-world use cases and scaling adoption.  
 Centres of Excellence for AI (CoE-AI) have been established in 
sectors such as healthcare, agriculture, sustainable cities, and 
education to drive applied research and innovation.  

101 | P a g e 
 
 The Government is supporting the startup and innovation 
ecosystem through financing, innovation challenges, and public-
private partnerships, enabling participation across industry and 
academia.  
 Efforts are underway to promote indigenous AI models and 
language technologies, including Vikram LLMs, BharatGen, and 
Bhashini, strengthening domestic capabilities and inclusion. 
 Skilling and capacity-building initiatives, including IndiaAI 
FutureSkills and Data & AI Labs, are being implemented to build a 
strong AI talent pipeline.  
 Responsible AI frameworks and institutional mechanisms, including 
the IndiaAI Safety Institute, are being developed to ensure safe, 
trusted, and ethical AI deployment.  
 The India AI Impact Summit 2026 was hosted as a global platform 
with 100+ countries and 90+ endorsements, leading to shared 
principles, working group deliverables, and strengthened global 
partnerships for inclusive and responsible AI. 
5.9 The IndiaAI Mission, approved by the Cabinet with an outlay of 
over ₹10,300 crore over five years, is the Government's central 
programme for building a robust AI ecosystem. Its funding approach is 
pillar-based and phased, addressing gaps in compute, data access, 
research and human capital while promoting indigenous AI capability. 
Asked about the present status of fund utilisation and how this allocation 
has contributed to strengthening the ecosystem, MeitY furnished the 
following account. 
● Compute: 38,231 GPUs have been onboarded from 14 empanelled 
service providers and data centres under the AI compute capacity 
framework, at subsidised rates of approximately ₹65 per GPU-hour, 
against a global market rate the Ministry elsewhere placed at two-
and-a-half to three US dollars, with a further 24,000 GPUs 
expected to come onto the pipeline shortly. 

102 | P a g e 
 
● Datasets: the AIKosh national datasets platform offers, on the 
most recent written figures, more than 10,200 datasets and 290-
plus AI models. 
● Applications: 30 AI applications have been supported under the 
Application Development Initiative across priority sectors. 
● Skilling: over 13,500 scholars are being supported under IndiaAI 
FutureSkills across undergraduate, postgraduate and PhD levels, 
with a network of 570 Data and AI Labs planned, of which 27 to 
30-plus are already operational, concentrated in Tier-2 and Tier-3 
cities. 
● Indigenous AI models: India has launched sovereign foundation 
models, including Sarvam AI's (30B and 105B large language 
models), BharatGen's Param2 (a 17B parameter multilingual model 
developed through an IIT Bombay-led academic consortium), and 
Gnani.ai's Vachana (a voice/speech AI model), each showcased at 
the AI Impact Summit 2026. Sarvam also unveiled Vikram, a 
multilingual chatbot assistant at the Summit. Twelve organisations 
and consortia have been selected for foundation model 
development, with the Government providing financial support for 
compute costs plus an additional 25% support for datasets and 
personnel. 
● Centres of Excellence for AI have been established in healthcare, 
agriculture and sustainable cities, with a further Centre recently 
announced in education. 
5.10 On India's domestic regulatory posture specifically, representative 
of MeitY deposed before the Committee on 2 February 2026 and stated 
as under: 
“In the space of regulation, our objective situation is such that 
today we are a little different from the situation that obtains say 
the European Union or in the United States. We have existing laws 
which allow us some latitude of how to regulate artificial 
intelligence both under the IT Act and under the IT Rules and also 
under the BNS. This is something that the group which MEITY had 

103 | P a g e 
 
set up under the Principal Scientific Advisor to frame guidelines on 
how AI governance should go, they have also indicated that in that 
framework there are a fair amount of substance in the kind of laws 
that we already have and we need to use them to the extent 
possible before we decide to legislate further and therefore, we 
believe that because we have also passed the Digital Personal 
Data Protection Act and the Rules under that and that Act is going 
to be under implementation soon, we will have a fair bit of control 
over many of the potentially harmful areas of AI.” 
5.11 At a later sitting on 30 April 2026, the Committee heard views of 
experts and during that sitting an expert witness made the following 
observation regarding the continuing relevance of India's national AI 
strategy: 
“The strategy should be refined. NITI Aayog has a national 
strategy for AI, but that was done in the pre-LLM days. We have to 
go back and look at this, and not push to invest in getting the next 
greatest LLMs out there — we are really not in a great position to 
compete with the US and China in terms of pushing the frontier. 
We should compete with Singapore instead. Singapore has an 
amazing model called SEA-LION, Southeast Asian Languages in 
One Network. Even though it is not better than, say, Google's 
Gemini, it is still competitive for most of the tasks they want to do 
in public service, and many Southeast Asian countries have 
adopted SEA-LION as their official language model.” 
 
Data Centres and Compute Capacity  
5.12 The growth of AI is expected to significantly increase the demand 
for advanced data centre infrastructure in India, as AI workloads require 
specialised, high-capacity systems beyond conventional data centres. 
 AI systems require high-density compute infrastructure, including 
advanced GPU clusters, along with high-speed storage, low-latency 
networks, and scalable architecture to support training and 
deployment of large models.  

104 | P a g e 
 
 Industry estimates indicate that India’s data centre capacity is 
expected to grow from around 1.5 GW in 2025 to approx. 8 GW by 
2030, driven by increasing demand from AI, cloud computing, and 
digital services.  
 There is a need for advanced cooling solutions, including liquid 
cooling and improved thermal management systems, to support 
energy-intensive AI workloads.  
 Future infrastructure must also focus on geographic diversification, 
including expansion into Tier-2 and Tier-3 cities, to reduce latency 
and improve access. 
5.13 On being asked about the present status of AI ready 
infrastructure, in India as compared to countries such as the United 
States and China, MeitY replied that currently 38,231 GPU have been 
onboarded from 14 empanelled service providers/data centres under the 
AI compute capacity framework. Data centre capacity in the country has 
grown from 520 MW in 2020 to more than 1500 MW till 2025. India has 
made steady progress in building AI-ready infrastructure; however, 
compared to leading economies, there remains a gap in compute 
capacity, data centres, and advanced infrastructure. 
 India currently generates a large share of global data (approx. 
20%), but its share of global data centre capacity remains 
relatively low, indicating a gap between data generation and 
infrastructure.  
 As per official data from the Press Information Bureau, India’s data 
centre capacity has increased from approx. 375 MW in 2020 to 
approx.1,500 MW (1.5 GW) by 2025.  
 India currently has around 1.5 GW installed data centre capacity 
(2025), with approximately 270+ data centres operational, 
reflecting rapid but still evolving infrastructure.  
 In comparison, global data centre capacity is projected to reach 
approx. 200 GW by 2030, indicating that AI infrastructure remains 
highly concentrated in a few advanced economies.  

105 | P a g e 
 
 India’s AI-ready compute infrastructure is expanding, with over 
38,000 GPUs onboarded under the national AI compute 
framework, made available at subsidised rates to startups and 
researchers.  
 Several studies and policy assessments have identified the 
infrastructure required for India to assume a leadership role in AI: 
o Scaling up high-performance compute infrastructure, 
including large GPU clusters and cloud capacity, is critical, as 
AI workloads require significantly higher compute intensity 
than traditional IT systems. 
o Expansion of data centre capacity from the current ~1.5 GW 
to multi-gigawatt scale (4.5–6.5 GW by 2030) has been 
identified as necessary to meet AI and cloud demand. 
o Adoption of advanced cooling technologies and efficient 
resource management systems, particularly for AI-grade 
infrastructure 
o Development of robust digital connectivity, including fibre 
networks and submarine cable systems, to enable high-
speed and low-latency data transfer. 
5.14 In response, the Government has initiated steps under the IndiaAI 
Mission, including expansion of compute infrastructure, support for data 
centre growth, and promotion of indigenous AI capabilities, to 
strengthen India’s position in the global AI ecosystem. 
 
5.15 On India's distinct approach to providing AI infrastructure, as 
compared with other countries, the Ministry of Electronics and 
Information Technology stated as under: 
“Fundamentally, our approach to the way that AI infrastructure is 
provided is completely different from the way the US or any other 
country provides it. In fact, the United Nations, the World Bank, 
and the Gates Foundation recognised our model in the AI Mission, 
of making sure that the private sector builds out the AI 
infrastructure, the data centres and the GPUs, and the 

106 | P a g e 
 
Government then contracts with them to provide the GPU as a 
service to innovators, researchers, students and others, subsidising 
that access. What it has effectively done is provide access to AI 
resources at about one-third the cost of the rest of the world — 
about ₹65 per GPU-hour, compared to a global rate of about two-
and-a-half to three dollars. We are not necessarily trying to 
dominate the space of infrastructure, nor the space of building 
models, both of which are much more expensive propositions; 
building applications is comparatively cheaper, and that is 
fundamentally our focus.” 
5.16 India is hosting nearly 20 % of the world’s data, however accounts 
for only 3 % of the global data centre capacity. In this context, the 
Committee enquired about the present status of data generated and 
existing data centre capacity. To this, the MeitY replied that India is 
among the fastest-growing data generating nations and contributes a 
significant share of global data traffic however , the specific figure of 
20% is not supported by standard global datasets and varies across 
studies. The observation that India’s installed data centre capacity is 
relatively lower than its data generation potential is broadly valid, 
though the 3% figure may be treated as indicative rather than precise as 
the Data Centre industry in India is showing consistent growth for the 
last few years. Data Centre industry in India is growing at a steady pace. 
Officially no data is maintained in MeitY regarding capacity of Data 
Centres. However as per industry estimates, Data centre capacity in the 
country has grown from 375 MW in 2020 to more than 1500 MW till 
2025. 
5.17 India hosts a significant share of global data while its data centre 
capacity remains comparatively lower, indicating a gap between data 
generation and domestic infrastructure capacity. 
 India generates and hosts nearly 20% of the world’s data, while its 
share of global data centre capacity is about 3%, reflecting an 
emerging infrastructure gap.  

107 | P a g e 
 
 As of 2025–2026, India’s data centre capacity is estimated at 
around 1.5–2 GW, with approximately 270+ data centres 
operational across the country.  
 Data centre capacity has grown rapidly, increasing from around 
520 MW in 2020 to 1.5 GW in 2025, and is projected to reach ~2 
GW by 2026 and over 8 GW by 2030.  
 Major data centre hubs include Mumbai, Bengaluru, Hyderabad, 
Chennai, and Delhi NCR, driven by connectivity, demand, and 
policy support.  
 The existing gap results in a portion of India’s data being stored or 
processed outside the country, raising concerns related to latency, 
cost, and data sovereignty.  
5.18 Regarding the steps being taken to address this gap, the 
Government is undertaking the following measures: 
 Under the IndiaAI Mission, efforts are underway to build AI-ready 
compute infrastructure, including onboarding of 38,000+ GPUs at 
subsidised rates, to support domestic AI development. 
 The Government is facilitating large-scale investments in data 
centre infrastructure, with major commitments including: 
○ Amazon Web Services (USD 8.3 billion) for expansion of 
cloud and data centre capacity in India  
○ Ongoing investments by global hyperscale’s and domestic 
players across key hubs such as Mumbai, Hyderabad, and 
Chennai 
● Data centres have been accorded infrastructure status, enabling 
access to long-term financing, lower cost of capital, and 
institutional funding, thereby supporting capacity expansion.  
● Several states, including Maharashtra, Tamil Nadu, Telangana, and 
Uttar Pradesh, have notified dedicated data centre policies, 
offering incentives such as land support, power tariff concessions, 
and faster approvals to attract investment. 

108 | P a g e 
 
● The Government is supporting reliable and sustainable power 
supply, including promotion of renewable energy integration, given 
that data centres are energy-intensive infrastructure. 
● As a result of these measures, India’s data centre capacity is 
projected to increase from around 1.5 GW in 2025 to ~2 GW by 
2026, with long-term projections indicating growth to around 8–10 
GW by 20301 
5.19 When asked about the future requirements for data centres for AI 
in India, MeitY stated that as per industry estimates, Data Centre 
industry in India is growing at a steady pace. Data centre capacity in the 
country has grown from 375 MW in 2020 to more than 1500 MW till 
2025. As per industry estimates, this capacity may surpass 5 GW till 
2030. The data centres are spread across regions of the country and are 
built to cater to multiple types of workloads including AI workloads with 
Central Processing Unit (CPU) as well as Graphics Processing Unit (GPU) 
compute. For meeting the demand of Artificial Intelligence 38,231 GPUs 
have been onboarded from 14 empanelled service providers/data 
centres under the AI compute capacity framework. 
5.20 The Government has taken various initiatives for facilitating 
investment in this industry. A few of the major initiatives are: 
 Government shall be providing a long-term tax holiday to foreign 
cloud service provider on procurement of Data Centre services 
from Indian Data Centres. This initiative is aimed at positioning 
India as a global digital infrastructure hub and is a major step in 
attracting investment in Data Centre industry provider on data 
centre and cloud infrastructure investment. 
 For facilitating ease of finance, Data Centres were included in the 
Harmonized list of Infrastructure Sub-Sectors (HML) qualifying 
them for the infrastructure status at par with other infrastructure 
projects. 
 
 
1 https://www.financialexpress.com/business/news/indias-data-centre-power-demand-to-jump-6x-by-2030-
