109 | P a g e 
 
5.21 AI data centres significantly increase energy demand. In the event 
of expanded AI infrastructure development, the Committee wanted to 
know about the projected increase in power requirements, and how the 
Government plans to address sustainability concerns. In a written reply, 
the Ministry of Electronics and Information and Technology submitted 
that as per the information available with Ministry of Power, Government 
of India, the electricity demand due to Data Centres is estimated to be 
13.56 GW by 2031-32. The capacity of National Grid is being expanded 
on a continuous basis commensurate with the growth in electricity 
generation and electricity demand. India’s national transmission 
infrastructure is adequately prepared to ensure a reliable power flow 
across regions. 
5.22 The rapid expansion of AI and data centre infrastructure is 
expected to lead to a substantial increase in energy demand, given the 
high compute intensity of AI workloads. 
 India’s data centre capacity is projected to increase from around 
1.5 GW in 2025 to approximately 8–10 GW by 2030, indicating a 
nearly 5–6 times increase in power capacity requirements.  
 Electricity consumption from data centres is expected to rise from 
about 10–15 TWh in 2024 to ~40–57 TWh by 2030, reflecting a 
sharp increase driven by AI workloads.  
 Data centres currently account for around 0.5% of India’s 
electricity consumption, which is projected to increase to ~2.5–3% 
by 2030, highlighting the growing pressure on power systems.  
 AI workloads are significantly more energy-intensive, with 
estimates suggesting that AI-related applications could account for 
nearly 20% of incremental electricity demand growth in India by 
2030.  
5.23 To address these sustainability and energy challenges, the 
Government is undertaking the following measures: 
 Under the IndiaAI Mission, large-scale AI compute infrastructure is 
being developed through a shared model. More than 38,000 GPUs 

110 | P a g e 
 
have been onboarded and made available at subsidised rates 
(around ₹65 per GPU-hour), enabling efficient utilisation of 
compute resources and reducing duplication of infrastructure. 
 This shared compute approach helps optimise resource utilisation 
and improve energy efficiency, as multiple users access common 
infrastructure instead of building separate, energy-intensive 
systems. 
 The Government is promoting renewable energy integration, with 
India adding a record 44.5 GW of renewable capacity in 2025, 
supporting cleaner power supply for data centres. 
 The Government is encouraging development of data centres in 
regions with access to renewable energy and sustainable 
infrastructure, to reduce environmental impact. 
Investment for development of AI 
5.24 Global companies such as Microsoft have pledged investment of 
17.5 billion US dollars in India to help build infrastructure, skills, and 
sovereign capabilities for AI future in India. On the total AI-related 
investment committed by domestic and foreign entities so far, MeitY 
stated that global technology companies have announced significant 
investments in India’s AI ecosystem, including infrastructure, cloud 
capacity, and skilling initiatives. For instance, Microsoft has announced 
an investment of USD 17.5 billion over the coming years towards AI 
infrastructure, cloud, and skilling in India.  In addition, multiple global 
and domestic players have made substantial investments in data 
centres, cloud infrastructure, and AI innovation, reflecting growing 
confidence in India’s digital and AI ecosystem. The Government, through 
initiatives such as the IndiaAI Mission with an outlay of ₹10,000+ crore, 
is supporting development of compute infrastructure, datasets, and AI 
innovation, thereby catalysing further private sector investments. 
5.25 The highest levels of investment are being directed towards AI 
infrastructure and data centres, including compute capacity and cloud 
ecosystems.  

111 | P a g e 
 
 Significant investments are also being made in cloud computing 
and digital infrastructure, which form the backbone for AI 
development and deployment.  
 AI skilling and workforce development is a key focus area, with 
large-scale programmes aimed at building AI capabilities across 
the workforce.  
 Investments are also increasing in AI applications across sectors, 
including healthcare, agriculture, governance, and financial 
services.  
Growing investments are being seen in AI startups and innovation 
ecosystems, particularly in areas such as foundation models and 
generative AI (IndiaAI Foundation Models). 
 
Data Protection and security 
5.26 On the issues related to data protection and security, MeitY stated 
that the increasing adoption of AI has raised important concerns related 
to data safety, privacy, and security, given the large-scale use of data 
and automated decision-making systems. 
 AI systems rely on large volumes of data, including personal and 
sensitive data, increasing risks of data breaches, unauthorised 
access, and misuse.  
 There are concerns related to data privacy, particularly in terms of 
collection, storage, consent, and profiling of individuals, as well as 
risks of surveillance.  
 AI models are vulnerable to security threats, including data 
poisoning, adversarial attacks, and model manipulation, which can 
affect system reliability and outcomes.  
 The use of AI may lead to bias and discrimination, especially 
where training data is incomplete or unrepresentative.  

112 | P a g e 
 
 The rapid growth of generative AI has increased risks related to 
deepfakes, misinformation, and content authenticity, affecting 
public trust.  
 Cross-border deployment of AI systems raises challenges related 
to data governance, jurisdiction, and regulatory enforcement. 
 There is a need for robust safeguards, including privacy-preserving 
technologies, secure data architectures, and strong cybersecurity 
measures, to ensure safe deployment of AI systems.  
 Development of standards for data governance, model evaluation, 
transparency, and accountability is essential to build trust and 
ensure responsible use of AI. 
5.27 Further, the Government is addressing these issues through AI 
governance frameworks and institutional mechanisms, including the 
India AI Governance Guidelines (2025) and the IndiaAI Safety Institute, 
which focus on safe, secure, and trustworthy AI.  The Safe & Trusted AI 
pillar under the IndiaAI Mission is addressing these concerns through 
concrete measures: 
 The Government has supported 13+ AI safety and governance 
projects, covering areas such as bias mitigation, explainable AI, 
synthetic data generation, and privacy-preserving machine 
learning. 
 Development of deepfake detection tools by leading institutions 
(including IITs) to address risks related to misinformation and 
content authenticity. 
 Creation of AI auditing and certification frameworks to assess 
fairness, transparency, and accountability of AI systems. 
 The IndiaAI Safety Institute is being established, with 75+ 
applications received under the Expression of Interest process for 
onboarding partner institutions, to advance research on AI safety, 
model evaluation, and risk testing. 
5.28 The overall approach focuses on building end-to-end safeguards, 
including secure data architectures, model evaluation, risk testing, and 

113 | P a g e 
 
regulatory standards, to ensure protection of data and trust in AI 
systems. 
 
Research and Innovation Ecosystem 
5.29 Regarding the current status of collaboration with various 
academic institutions industries, research organisations and industry for 
the promotion of AI-driven innovation and research, the Ministry of 
Electronics and Information Technology informed that the Government is 
fostering collaboration with academic institutions, industry, research 
organisations, and startups through the IndiaAI Mission, which adopts a 
multi-stakeholder approach. This includes engagement in foundation 
model development (500+ proposals received), joint evaluation 
mechanisms under application initiatives, partnerships with global 
technology companies, development of the AIKosh datasets platform, 
and establishment of the IndiaAI Safety Institute through a hub-and-
spoke model. 
Academic Collaboration 
 Collaboration with academia is being strengthened through IndiaAI 
FutureSkills, including fellowships, PhD programmes, and Data & 
AI Labs across institutions.  
 Programmes such as FutureSkills PRIME are enabling reskilling and 
upskilling in emerging technologies including AI for the workforce 
and academia.  
 The Visvesvaraya PhD Scheme is supporting large-scale doctoral 
and post-doctoral research in AI and emerging technologies.  
 Initiatives such as YUVAi (Youth for Unnati and Vikas with AI) and 
Responsible AI for Youth are promoting early-stage engagement 
with students and building a future talent pipeline.  
 Data and AI Labs are being established in collaboration with 
NIELIT and industry partners, including in Tier-2 and Tier-3 cities, 
to expand access to AI training and research infrastructure.  

114 | P a g e 
 
 Under the Safe & Trusted AI pillar, 13 projects are being 
implemented by academic institutions and research organisations 
(including IITs, IIITs, and NITs) across areas such as bias 
mitigation, explainable AI, synthetic data, and AI safety tools. 
Industry and Research Collaboration 
 The Government has partnered with global technology companies 
such as IBM, Meta, and Microsoft to support AI innovation, skilling, 
and ecosystem development.  
 A MoU with Microsoft (2025) aims to train 5 lakh individuals by 
2026, including students, developers, and government officials, 
and establish AI Productivity Labs across 10 states.  
 Under the IndiaAI Startup Financing pillar, collaboration with 
Station F (France) and HEC Paris is supporting 10 Indian AI 
startups through a global acceleration programme.  
 The IndiaAI Foundation Models initiative has received 500+ 
proposals, with multiple startups and academic consortia 
(including IITs and private firms) shortlisted for development of 
indigenous AI models.  
 Under the Application Development Initiative, 30 AI applications 
have been shortlisted for further development. In addition, 
multiple innovation challenges and hackathons have been 
conducted in partnership with organisations such as I4C, 
Geological Survey of India (GSI), and National Cancer Grid (NCG), 
receiving over 250 submissions in cybersecurity, over 100 
submissions in mineral targeting, and over 280 applications under 
healthcare-focused AI programmes.  
5.30 The Committee were keen to learn about the role that has been 
envisaged for Universities and Industries for promotion of innovation and 
Research in AI in the country. The Ministry of Electronics and 
Information Technology in a written reply stated that universities and 
industries have been envisaged as core partners in advancing AI 
research, innovation, and deployment, with clearly defined roles 
supported through national programmes.  

115 | P a g e 
 
Role of Universities 
 Academic institutions are leading AI research and model 
development, including participation in the IndiaAI Foundation 
Models programme, which has received 500+ proposals, with 
multiple IITs and academic consortia involved.  
 Through IndiaAI FutureSkills, universities are supporting 13,000+ 
students across UG, PG and PhD levels.  
 Under the Visvesvaraya PhD Scheme, academic institutions are 
supporting advanced research in AI and emerging technologies.  
 Academic institutions are implementing 13+ Responsible AI 
projects across areas such as bias mitigation, explainable AI, 
synthetic data, and privacy-enhancing technologies.  
 Data and AI Labs are being established across institutions, with 
27+ labs operational and expansion across ITIs and polytechnics 
underway.  
Role of Industry 
 Industry is driving application development and deployment, 
including participation in the IndiaAI Application Development 
Initiative, with 30 AI applications shortlisted.  
 Industry is actively participating in innovation programmes, 
including:  
o Cybercrime AI Hackathon (I4C): 263 submissions  
o Mineral Targeting Challenge (GSI): 102 submissions  
o Cancer AI Challenge (NCG): 285 applications  
 Under the IndiaAI Startup Financing pillar, collaboration with 
Station F and HEC Paris is supporting 10 Indian AI startups for 
global expansion.  
 Industry partnerships with companies such as Microsoft, Meta, 
IBM, and Intel are supporting skilling and innovation, including 
programmes targeting large-scale workforce training.  

116 | P a g e 
 
 Industry is contributing to compute infrastructure, including 
onboarding of 38,000+ GPUs under the IndiaAI Mission. 
5.31 When asked about the way in which the Government is creating an 
enabling environment for effective private sector, MSMEs participation in 
the AI ecosystem in the country, MeitY stated that the Government is 
creating an enabling environment for private sector and MSME 
participation through targeted interventions under the IndiaAI Mission, 
focusing on access to infrastructure, data, financing, and innovation 
platforms. 
 The IndiaAI Mission (₹10,300 crore) provides a comprehensive 
framework covering compute, datasets (AIKosh), innovation, 
startup financing, applications, and skilling, with active 
participation from startups and MSMEs.  
 Under the IndiaAI Compute pillar, over 38,000 GPUs have been 
onboarded and made available at subsidised rates (~₹65 per GPU-
hour), enabling affordable access to high-performance computing 
for startups and MSMEs.  
 The AIKosh – National Datasets Platform provides access to 
10,200+ datasets and 292 AI models, reducing entry barriers and 
supporting development of AI solutions.  
 The IndiaAI Startup Financing pillar supports startups across the 
lifecycle, including internationalisation through partnerships such 
as Station F and HEC Paris, benefiting 10 Indian AI startups.  
 Under the Application Development Initiative, 30 AI applications 
have been shortlisted, enabling startups and MSMEs to participate 
in development of real-world AI use cases.  
 Multiple innovation challenges and hackathons have been 
conducted in partnership with organisations such as I4C, 
Geological Survey of India, and National Cancer Grid, receiving 
263, 102, and 285 submissions respectively, facilitating 
participation of startups and MSMEs.  

117 | P a g e 
 
 The Government is promoting public-private partnerships (PPPs) 
for development of compute infrastructure, datasets, and AI 
solutions, ensuring private sector participation across the value 
chain. 
5.32 On the measures put in place to streamline access to funding, 
compute resources and datasets for AI start-ups specifically, MeitY 
stated that the Government has put in place targeted mechanisms to 
reduce entry barriers, including the Antariksh-style dedicated funds for 
the AI sector, subsidised compute access, and open datasets through 
AIKosh. 
AI start ups 
5.33 The Committee further enquired about the major challenges faced 
by AI start-ups in developing, scaling, and commercialising their 
products. MeitY responded that AI start-ups in India face several 
challenges across the lifecycle of development, scaling, and 
commercialisation, particularly in areas of infrastructure, data, financing, 
and market access. 
 The high cost of compute infrastructure, including GPUs and cloud 
resources, limits the ability of startups to undertake large-scale 
model development and experimentation.  
 The limited availability of high-quality, domain-specific datasets 
increases the time and cost required for developing robust AI 
solutions.  
 There are funding gaps in growth and deep-tech stages, as AI 
startups require longer gestation periods and higher capital 
investment.  
 Startups face challenges in commercialisation, including limited 
access to pilot opportunities, procurement systems, and large-
scale deployment platforms.  
 There is a shortage of specialised AI talent, particularly in 
advanced areas such as foundation models and AI safety.  

118 | P a g e 
 
 Weak research-to-industry linkages affect the translation of 
innovations into scalable and market-ready products.  
 Data governance and compliance requirements increase 
operational complexity and costs for startups.  
 Indian startups face strong global competition, with relatively 
limited access to capital, compute resources, and large-scale 
datasets.  
 
II. DEVELOPMENT OF INDIA SPECIFIC LARGE LANGUAGE 
MODEL 
5.34 Developing domestic AI capabilities could help strengthen India’s 
technological sovereignty and reduce reliance on foreign models. About 
the potential benefits and strategic opportunities associated with 
developing India-specific Large Language Models tailored to Indian 
languages, datasets, and socio-economic contexts, MeitY stated that 
developing India-specific Large Language Models (LLMs) presents 
significant strategic, economic, and technological opportunities, 
particularly in strengthening domestic capabilities and enabling inclusive 
AI deployment. 
 Development of indigenous LLMs enhances technological 
sovereignty, reducing dependence on foreign AI systems and 
ensuring greater control over critical digital infrastructure and 
data.  
 India-specific LLMs are trained on Indian datasets and multilingual 
corpora, enabling better understanding of 22+ Indian languages, 
dialects, and code-mixed usage, which global models often fail to 
capture.  
 This enables inclusive access to digital services, particularly for 
non-English users, supporting applications in governance, financial 
inclusion, education, and healthcare.  

119 | P a g e 
 
 Indigenous models are better suited to address India’s socio-
economic and sectoral challenges, including use cases in 
agriculture, climate, public service delivery, and rural development.  
 Development of domestic LLMs strengthens the innovation 
ecosystem, with over 500 proposals received and multiple startups 
and research institutions selected under the IndiaAI Mission to 
build foundation models.  
 India has already launched sovereign AI models such as Sarvam 
AI’s LLMs (30B and 105B parameters), BharatGen Param2 
(multilingual model), and Gnani.ai’s Vachana (speech AI), 
demonstrating growing domestic capabilities.  
 These models enable development of cost-efficient and scalable AI 
solutions, tailored for India’s large population and diverse use 
cases, compared to high-cost global models.  
 Domestic LLMs support creation of new industries and jobs, 
including in areas such as model development, data annotation, AI 
deployment, and sector-specific AI services.  
 Strategic development of LLMs also enables India to play a 
stronger role in global AI governance, contributing perspectives 
from the Global South and promoting inclusive AI frameworks.  
5.35 Explaining the strategic importance of having indigenous Large 
Multimodal Models (LMMs) for India, MeitY stated that Indigenous Large 
Multimodal Models (LMMs), which can work with text, images, audio, 
and video together, are important for India as they help build advanced 
AI systems suited to Indian needs and real-world use cases. 
 LMMs can process multiple types of data at once, which makes 
them useful for real-life applications such as healthcare, 
agriculture, education, and governance.  
 Developing these models in India helps improve technological self-
reliance, reducing dependence on foreign AI systems and ensuring 
better control over data and digital infrastructure.  

120 | P a g e 
 
 India-specific LMMs can be trained on Indian datasets and 
multilingual data, helping them understand 22+ Indian languages 
and diverse local contexts, which global models often struggle 
with.  
 These models support large-scale public services, such as:  
o voice-based government services in regional languages  
o healthcare solutions combining medical images and patient 
data  
o agriculture advisory using satellite and field-level data  
 They improve inclusion, as people can use AI through voice, 
images, and local languages, making technology accessible even 
to non-English users.  
 Under the IndiaAI Mission, there has been strong ecosystem 
participation, with 500+ proposals received for building foundation 
models, including multimodal systems, from startups and academic 
institutions.  
 India has already launched indigenous models such as BharatGen 
(multimodal model), Sarvam AI models (30B and 105B), and 
Gnani.ai’s Vachana, showing growing domestic capability.  
 These efforts are also creating new jobs and industries, especially 
in areas such as data services, model training, AI deployment, and 
digital platforms.  
 Overall, indigenous LMMs help India strengthen its position 
globally by enabling affordable, scalable, and locally relevant AI 
solutions, especially for the Global South. 
 
5.36 Providing the current status and details of the government 
agencies, academic institutions, and private sector entities involved, 
MeitY stated that the Government has initiated a comprehensive plan for 
the development of India-specific Large Language Models (LLMs) under 
the IndiaAI Foundation Models pillar, with active participation from 
government agencies, academic institutions, and private sector entities. 

121 | P a g e 
 
 The IndiaAI Foundation Models pillar aims to develop indigenous 
large and small language models, including multimodal systems, 
trained on Indian datasets and languages to support diverse 
national use cases.  
 A total of 12 organisations and consortia have been selected for 
development of these models, including:  
o Startups and industry players: Sarvam AI, Soket AI, Gnani 
AI, Gan AI, Avatar AI, GenLoop, Zentieq, Intellihealth, Shodh 
AI, Fractal Analytics Ltd., Tech Mahindra Maker’s Lab  
o Academic consortium: IIT Bombay-led BharatGen initiative  
 The Government is providing financial support for compute 
infrastructure, covering actual compute usage costs, along with an 
additional 25% support for datasets and personnel, to enable 
model development at scale.  
 As part of ongoing progress, sovereign models developed by 
Sarvam AI, BharatGen, and Gnani were unveiled at the IndiaAI 
Impact Summit 2026. These models have demonstrated strong 
performance on Indic language benchmarks and, in some cases, 
outperform leading global models on specific tasks. 
 The initiative involves multiple government agencies, including:  
o Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY)  
o IndiaAI Mission (Digital India Corporation)  
o Department of Science and Technology (DST)  
o UIDAI, among others  
 These efforts were showcased at the IndiaAI Impact Summit 2026, 
where indigenous models demonstrated strong performance on 
Indic language benchmarks, in some cases outperforming global 
models on specific tasks.  
 
5.37 The Ministry further stated that the development of India-specific 
LLMs may present challenges in ensuring interoperability with global AI 
ecosystems, due to differences in technology, data frameworks, and 
standards. 

122 | P a g e 
 
 Differences in model architectures, training datasets, and 
evaluation methods may limit seamless integration with global AI 
platforms.  
 Variations in data protection and privacy frameworks across 
countries may affect cross-border deployment of AI models.  
 Lack of standardised benchmarking and certification frameworks 
may create challenges in ensuring comparability and trust.  
 Use of Indic languages and local datasets may require additional 
adaptation for global applications.  
 Dependence on global cloud and semiconductor ecosystems may 
impact deployment compatibility. 
5.38 To address these challenges, the Government is taking the 
following concrete measures: 
 Under the IndiaAI Foundation Models pillar, selected organisations 
are developing models using standard ML frameworks and APIs, 
ensuring compatibility with global developer ecosystems. This also 
ensjures that the public-private collaboration is enabled. 
 AI models under the IndiaAI Foundation Model are expected to 
contribute to the open-source ecosystem by making them available 
through AIKosh platform for other startups and researchers. This 
will fuel innovation across India’s startup and research community. 
 The Government is supporting model benchmarking and evaluation 
frameworks under the Safe & Trusted AI pillar, including 
development of tools for fairness, robustness, and performance 
testing, to align with global evaluation practices. 
 Through the IndiaAI Compute infrastructure (38,000+ GPUs), 
models are being trained using globally accepted cloud-based 
environments, ensuring compatibility with international 
deployment systems. 
 The India AI Governance Guidelines (2025) provide a structured 
approach to data protection, transparency, and accountability, 
helping align domestic AI systems with emerging global norms. 
 

123 | P a g e 
 
5.39 On the absence of a clear domestic adoption pathway for India's 
own foundation models, NASSCOM submitted before the Committee on 
30 April 2026: 
“We do not have an AI inclusion roadmap right now. We have an 
India AI stack and all the layers we talk about, but how will it 
translate into an inclusion story, where we are building assets that 
will actually be used by Indians? We are investing in foundational 
companies like Sarvam AI and BharatGen, who are building India's 
AI models, but we are not talking about how they will get 
consumed by Indians. Government procurement for these models 
still has to follow the L1 tender process. China gave real 
preference to its own companies for their models to be adopted; if 
we still compete purely on price with Meta and Google and others, 
who have spent hundreds of billions of dollars, we will never have 
a level playing field. We need to look at our procurement 
processes to ensure that what we are investing in gets some 
disproportionate preference.” 
III. SKILLING AND THE EMPLOYMENT TRANSITION   
5.40 To a query on how AI will affect the global workforce, particularly 
in labour-intensive countries such as India, MeitY stated that AI is 
expected to significantly reshape the global workforce, with a shift from 
routine, labour-intensive roles towards skill-intensive and technology-
enabled employment. AI is expected to automate routine tasks in 
sectors such as manufacturing, retail, customer support and back-office 
services; since a large proportion of India's workforce is engaged in low- 
and mid-skill jobs, this segment is more exposed to such automation. At 
the same time, the Ministry emphasised that the effect is expected to be 
one of job reconfiguration rather than wholesale displacement, with 
existing roles evolving to include digital, analytical and AI-assisted 
functions, and with job polarisation - rising demand for high-skill roles 
alongside a relative decline in routine middle-skill roles - carrying 
implications for income distribution that will require large-scale reskilling 
and workforce adaptation. 
 

124 | P a g e 
 
5.41 Asked specifically about the key challenges and opportunities 
arising from these AI-driven changes in employment patterns, MeitY 
reiterated the risk of job polarisation and emerging skill gaps, while 
identifying new opportunities in AI development, data services, model 
training and AI-enabled platforms, including new forms of work and 
entrepreneurship through digital platforms, remote work and AI-driven 
start-ups. The Ministry stated that this transition is being supported 
through the IndiaAI Mission's FutureSkills programme, its network of 
Data and AI Labs, the Application Development Initiative, start-up 
financing, and the AIKosh datasets and compute infrastructure described 
in Section II above, which are together enabling growth in new roles 
such as data annotation, model training and AI services. 
 
5.42 On the sectors that are likely to witness job displacement, and are 
expected to see the creation of new and specialised employment 
opportunities, MeitY gave a sector-by-sector account. Manufacturing, 
retail, customer support and back-office services, including BPO/KPO, 
are more likely to witness displacement, as are routine testing, 
maintenance and support functions within IT services, and manual, 
repetitive roles in logistics and operations. By contrast, information 
technology, data services, cybersecurity, cloud computing, healthcare, 
education and financial services are expected to witness significant job 
creation, with new roles emerging in data annotation, model training, AI 
system monitoring and human-in-the-loop operations. Globally, the 
Ministry noted, estimates suggest that around 92 million jobs may be 
displaced while 170 million new roles could be created by 2030, 
reflecting a net expansion in employment; in India specifically, around 
24% of tasks may be automated and 42% augmented, indicating a shift 
toward human-AI collaboration rather than complete job loss. 
 
5.43 When the Committee desired to know about the measures being 
taken by the Government to promote AI for development, including job 
creation, MeitY set out a consolidated account of the IndiaAI Mission's 
employment-generating effects: the Compute pillar's 38,000-plus 
subsidised GPUs enabling start-ups, researchers and MSMEs to build AI 
solutions; the AIKosh datasets platform reducing entry barriers for 

125 | P a g e 
 
innovation in healthcare, agriculture and education; the Application 
Development Initiative's 30 approved AI applications generating demand 
for sector-specific AI roles; support for indigenous foundation model 
development strengthening domestic capabilities and high-skill 
employment; and IndiaAI FutureSkills and the planned network of 570 
Data and AI Labs building a skilled workforce pipeline extending into 
Tier-2 and Tier-3 cities. 
 
5.44 The representatives of MeitY addressed the employment question 
directly in testimony on 2 February 2026: 
“The other question on the human aspect and the impact on 
employment, I think we are very, very clear as far as India is 
concerned that while there could be some jobs lost, this is an 
analysis that NASSCOM has done, this is an analysis that NITI 
Aayog has done for us and the economic survey which came out 
last week has also analysed this aspect, that the job loss fears are 
not as severe as was originally thought to be. Our present 
situation, as far as job losses are concerned, is that while in 
certain segments like programming and coding there could be job 
losses, there could be many jobs which are created in other 
segments, especially in terms of creating applications and 
deploying them and in terms of enhancing productivity. 
The second aspect which I think is critical in India's context 
is that there are many services today which we are not able to 
deliver with the kind of human resources we have. In terms of the 
proportion of doctors or teachers or nurses or judges or policemen 
to the total population, we are far lower than many other countries 
in the world. So technology is one way in which the quality of 
services can be substantially enhanced in each of these spheres. 
Therefore, the immediate prospect of job loss is not so much the 
worry as the necessity to equip our population with the skills to 
adapt. That is where we run an extensive FutureSkills PRIME 
programme with NASSCOM.” 

126 | P a g e 
 
5.45 At a later sitting, on 18 March 2026, the representative of Ministry 
of Electronics and Information Technology elaborated further on the 
comparative nature of this disruption and submitted as under: 
“The first and most important difference between the Western 
countries and us, as far as AI is concerned, is the fact that AI is a 
technology which is cognitively based. So what it replaces is 
cognitive labour, white-collar jobs, much more than blue-collar 
jobs, unlike on previous occasions where industrial revolutions 
actually mechanised or automated manual labour. We have done a 
fairly detailed analysis in consultation with NITI Aayog, and the 
risk to a country like India from immediate direct job loss is 
significantly lower. We have six million people in the IT industry, 
which is our main chunk of white-collar jobs, and the basic idea is 
that they need to pivot from coding and programming jobs, which 
will undoubtedly decline. A detailed NITI Aayog study shows that 
about two million jobs will go, but four million more jobs will get 
created for creating applications and solutions — which is what we 
want to focus on, making India the capital of AI solutions and 
applications for the world.” 
5.46 During their oral evidence on 2 February 2026, NASSCOM 
separately flagged a gender dimension to this transition that merits 
continued attention: 
“While there is a gender bias in data, we also believe that if you 
look at overall women in the tech industry, it is about 36 per cent, 
but if you look at women in AI, it is about 24 per cent. So there is 
a gap divide that is already coming there, and it requires far 
greater intervention to make sure women in tech are not getting 
left behind with AI.” 
5.47 In the backdrop of rapid advancements in AI, the Committee 
enquired about steps being taken to ensure India's AI workforce is 
adequately skilled and ready to take on leadership roles in the global AI 
ecosystem. MeitY stated that India is strengthening its AI workforce 
through a combination of advanced skilling, research capacity building, 
industry alignment and global exposure, relying on IndiaAI FutureSkills, 

127 | P a g e 
 
a planned network of 570 Data and AI Labs, the FutureSkills PRIME 
reskilling programme, YUVAi and YUVA AI for All for school-age 
engagement, and the Skill India Digital Hub, which offers over 120 AI-
related digital courses. 
 
5.48 The Committee also enquired about steps being taken to ensure 
its AI workforce is adequately skilled and ready to take on leadership 
roles in the global AI ecosystem. In a written reply, MeitY stated that 
India is strengthening its AI workforce through a combination of 
advanced skilling, research capacity building, industry alignment, and 
global exposure, with a focus on enabling Indian talent to take on 
leadership roles in the global AI ecosystem. 
 The Government is implementing IndiaAI FutureSkills, supporting 
13,500 scholars across undergraduate, postgraduate, and PhD 
levels, to build a strong pipeline of advanced AI researchers and 
practitioners.  
 A nationwide network of AI and Data Labs is being established, 
with 30+ labs already launched as part of a 570-lab network, 
providing hands-on training and research exposure, particularly in 
Tier-2 and Tier-3 cities.  
 Programmes such as FutureSkills PRIME are enabling reskilling and 
upskilling of professionals in AI and emerging technologies, 
strengthening industry-ready capabilities.  
 Initiatives such as YUVAi (Youth for Unnati and Vikas with AI) are 
building early-stage talent, with programmes reaching school 
students nationwide and promoting applied AI learning.  
 The IndiaAI Fellowship Programme and advanced research 
initiatives are supporting development of high-end AI talent, 
including researchers working on foundation models, AI safety, 
and emerging technologies.  
 The Government is promoting industry-academia collaboration, 
enabling exposure to real-world problem solving through 
innovation challenges, hackathons, and applied research 

128 | P a g e 
 
programmes, including participation from startups and global 
technology companies.  
 Platforms such as the IndiaAI Impact Summit 2026 are providing 
global exposure, where 600+ startups and organisations 
showcased AI solutions, enabling Indian talent to engage with 
international experts and industry leaders.  
 The overall approach focuses on scaling talent across the pipeline 
(school to research), aligning skills with industry needs, and 
promoting globally relevant AI capabilities, positioning India’s 
workforce for leadership roles in the global AI ecosystem. 
 
5.49 Industry evidence placed a specific figure on the scale of the 
skilling task required, NASSCOM testified before the Committee on 2 
February 2026 and stated as under: 
“The issue of AI in employment is indeed crucial. India aims to 
train 8 to 10 million people. Not everyone will work directly in AI, 
but every job, be it a healthcare practitioner or a lawyer, will 
necessitate competence in AI. We will need a workforce to build 
AI, a workforce to infuse AI, and a workforce to utilise AI, and 
must focus on skilling for all three categories. Already, over a 
million individuals have been upskilled by our industry.” 
5.50 To the Committee’s query about how India compares with 
countries such as the United States and China in terms of existing AI 
skills and knowledge base, MeitY stated that India has developed a 
strong and expanding AI talent base, positioning itself among the 
leading global AI ecosystems, including ranking 3 rd globally in AI 
competitiveness, driven by its large workforce and high skill penetration. 
India accounts for a significant share of the global AI talent pool, 
supported by rapid growth in hiring and a strong digital and IT 
ecosystem. At the same time, countries such as the United States and 
China continue to have established strengths in frontier research, 
advanced model development, high-end talent, and large-scale compute 
infrastructure, which support their leadership in cutting-edge AI 
capabilities. 

129 | P a g e 
 
5.51 During testimony on 30 April 2026, one of the experts also 
suggested a call for an AI-literacy counterpart to India's earlier digital 
literacy campaigns as under: 
“Just as we did a digital literacy campaign, we need an AI literacy 
campaign. We cannot assume it will automatically percolate down 
to the last mile. We do need to think about AI literacy at the last 
mile, because of issues like deepfakes - a common person sitting 
in a village in India will still assume a fabricated voice recording is 
genuine. How do we even educate them about what is a deepfake 
and what is not?” 
IV. INDIA AND THE WORLD IN AI 
5.52 In response to the Committee’s specific query about the countries 
that are currently leading the world today in the field of AI, MeitY replied 
that the global AI landscape is being shaped by a group of advanced 
and emerging economies with complementary strengths across the AI 
value chain. The United States has developed strong capabilities in 
research, innovation, and advanced AI systems, while China has made 
significant progress in large-scale deployment and data-driven 
applications. Other countries such as the United Kingdom, Canada, 
Germany, France, Israel, Singapore, and the UAE are also advancing AI 
through targeted investments in research, governance, and sectoral 
adoption. India is emerging as a key player, with strengths in talent, 
digital public infrastructure, and scalable, inclusive deployment of AI 
solutions. 
 
5.53 When asked to indicate the areas in which India has comparative 
advantages or disadvantages when compared with other countries with 
well-developed AI ecosystems, MeitY responded that India has emerged 
as a leading AI ecosystem with strong structural strengths, while 
continuing to address key gaps in infrastructure and advanced research 
capabilities. 

130 | P a g e 
 
 India has a large and highly skilled talent base, ranking among the 
top countries globally in AI skill penetration, supported by a strong 
IT and digital workforce.  
 The country benefits from access to large and diverse datasets, 
driven by population-scale digital adoption and platforms, enabling 
development of inclusive and scalable AI solutions.  
 India’s Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI), including Aadhaar, UPI, 
and DigiLocker, provides a unique foundation for deploying AI at 
scale in governance and public service delivery.  
 A growing startup and innovation ecosystem, supported by 
initiatives such as the IndiaAI Mission, is driving development and 
adoption of AI solutions across sectors.  
 India’s balanced and inclusive policy approach (“AI for All”) 
positions it as a key voice in global AI governance, particularly for 
developing countries.  
 At the same time, India faces gaps in high-end compute 
infrastructure, including access to advanced chips and large-scale 
GPU capacity, compared to leading economies such as the US and 
China.  
 Private investment in AI, especially in frontier technologies and 
large-scale model development, remains lower relative to global 
leaders.  
 India is also working to strengthen advanced research output, 
intellectual property creation, and indigenous foundation model 
development.  
 Challenges related to standardisation, accessibility, and 
interoperability of datasets are being addressed through initiatives 
such as AIKosh. 
 
5.54 The Committee enquired about how the Government envisage 
positioning India distinctly from existing AI governance models being 
advanced by major powers such as the United States, the European 

131 | P a g e 
 
Union, and China and what is the uniquely Indian proposition in global 
AI governance. In a written reply, MeitY stated that India is adopting a 
balanced, inclusive, and development-oriented approach to AI 
governance, distinct from existing global models, with a focus on 
innovation, trust, and equitable access. 
 Human-centric and inclusive approach (AI for All): India’s 
approach prioritises inclusive growth and equitable access to AI 
benefits, ensuring that AI supports socio-economic development 
and public welfare, especially in developing country contexts.  
 Balanced governance model (innovation and safeguards): Unlike 
highly prescriptive regulatory models, India follows a pro-
innovation and risk-based approach, balancing the need for 
innovation with safeguards for safety, privacy, and accountability.  
 Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI)-led model: India leverages 
population-scale DPI (Aadhaar, UPI, DigiLocker) as a foundation 
for AI deployment, enabling scalable, cost-effective, and inclusive 
AI solutions for governance and public services.  
 IndiaAI Governance Guidelines (2025): India has released 
comprehensive AI governance guidelines based on principles such 
as trust, fairness, accountability, and safety, supported by 
actionable recommendations across infrastructure, risk mitigation, 
and institutional mechanisms.  
 Democratisation of AI resources: Through the IndiaAI Mission, 
India is focusing on expanding access to compute (GPU 
infrastructure), datasets (AIKosh), and skilling, reducing 
concentration of AI capabilities and enabling broader participation.  
 Global South leadership and cooperation: India actively advocates 
for an inclusive global AI order, ensuring that the needs and 
priorities of developing countries are reflected in global AI 
governance frameworks.  
 Open, interoperable, and trusted ecosystems: India promotes open 
standards, interoperability, and trusted cross-border AI 

132 | P a g e 
 
deployment, while supporting indigenous innovation through 
initiatives like BharatGen and Bhashini.  
5.56 On the Committee’s query about the countries which have adopted 
regulatory framework for AI, MeitY submitted that several countries and 
regions have adopted or are in the process of adopting regulatory and 
governance frameworks for Artificial Intelligence, reflecting a global shift 
towards ensuring safe, responsible, and trustworthy AI deployment. 
 A number of jurisdictions have introduced comprehensive, legally 
binding frameworks, such as the AI Act adopted by the European 
Union, which follows a risk-based classification approach, with 
stricter obligations for high-risk AI systems.  
 Some countries, including the United States, have adopted a 
principles-based and sectoral approach, supported by instruments 
such as the Executive Order on Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy AI 
and technical frameworks like the AI Risk Management Framework 
(AI RMF).  
 Certain jurisdictions have implemented targeted regulations for 
specific AI applications, including rules on generative AI services, 
algorithmic recommendation systems, and deep synthesis 
technologies, focusing on content authenticity, safety, and 
compliance.  
 Several countries have developed model governance frameworks 
and toolkits, such as the Model AI Governance Framework and AI 
Verify testing toolkit, to support organisations in implementing 
responsible AI practices.  
 Many countries have issued national AI strategies and ethical 
guidelines, including human-centric AI principles, to guide 
development while maintaining flexibility for innovation.  
 At the global level, organisations such as the Organisation for 
Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) and the United 
Nations have established principles and recommendations for 
responsible AI, which serve as reference frameworks for national 
policies.  

133 | P a g e 
 
5.57 On being asked about India's own stance on the global regulation 
of AI, particularly its potential impact on developing economies, MeitY 
stated that this stance is guided by the need to ensure that AI 
governance frameworks remain inclusive, development-oriented and 
equitable, particularly for developing economies.  
 India advocates a balanced approach to AI regulation, ensuring 
safety, transparency, and accountability, while also enabling 
innovation and wider adoption of AI technologies.  
 A key element of India’s position is the principle of “AI for All”, 
which emphasises democratisation of access to AI technologies, 
data, and compute, so that benefits are not concentrated in a few 
economies.  
 India has highlighted the risk of a widening global AI divide, and 
therefore supports frameworks that promote equitable access, 
capacity building, and technology sharing for developing countries.  
 India’s approach emphasises alignment of AI with development 
priorities, including healthcare, agriculture, education, and public 
service delivery, which are critical for emerging economies.  
 India is promoting the concept of shared AI resources, including 
through initiatives under the IndiaAI Mission, such as: 
o AIKosh, a national platform providing access to datasets, 
models, and tools, enabling wider and responsible use of 
data for AI development. 
o Provision of shared compute infrastructure (38,000+ GPUs) 
at subsidised rates, lowering entry barriers for startups, 
researchers, and developing country partners. 
o Support for development of indigenous foundation models, 
which can be adapted for multilingual and Global South use 
cases. 
 At the multilateral level, India supports inclusive and participatory 
governance frameworks, ensuring that developing countries have a 
meaningful role in shaping global AI norms and standards.  

134 | P a g e 
 
 Through platforms such as the IndiaAI Impact Summit 2026, India 
is facilitating Global South-led dialogue, bringing together 
governments, industry, and international organisations to advance 
inclusive and responsible AI governance.  
 Overall, India’s position is that global AI regulation should be 
flexible, inclusive, and supportive of development, ensuring that it 
enables rather than constrains the ability of developing economies 
to benefit from AI.  
5.58 To the same question, the Ministry of External Affairs replied that 
India has articulated, in its external engagements, support for principles-
based, risk-proportionate and innovation-friendly approaches to AI 
governance rather than prescriptive, one-size-fits-all global rules. India 
has emphasised that any emerging global framework must safeguard 
national policy space, acknowledge diverse levels of technological 
maturity and avoid regulatory burdens that could disadvantage 
developing economies. India therefore advocates a flexible architecture 
combining shared principles of safety, accountability, transparency and 
inclusiveness with context-specific national implementation. It also calls 
for enhanced international cooperation on capacity-building, technology 
access and open, multilingual AI systems so that developing countries 
can benefit from AI while managing its risks. 
5.59 When asked about the manner in which India is addressing the 
ethical and societal implications of AI adoption, both domestically and 
globally, MeitY responded that India is addressing the ethical and 
societal implications of AI adoption through a combination of national 
governance frameworks, institutional mechanisms, regulatory measures, 
and global engagement, aimed at ensuring that AI is developed and 
deployed in a safe, inclusive, and accountable manner. 
 India has established a comprehensive governance framework 
through the India AI Governance Guidelines (2025), which 
emphasise fairness, transparency, accountability, and safety in AI 
systems, while adopting a risk-based approach.  

135 | P a g e 
 
 The framework focuses on human-centric AI, ensuring protection 
against risks such as bias, discrimination, privacy violations, and 
misuse of AI systems.  
 India is strengthening institutional mechanisms through the 
IndiaAI Mission, which includes targeted interventions to 
operationalise responsible AI: 
o Under the Safe & Trusted AI pillar, 13+ projects have been 
supported across areas such as bias mitigation, explainable 
AI, privacy-preserving machine learning, and AI auditing 
frameworks, enabling development of indigenous safety 
tools. 
o The IndiaAI Safety Institute is being established to advance 
research on AI safety, model evaluation, and risk testing, 
with 75+ applications received under the Expression of 
Interest process for onboarding partner institutions. 
o Through platforms such as AIKosh, structured access to 
datasets and models is being enabled, with focus on 
responsible data use and governance standards. 
 To address risks such as deepfakes and misinformation, measures 
such as labelling of AI-generated content and strengthening digital 
platform accountability are being considered.  
 India is promoting capacity building and awareness, including 
integration of ethical AI practices in skilling programmes and public 
engagement platforms.  
5.60 At the global level, India is actively contributing to discussions on 
responsible AI governance, advocating for inclusive, human-centric, and 
development-oriented frameworks, particularly for developing countries. 
Through initiatives such as the IndiaAI Impact Summit 2026, India is 
fostering multi-stakeholder dialogue and international  collaboration on 
ethical and societal dimensions of AI. 
5.61 On the question of international regulatory harmonisation, MeitY 
candidly stated as under: 

136 | P a g e 
 
“On the issue of regulatory harmonisation in AI, data sovereignty, 
data privacy, certainly these are issues which are up for discussion 
right now. There are no binding international norms in digital 
governance as of now. Every country is having its own template of 
developing these rules and procedures, and so we have also our 
own regulations. Only recently we have notified our data privacy 
rules under the Act. So as we see, this is an evolving landscape in 
terms of regulatory postures of different countries. We are very 
alive to what is happening globally. We are developing our own 
systems, and we will be working very closely with the other 
countries here to share what we are hearing from other partner 
countries in this domain, and see how we can sort of take that into 
account by developing our own ecosystem. There is certainly no 
single harmonization effort that we see at this stage. Our approach 
remains one of self-regulation which emphasises trusted 
responsible innovation and also sector-specific oversight by 
existing regulators, rather than a one-size-fits-all regime, so as to 
promote innovation and accountability while respecting privacy 
rights.” 
5.62 As per the Stanford Global AI Vibrancy Tool 2025, rising from 
seventh place the previous year, India has achieved a landmark 3rd 
position globally in AI competitiveness. India now ranks behind only the 
United States and China, surpassing established leaders like the United 
Kingdom and South Korea. The surge is supported by government 
initiatives like the IndiaAI Mission which prioritizes computing 
infrastructure and multilingual AI models. India's high ranking is further 
driven by significant improvements in talent development, research 
output (patents/publications), and increasing startup funding. Through 
active engagements supported by a robust domestic ecosystem, India 
positions itself as a bridge between advanced AI economies and the 
Global South, advocating for more democratic access to AI capabilities. 
Multilateral Engagement 
5.63 India has consistently advanced a human-centric, trustworthy and 
responsible approach to AI in multilateral and plurilateral forums, 

137 | P a g e 
 
including the UN processes related to the Global Digital Compact, GPAI, 
G20 and BRICS. India had also accepted UNESCO’s Global 
Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence adopted by UN 
Member States in 2021. India’s interventions underline safety, 
transparency, accountability and respect for human rights, while 
preserving innovation and development priorities, especially for the 
Global South. 
5.64 Bilateral partnerships increasingly include dedicated mechanisms 
and on digital and emerging technologies, through which India promotes 
cooperation on AI standards, risk-based governance and capacity-
building for safe deployment. It may be recalled that during our G20 
2023 presidency, India introduced a framework for "Responsible Human-
Centric AI Governance," emphasising the inclusion of the Global South. 
Government of India also facilitates participation of Indian experts in 
international expert groups and multi-stakeholder platforms to shape 
global best practices on responsible AI. 
5.65 On the steps India is taking bilaterally and multilaterally to 
promote responsible AI, MeitY pointed out that India is actively 
promoting the responsible, safe, and inclusive use of AI through a 
combination of domestic initiatives, bilateral engagement, and 
participation in multilateral platforms. 
 India has developed a national approach to responsible AI through 
initiatives such as the IndiaAI Mission and the India AI Governance 
Guidelines (2025), which emphasise safety, transparency, 
accountability, and ethical use of AI. It has established the IndiaAI 
Safety Institute to advance research on AI safety, model 
evaluation, and risk mitigation, including development of tools for 
bias detection, auditing, and secure deployment.  
 India is also using global platforms such as the IndiaAI Impact 
Summit 2026 to bring together governments, industry, academia, 
and international organisations to discuss responsible AI, 
governance frameworks, and global cooperation, and to showcase 
India’s approach to inclusive and trustworthy AI.  

138 | P a g e 
 
 At the bilateral level, India is engaging with partner countries on 
AI governance frameworks, safety standards, model evaluation, 
and capacity building, along with collaboration on research and 
innovation.  
 India is also working with partner countries on knowledge sharing 
and joint initiatives, including in areas such as digital public 
infrastructure and AI applications for development.  
 At the multilateral level, India is actively participating in global 
platforms such as the G20, Global Partnership on Artificial 
Intelligence (GPAI), United Nations, and BRICS, contributing to 
discussions on responsible AI, governance frameworks, and 
inclusive innovation.  
 India is advocating for equitable access to AI resources, including 
compute, data, and talent, to address the global AI divide and 
promote inclusive growth, particularly for developing countries.  
 India is also supporting development of interoperable standards, 
trusted AI systems, and cross-border cooperation frameworks, to 
enable safe and responsible deployment of AI globally. 
5.66 Beyond its bilateral and plurilateral engagement, India participates 
in a number of multilateral scientific and science-adjacent efforts on AI, 
set out in a written submission placed before the Committee. 
● Under the United Nations Office for Digital and Emerging 
Technologies, India has offered IIT Madras as a Centre of 
Excellence in the UN-supported network for AI capacity 
development, giving India a concrete institutional mechanism to 
support AI skilling, responsible deployment and capacity-building 
for developing countries, especially in the Global South. India is 
co-facilitating this network with Senegal, with formal launch 
planned for July in Geneva. 
● India has engaged directly with the UN's emerging institutional 
architecture for AI, including the Independent International 
Scientific Panel on AI and the Global Dialogue on AI Governance. 
India is one of 37 countries with representation on the Panel, 

139 | P a g e 
 
giving it a voice in the global scientific assessment of AI risks, 
capabilities and societal impacts. 
● India, through its representative in the advisory group to the UK-
commissioned International Scientific Report on Advanced AI 
Safety, chaired by Yoshua Bengio, helped ensure that the concerns 
of the Global South found mention in the report, the first version 
of which had been considered largely Western-focused. 
● The New Delhi Frontier AI Impact Commitments, announced at the 
AI Impact Summit 2026, were endorsed by 13 global and Indian 
AI developers, focusing on advancing real-world AI usage through 
anonymised insights for evidence-based policymaking, and 
multilingual AI evaluations to ensure cultural awareness, with a 
specific focus on human safety, equity and the priorities of the 
Global South. 
● The New Delhi Declaration from the Summit introduced several 
further deliverables: the Global AI Impact Commons, a voluntary 
platform encouraging the replication and scaling of successful AI 
use cases across regions; the Trusted AI Commons, a non-binding 
repository of technical resources and best practice for AI 
developers; the Charter for the Democratic Diffusion of AI, 
promoting affordable and equitable access to foundational AI 
computing; the International Network of AI for Science 
Institutions, connecting international researchers and pooling 
shared AI capabilities; and AI Governance Guidelines and an AI 
Workforce Development Playbook, on energy-efficient AI systems, 
infrastructure resilience, and AI literacy and reskilling. 
● The Global South Network for Trustworthy AI, a civil society-led 
initiative launched at the Summit, is designed to elevate the 
representation of the Global South in global AI governance, 
moving these regions from participants to active leaders in shaping 
AI safety standards, with founding members including the Digital 
Futures Lab, the Global Centre on AI Governance, and the Centre 
for Responsible AI at IIT Madras. 

140 | P a g e 
 
● On AI standards diplomacy, India has become an active 
contributor to global AI standard-setting through the Bureau of 
Indian Standards. BIS LITD 30, the Artificial Intelligence Technical 
Committee, develops AI standards for India and contributes to 
ISO/IEC JTC 1/SC 42, the principal international standards 
committee on artificial intelligence. India hosted the 15th ISO/IEC 
JTC 1/SC 42 plenary and subgroup meetings in New Delhi, 
bringing together more than 350 experts from 70 countries, and 
has led discussions on standards for the resilience assessment of 
AI systems, while participating in global work on data governance, 
trustworthiness, foundational AI standards, de-identification, and 
quality assurance for generative AI applications. 
 
5.67 India has been advocating for an inclusive approach to AI 
governance at international forums like the UN and BRICS. When asked 
about how India ensures that the interests of the Global South are 
adequately represented in AI governance frameworks, MeitY responded 
that India has been consistently advocating for an inclusive, equitable, 
and development-oriented approach to AI governance, ensuring that the 
interests of the Global South are adequately reflected in emerging global 
frameworks. 
 India’s approach to AI governance is anchored in the principle of 
“AI for All”, which emphasises democratisation of access to AI 
technologies, data, and compute, so that benefits are not 
concentrated in a few countries or corporations. 
 India has promoted the idea of inclusive and human-centric AI 
governance frameworks, ensuring that AI supports development 
priorities such as agriculture, healthcare, education, and public 
service delivery, which are critical for developing countries. 
 At the multilateral level, India has played an active role in shaping 
discussions within forums such as BRICS, where member countries 
have jointly emphasised that AI governance should not deepen 
global inequalities and should support inclusive growth and 
sustainable development. 

141 | P a g e 
 
 India has also supported a stronger role for the United Nations in 
developing globally accepted AI governance frameworks, ensuring 
that all countries, including developing economies, have a voice in 
rule-making processes. 
 Through initiatives such as the IndiaAI Impact Summit 2026, India 
has created a platform hosted in the Global South, bringing 
together leaders from multiple countries, international 
organisations, and stakeholders to discuss inclusive AI governance 
and development priorities.  
 India has also proposed concepts such as a “global AI commons”, 
aimed at enabling shared access to datasets, compute 
infrastructure, and AI tools, particularly for developing countries, 
to reduce the global AI divide.  
 Through its upcoming leadership roles in groupings such as BRICS 
(2026), India is further working to strengthen South-South 
cooperation, capacity building, and knowledge sharing in AI 
governance and deployment. 
 Domestically, India’s governance approach, through initiatives such 
as the IndiaAI Mission and governance guidelines, focuses on 
affordable compute, open datasets, and scalable digital public 
infrastructure, which serve as models that can be adapted by other 
developing countries. 
 
5.68 On the same question, the Ministry of External Affairs replied that 
through its diplomatic outreach, India systematically brings Global South 
perspectives into AI discussions at the UN and BRICS. India stresses 
issues such as equitable access to AI, avoidance of one-size-fits-all 
regulations, recognition of diverse development paths and the need to 
prevent new digital dependencies. In particular, we have utilised these 
forums to create shared AI ethics, strengthen South-South cooperation, 
and bridge the gap for countries marginalized in global discourse. 
5.69 During BRICS 2025, India actively advocated the inclusive 
approach to AI governance which is reflected in the BRICS Leaders' 

142 | P a g e 
 
Statement on the Global Governance of Artificial Intelligence. GoI has 
also been supporting capacity-building, sharing India’s digital public 
infrastructure experience and exploring cooperative arrangements on 
data, compute and AI applications tailored to partner countries, thereby 
anchoring governance debates in the realities of developing economies. 
5.70 On being asked about the role of Indian companies and the private 
sector play in diplomatic engagements on AI and digital infrastructure, 
and notable collaborations, MeitY submitted that Indian companies and 
the private sector play an important role in supporting India’s diplomatic 
engagements in AI and digital infrastructure, contributing through 
investments, innovation, global partnerships, and participation in 
international platforms, and positioning India as a credible partner in the 
global AI ecosystem. 
 Indian companies are actively contributing to the development of 
AI models, digital platforms, and infrastructure, supporting India’s 
efforts to build sovereign and scalable AI capabilities under the 
IndiaAI Mission.  
 Leading domestic industry players have announced large-scale 
investments in AI and digital infrastructure, including long-term 
commitments towards development of AI-ready data centres, 
compute infrastructure, and digital ecosystems, which also 
strengthen India’s global positioning in AI.  
 Indian IT and technology companies such as Infosys, Tata 
Consultancy Services, and Wipro are actively engaged in global AI 
partnerships, cross-border technology collaboration, and 
participation in international AI governance and ethics discussions.  
 Industry bodies such as NASSCOM play a key role in representing 
Indian industry perspectives in international policy dialogues, trade 
discussions, and regulatory engagements related to AI and digital 
technologies.  
 Indian startups and innovation-led firms, including those supported 
under the IndiaAI Mission, are contributing to development of

 Platforms such as the IndiaAI Impact Summit 2026 have enabled

governance and infrastructure.  
o The Summit brought together leading global AI companies, 
policymakers, and Indian industry stakeholders, providing a

capabilities and engage in international collaborations, 
reinforcing India’s position as a technology partner in the 
global AI ecosystem.  
 Indian companies are also contributing to capacity building and

5.71 Moreover, their participation in GPAI, global AI summits and 
standard-setting processes allows India to bring practical innovation 
perspectives into intergovernmental negotiations on AI, data and 
connectivity. GoI engages these actors through structured consultations, 
business delegations and track-1.5/track-2 dialogues linked to major 
international meetings. Indian entities are involved in cross-border 
digital public infrastructure projects and collaborative AI pilots in priority 
sectors, reinforcing India’s profile as a trusted and development-oriented 
digital partner for the Global South. 
5.72 Further, GoI proactively encourages the private sector (including 
companies, civil society organizations, academicians, NGO’s etc) to 
participate as key stakeholders in relevant UN mechanisms and 
international forums (like Global Digtal Compact’s Global Dialogue on AI 
Governance, UN Commission on Science and Technology for 
Development -CSTD, etc.) leveraging Indian tech expertise to shape 
global standards, including in AI safety, data governance and DPI. 

144 | P a g e 
 
V. THE GLOBAL AI DIVIDE AND INDIA'S RESPONSE  
5.73 The Global AI Divide continues to widen, with AI resources and 
capabilities concentrated among select nations and corporations. When 
asked about the major countries and corporates controlling AI resources 
and capabilities, MEA stated that as per information available from public 
sources, most of the cutting-edge AI resources are concentrated mainly 
in a few advanced economies in North America, Europe and East Asia 
and in a small number of global technology companies that operate 
major cloud and AI platforms.  
5.74 On this issue, MeitY further added: 
 A significant share of global AI investment is concentrated in a 
small number of economies, with private AI investment in leading 
markets exceeding USD 100 billion in 2024, and continuing to 
remain at elevated levels, reflecting the high capital intensity of AI 
development.  
 A large share of global high-end AI compute capacity is 
concentrated in a few regions, given the high cost, infrastructure 
requirements, and technological complexity involved.  
 The global AI landscape is also shaped by a limited number of 
large technology companies that play a central role in cloud 
infrastructure, foundation models, and semiconductor supply 
chains.  
 These entities benefit from access to large-scale datasets, 
advanced research ecosystems, and significant capital investment, 
contributing to the concentration of AI capabilities. 
5.75 Asked as to where does India stand in the global concentration of 
AI capabilities, MeitY stated that India has emerged as a significant 
player in the global AI ecosystem, with strong performance across key 
indicators such as talent, skill penetration, innovation activity, and 
market growth, reflecting its steadily expanding capabilities in 
comparison to other leading ecosystems. 

145 | P a g e 
 
 India ranks among the top three global AI ecosystems, as per 
global AI competitiveness/vibrancy indices, reflecting its growing 
capabilities in talent, innovation, and adoption.  
 India has one of the largest AI talent bases globally, accounting for 
around 16% of the global AI workforce, with a rapidly expanding 
pool of professionals.  
 India leads globally in AI talent acquisition, with an annual hiring 
growth rate of approximately 33%, indicating strong demand and 
ecosystem expansion.  
 India is a major contributor to global AI development, accounting 
for 19.9% of global AI projects on GitHub, reflecting strong 
developer participation.  
 India ranks first globally in AI skill penetration, with levels 
significantly above the global average, supported by a large digital 
workforce.  
 India’s AI workforce is projected to more than double to 1.25 
million by 2027, indicating strong growth in human capital 
capacity.  
 India’s AI market is growing rapidly, with projections to reach over 
USD 130 billion by 2032, reflecting increasing adoption across 
sectors.  
 At the same time, India continues to build capabilities in advanced 
compute infrastructure and frontier research, indicating an 
evolving position in the global AI landscape. 
5.76 Regarding the implications of the global AI divide for collective 
global development goals, particularly for developing countries and the 
measures India has undertaken to promote the democratisation of AI 
and digital access, especially for the Global South, MeitY stated that the 
widening global AI divide has important implications for equitable global 
development, particularly for developing countries, while also 
highlighting the need for inclusive frameworks and international 

146 | P a g e 
 
cooperation. India has undertaken several initiatives to promote 
democratisation of AI and digital access, especially for the Global South. 
 The concentration of AI capabilities can lead to unequal access to 
technology, data, and compute resources, resulting in widening 
economic and productivity gaps and limited representation of 
developing countries in global AI governance frameworks.  
 At the same time, AI presents an opportunity for developing 
countries to leverage technology for leapfrogging traditional 
development pathways, particularly in sectors such as healthcare, 
education, and agriculture.  
 India has adopted an approach centred on “AI for All”, promoting 
inclusive and development-oriented use of AI across sectors, 
including governance, financial inclusion, and social services.  
 Through initiatives such as the IndiaAI Mission, India is working 
towards democratising access to compute, datasets, and 
innovation platforms, enabling wider participation from startups, 
academia, and public institutions.  
 India has played an active role in multilateral forums such as G20, 
GPAI, and BRICS, advocating for inclusive, human-centric, and 
development-oriented AI governance frameworks.  
 The IndiaAI Impact Summit 2026, the first such global AI summit 
hosted by a Global South country, brought together countries, 
industry, and institutions to advance shared principles, 
partnerships, and practical pathways for inclusive AI development.  
 India is also leveraging its Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI) and 
multilingual AI initiatives to enable scalable and affordable AI 
solutions, which can be adapted and replicated across developing 
countries.  
5.77 India, through its engagements at multilateral, plurilateral and 
bilateral platforms, as well as through the recently hosted AI Impact 
Summit has endeavored to promote the diffusion of inclusive AI and 
digital public infrastructure models and has advocated open, multilingual 

147 | P a g e 
 
and affordable AI tools for sectors such as agriculture, health and 
education in the Global South. The Summit’s ‘Democratising AI 
Resources’ working group and its deliverable ‘Charter for the Democratic 
Diffusion of AI’ have stressed the need for supporting pooled AI 
infrastructure, enhance capacity building, openness and trustworthiness 
in AI innovation and development so as to develop resilient, equitable 
and affordable AI ecosystem. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

148 | P a g e 
 
CHAPTER 6 
AI IMPACT SUMMIT 
The Government of India hosted the AI Impact Summit in 
February 2026 at Bharat Mandapam, New Delhi, as the largest global 
convening to shape the future of inclusive, responsible, and resilient 
Artificial Intelligence (AI). Announced by the Hon’ble Prime Minister 
during the Action Summit in Feb 2025, the summit built on the 
momentum of previous multilateral initiatives including the Bletchley 
Park, Seoul, Paris, and Kigali Summits, and marked a shift from 
aspirations to impact, demonstrating how AI can deliver tangible 
outcomes for People, Planet, and Progress. 
 
I. PREPARATORY PROCESS AND PRE SUMMIT EVENTS 
6.2 The Summit was conceptualised based on extensive groundwork, 
including multiple rounds of public consultations and global outreach 
sessions, including in Paris, Berlin, Oslo, New York, Geneva, Bangkok, 
and Tokyo. Seven Regional events across India in the lead up to the 
Summit ensured grassroots voices are represented, generating 
actionable insights and broadened participation. Complemented by 
curated consultation sessions for the flagship events and working 
groups, these efforts reflect India’s deeply participatory approach to the 
Impact Summit. 
6.3 Following this decentralised and democratised approach, the lead-
up to the AI Impact Summit began nine months in advance, with more 
than 550 pre-summit events conducted across 30+ countries, 
culminating in what became the largest AI-focused summit to date.   
 
6.4 Apprising the Committee about the strategic objectives behind 
India AI Impact Summit, the representative of the Ministry of External 
Affairs on 2.2.2026 submitted as under: 
“The strategic objectives that we are aiming for , along with some 
additional context and updates, are as follows. The first objective 

149 | P a g e 
 
is to translate existing AI regulatory principles into actionable 
policy frameworks, cooperative mechanisms, and leading initiatives 
that can be adopted by countries at different levels of digital 
maturity. Another objective is to advance safe, trusted, and 
responsible AI while ensuring that innovation flourishes, with a 
special focus on equitable access to foundational AI resources, 
such as computing power, high-quality datasets, and skills.  
The third objective is to amplify global south perspectives in AI 
governance and norm-setting, positioning developing countries as 
co-creators and innovators rather than mere adopters of AI 
technologies and standards. The next objective involves 
showcasing real-world AI use cases that deliver measurable impact 
in public services, sustainability, and economic growth.” 
 
6.5 About reframing of the Summit in relation to previous Summits, 
the representatives of the Ministry of External Affairs during their 
evidence before the Committee on 2 February 2026 stated as under: 
“While earlier meetings of the AI summits primarily focussed on 
safety, security and environmental risks. Essentially, they 


150 | P a g e 
 
remained transatlantic in their framing. That has changed in this. 
Our Summit deliberately shifted the focus of its conversation.  We 
have moved the discourse from a narrow focus on safety and 
regulation to development first. Global South anchored agenda, 
their applications of AI, access to resources and inclusion are 
treated as core governance questions rather than as an 
afterthought. The fact that this was the first summit of its series 
held in the Global South holds both political and symbolic 
significance from a diplomatic foreign policy perspective.” 
6.6 Regarding the specific outcomes on which India sought consensus, 
the representatives of the Ministry of External Affairs on 2 February 2026 
stated: 
“We are striving to achieve consensus on several outcomes. First, 
charter for the democratisation and diffusion of AI resources, 
computing power, data, models, talent, etc., with an emphasis on 
affordability, regional collaboration, and shared infrastructure. 
Second, on safe and trusted AI, covering risk management, 
governance, accountability, and trust-building aligned with real-
world deployment. Third, frameworks and playbooks on human 
capital and skills for the AI era, including AI literacy, reskilling and 
upskilling, MSME support, and cross-border recognition of 
credentials. Fourth, a global AI commons platform focusing on AI 
for social good and sectoral impact, along with a voluntary alliance 
on AI for social empowerment and AI for scientific advancement.” 
 
6.7 The Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology has 
informed that altogether 569 events were organised all across the globe 
in 160 cities and 37 countries with almost 2.9 lakh participants engaged 
worldwide. Seven regional events and 7 Regional Events & 7 Working 
Group Meetings were also held across 12 Cities. 

151 | P a g e 
 
 
II. MAIN SUMMIT AND PARTICIPATION 
6.8 The main summit was hosted at Bharat Mandapam alongside 
simultaneous events across the city, supported by a dedicated media 
pavilion and studios. Over 580 sessions were held at Bharat Mandapam 
and another 150 city-wide, curated from 700+ global proposals received 
through an open call. The Summit witnessed participation from 22 
Heads of State/Government, more than 100 countries and 10 
international organisations, and more than 100 CEOs/CTOs (including 
leadership from almost all major AI companies). Further, the India AI 
Impact Expo provided an opportunity for over 900 organisations to 
showcase the cutting edge of AI Innovation (250+ corporates and 600+ 


152 | P a g e 
 
startups booths). Visitor footfall exceeded six lakh, with strong media 
presence and over 9 Lakh video views online, 72,700 hours of watch 
time, and nearly 35,000 new subscribers to the IndiaAI YouTube 
channel. 
 
6.9 While giving testimony before the Committee on 18 March 2026, 
Secretary MEITY provided the following details about the participation 
during the India AI Impact Summit: 
“The actual number of countries who attended eventually was 
more than 100. We had more than a hundred global CEOs. We 
had about 700 major events. In terms of the number of people 
who attended, compared to what happened in France, about 1500 
with about 35 rotating booths, we had more than 900 booths and 
about six lakh people attending. The number of countries and 
international organisations who signed the Declaration in France 
was 58; and here, it was 92. I would like to underline that the US 
and the UK, who had not signed in France, signed here. And China
