was also represented here. So, I think, in that sense, the largest 
countries in terms of AI were represented. 
At the main Summit, we had three major events which 
demonstrated what exactly was going on and widespread 
participation including from across the world. The AI for All was 
actually demonstrable AI applications because we believe that 
India would eventually become the Capital of AI applications and 
solutions. This particular part of the Summit was very important to 
us.  
‘AI by HER’ focussed primarily on women and ‘YUVAi’ was 
basically participants in the age group of 13 to 20. And all of this 
had extensive participations.” 
6.10 He further added: 
“In addition to the events officially held at Bharat Mandapam and 
Sushma Swaraj Bhawan, a large number of private agencies 
organised events in hotels and other centres across the city. These 
events also attracted significant attention. As I mentioned earlier, 

153 | P a g e 
 
several leading personalities participated. Almost every major AI 
company was represented. Companies like Google and OpenAI 
were present. Mr. Sundar Pichai of Google, Mr. Cristiano Amon of 
Qualcomm, and Philips, which is a major user of AI particularly in 
the healthcare sector, were all present. The Chief AI Officer of 
Meta was also there. Indian CEOs were adequately represented as 
well. Companies like NVIDIA, Microsoft, and Adobe, all of whom 
are working in this space, were present.” 
 
 


154 | P a g e 
 
Structure of the Summit: Sutras and Chakras 
6.11 The Summit was anchored in three guiding principles or Sutras of 
People, Planet, and Progress, which frame how AI should serve 
humanity, safeguard the environment, and drive inclusive growth. These 
guiding Sutras were further operationalized through seven Chakras or 
thematic working groups, each focusing on a critical dimension of AI’s 
global impact. These were human capital; democratizing AI resources; 
inclusion for social empowerment; economic growth and social good; 
resilience, innovation and efficiency; AI in science; and safe and trusted 
AI. In the run-up to the Summit, four rounds of working group meetings 
under the seven themes were organised, co-chaired by India and 
partner countries: The details of the co-chairs and their respective 
themes are tabulated below: 
 
Working Group Deliberations 
6.12 The seven working groups were mandated to translate the 
Summit’s three foundational pillars into concrete areas of action across 
their respective themes. The deliberations of all seven working groups 
were designed to feed into the leaders’ outcome document and 
associated deliverables. 
Theme Co-chairs 
Human Capital The Philippines, Rwanda 
Inclusion for Social 
Empowerment 
Nigeria, Switzerland 
Safe and Trusted AI Brazil, Japan 
Resilience, Innovation and 
Efficiency 
France 
Science Singapore, Canada 
Democratizing AI Resources Egypt, Kenya 
AI for Economic growth and 
Social Good 
Indonesia, The Netherlands 

155 | P a g e 
 
 Under “AI in Science”, the working group considered institutional 
arrangements among research institutions, equitable participation 
in AI-driven science and research, mechanisms for sustained 
knowledge exchange and capacity building, bridging gaps in 
access to AI research infrastructure, and collaborative approaches 
to enabling research ecosystems for AI in science. Its deliverable 
Network of AI for Science Institutions has been endorsed by 19 
countries. 
 Under “Human Capital”, discussions focused on practical measures 
to accelerate AI reskilling and upskilling, collaboration on scalable 
and inclusive AI capacity-building initiatives, common guiding 
principles for AI workforce transition, best practices in vocational 
and technical training ecosystems, and inclusive investments in AI 
human capital. Its deliverable Voluntary Guiding Principles for 
Skilling and Reskilling in the Age of AI has been endorsed by 25 
countries and IO’s. 
 Under “Economic Growth and Social Good”, the working group 
examined applications of AI for inclusive economic growth and 
societal well-being, mechanisms to promote diffusion of high-
impact AI use cases, international collaboration on exchanging 
knowledge, best practices and resources to develop and scale AI 
applications, and frameworks to identify and incentivise AI 
solutions.  
 The “Safe and Trusted AI” working group deliberated on enhancing 
common understanding of AI safety and principles, facilitating 
equitable access to AI safety knowledge, tools, protocols and 
technical guidance, and international cooperation on interoperable 
and context-relevant governance frameworks or guidelines that 
enable innovation while mitigating potential harms. Its deliverables 
Framework for the Trusted AI Commons and Guidance Note on AI 
Governance have been endorsed by 23 countries and IO’s. 
 Under “Democratizing AI Resources”, the working group explored 
international cooperation to promote greater access and 
affordability of foundational AI resources, particularly for the 

156 | P a g e 
 
Global South, including practical ways of pooling AI infrastructure, 
supporting equitable development of AI ecosystems, and 
encouraging stakeholders to support access to and affordability of 
foundation models and infrastructure. Its deliverable Charter for 
the Democratic Diffusion of AI has been endorsed by 24 countries 
and IO’s. 
 The “Inclusion for Social Empowerment” working group discussed 
strategies and frameworks for AI systems that advance social 
empowerment, successful AI-driven interventions for social 
inclusion, critical enablers and barriers to scaling such solutions 
across regions and populations, and institutional or multilateral 
platforms for sharing learning and coordinating efforts. Its 
deliverable Alliance for Advancing Inclusion Through AI has been 
endorsed by 21 countries and IO’s. 
 Under “Resilience, Innovation and Efficiency”, the working group 
examined enhancing technical efficiency, adaptability and reliability 
in AI system development, successful R&D efforts on resilient and 
resource-conscious AI systems, capacity building and institutional 
readiness for resource-efficient AI solutions, and knowledge-
sharing platforms and partnerships for resilient, efficient AI.  Its 
deliverable Voluntary Guiding Principles on Resilient, Innovative, 
and Efficient AI has been endorsed by 22 countries and IO’s. 
 
III. KEY OUTCOMES OF THE SUMMIT 
6.13 The India AI Impact Summit 2026 marked a significant milestone 
in global AI cooperation, positioning India as a key player in advancing 
inclusive, development-oriented, and impact-focused AI governance. The 
key outcomes and achievements of the Summit are given below: 
1. Broad based global participation : The Summit witnessed 
broad-based global participation, with delegations from 100+ countries 
and international organisations, along with industry, academia, 
startups, and civil society, reflecting an inclusive and multi-stakeholder 
approach.  

157 | P a g e 
 
 
2. New Delhi Declaration on Impact Summit : A key outcome 
was the adoption of the AI Impact Summit Declaration, endorsed by 92 
countries and international organisations, marking a shift in global 
discourse from a risk-centric approach to an impact- and development-
oriented approach to AI governance. Guided by the principle of 
“Sarvajan Hitaya, Sarvajan Sukhaya” (Welfare for all, Happiness for all), 
the Declaration underscored that AI’s benefits must be equitably shared 
across humanity and emphasises strengthening international 
cooperation and multi-stakeholder engagement, respecting national 
sovereignty, and advancing AI through accessible and trustworthy 
frameworks.  
3. The Summit produced a comprehensive set of policy and 
technical deliverables through seven thematic Working Groups and 
Expert Engagement Groups, covering key areas such as: 
 Human capital and workforce transitions, including the Equitable 
AI Transitions Playbook and Voluntary Guiding Principles for 
Skilling and Reskilling, providing actionable frameworks for 
managing AI-driven labour shifts.  
 Trusted and responsible AI systems, including frameworks for AI 
safety, governance, and child protection, and the Guidance Note 
on AI Governance, endorsed by multiple countries.  
 Sustainable and resilient AI infrastructure, including the Playbook 
on Advancing Resilient AI Infrastructure and guiding principles on 
efficient and resource-conscious AI systems.  
 Data governance frameworks, including the New Deal for Data, 
focusing on accountability-based approaches to data governance 
rather than restrictive data access.  
 Inclusion and scientific collaboration, including the Charter for the 
Democratic Diffusion of AI and creation of institutional networks 
for AI for science and social inclusion.  
 
4. New Delhi Frontier AI Impact Commitments : At the 
inaugural ceremony of the Summit 2026, Minister , MeitY announced the 
New Delhi Frontier AI Impact Commitments. These voluntary 

158 | P a g e 
 
Commitments reflect a shared vision to ensure that development and 
deployment of frontier AI systems are aligned with equity, cultural 
diversity and real-world needs, particularly across the Global South. 
The first commitment, “Advancing Understanding of Real-World AI 
Usage”, focuses on generating anonymised and aggregated insights on 
AI usage to support policymaking on jobs, skills, productivity and 
economic transformation. The second commitment, “Strengthening 
Multilingual and Contextual Evaluations”, centres on improving the 
effectiveness of AI systems across languages, cultures and real-world 
use cases by developing datasets, benchmarks and expertise for under-
represented languages and contexts in collaboration with governments 
and local ecosystems. These commitments are intended to democratize 
AI access and innovation and to help shape “an AI future of the 
humans, by the humans, and for the humans”. 
 
5. Private sector commitments : The Summit also formalised 
private sector commitments, with leading AI companies agreeing to:  
 advance real-world AI usage analysis and data sharing  
 strengthen multilingual evaluation and benchmarking for low-
resource contexts, supporting Global South needs.  
The Summit catalysed large-scale investments and infrastructure 
expansion, with over USD 200 billion in AI-related commitments across 
compute, data centres, and AI innovation ecosystems.  
6. AI for social good: A major outcome was the emphasis on AI for 
social good, with the AI Impact Expo showcasing 300+ real-world AI 
solutions across sectors such as healthcare, agriculture, logistics, and 
climate.  
7. It also strengthened India’s position in global AI supply chains and 
infrastructure partnerships, including collaboration initiatives aimed at 
securing semiconductors, critical minerals, and AI infrastructure 
ecosystems.  
6.14.  Other key outcomes include the following: 
 Release of the Equitable AI Transition Playbook in partnership with 
the International Labour Organisation (ILO)  

159 | P a g e 
 
 Signing of the Voluntary Guiding Principles for Resilient, Innovative 
& Efficient AI, endorsed by 22+ Countries 
 Signing of the Voluntary Guiding Principles for Reskilling in the Age 
of AI, endorsed by 25 Countries & IOs 
 Charter for the Democratic Diffusion of AI, including the MAITRI 
platform, released with support from 24 countries as well as major 
international organisations such as the Asian Development Bank, 
World Bank, Rockefeller Foundation, Gates Foundation 
 Release of the Playbook for Advancing Resilient AI Infrastructure 
 Release of Guidance Note on AI Governance, endorsed by 23 
countries  
 Release of Framework for Trusted AI Commons with 23 partner 
countries & IOs prior to launch 
 Alliance for Advancing Inclusion Through AI announced, endorsed 
by 21 Countries & UNICEF  
 Network of AI For Science Institutions announced, 20 partner 
countries at launch 
 Launch of the Resilient AI Challenge in partnership with France & 
UNESCO 
 AI Responsibility Campaign – Global Record: India successfully 
achieved a Guinness World Record for the “Most pledges received 
for an AI responsibility campaign in 24 hours,” with over 2.5 lakh 
validated pledges. 
 The Summit also unveiled six global AI casebooks across health, 
energy, gender empowerment, agriculture, education, and 
accessibility, launched the AI Impact Startup Book, implemented 
zero-waste guidelines at Bharat Mandapam, and demonstrated 
digital-first execution with 80% of food court transactions 
conducted via UPI with zero commission. 
 India also joined Pax Silica at India AI Impact Summit 2026 
 
6.15 Overall, the Summit positioned India as a bridge between 
developed and developing countries, shaping a global AI agenda focused 
on inclusion, real-world deployment, and development outcomes rather 
than purely regulatory considerations. 
 

160 | P a g e 
 
6.16 The Committee were also keen to know the achievements of the 
Summit from the perspective of the Global South. In a written reply, the 
Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology submitted that from 
the perspective of the Global South, the India AI Impact Summit 2026 
delivered concrete and actionable outcomes across governance, access 
to resources, capacity building, and inclusive participation. 
 The Summit secured adoption of the AI Impact Summit 
Declaration by 92+ countries, reflecting broad consensus on 
human-centric, development-oriented AI, and shifting global 
discourse from a risk-only approach to an impact-driven approach 
more aligned with developing country priorities.  
 It produced practical policy tools and frameworks through Working 
Groups, including: 
o Equitable AI Transitions Playbook and skilling principles, 
providing governments with actionable guidance on 
managing workforce transitions due to AI.  
o Guidance Note on AI Governance, offering a flexible 
framework for building national AI governance systems, 
particularly useful for countries at different stages of 
readiness.  
o New Deal for Data, which promotes an accountability-based 
approach to data governance, enabling the use of data while 
ensuring safeguards against potential harms, particularly for 
countries balancing innovation with regulation. 
o Playbook on Resilient AI Infrastructure, providing guidance 
on sustainable and resource-efficient AI systems, relevant for 
countries with infrastructure constraints.  
 The Summit advanced the concept of shared AI resources, 
including frameworks such as the Trusted AI Commons, enabling 
knowledge sharing, datasets, and tools that can be accessed by a 
wider set of countries.  
 It enabled private sector commitments focused on Global South 
needs, including efforts to:  
o develop multilingual AI systems  

161 | P a g e 
 
o improve benchmarking for low-resource languages  
o generate data on real-world AI usage to support 
policymaking.  
 The Summit showcased deployable AI solutions for development, 
with 300+ real-world applications and 170+ use cases across 
sectors such as healthcare, agriculture, education, and financial 
inclusion, demonstrating practical pathways for adoption.  
 It catalysed large-scale investments in AI infrastructure, including 
compute, data centres, and innovation ecosystems, which are 
expected to improve access to AI capabilities over time.  
 A key achievement was the democratisation of participation, with 
engagement from 100+ countries, 6 lakh participants, and 
extensive youth and civil society involvement, ensuring that AI 
governance discussions were not limited to a few advanced 
economies.  
Overall, the Summit enabled the Global South to move from being rule-
takers to active contributors, by shaping governance frameworks, 
accessing shared resources, and participating in global AI discourse. 
 
6.17 On the Committee’s further query about the measures being taken 
to monitor or track the implementation of various resolutions taken 
during the recently concluded AI Summit, the Ministry stated that India 
is adopting a structured, multi-layered approach to monitor and track 
the implementation of outcomes from the IndiaAI Impact Summit 2026, 
focusing on institutional mechanisms, periodic reporting, and multi-
stakeholder engagement. 
 The Summit outcomes are being taken forward through the 
respective Working Groups and Expert Engagement Groups, which 
continue to act as implementation and review platforms for the 
deliverables, including playbooks, frameworks, and guiding 
principles.  
 Many of the Summit outputs, such as playbooks and voluntary 
guiding principles, include built-in monitoring approaches, enabling 

162 | P a g e 
 
countries and stakeholders to track adoption, share progress, and 
exchange best practices.  
 A system of periodic progress reviews is being envisaged, with 
updates to be shared through international forums and follow-up 
engagements, ensuring continuity between successive global AI 
summits.  
 The Summit has also initiated data-driven tracking mechanisms, 
including commitments by participating stakeholders to publish 
insights on real-world AI usage, adoption trends, and impact 
assessment, supporting evidence-based policymaking.  
 Institutional mechanisms such as the IndiaAI Mission are being 
leveraged to operationalise and monitor domestic implementation, 
including tracking progress on:  
o AI infrastructure (compute and data platforms)  
o development of foundation models  
o AI applications across sectors  
o skilling and capacity-building initiatives  
 Continued engagement through global and regional platforms will 
enable monitoring of progress on shared priorities such as 
responsible AI, inclusion, and access to resources, while also 
facilitating course correction where required.  
 Multi-stakeholder participation, including governments, industry, 
academia, and civil society, ensures distributed accountability and 
ongoing feedback, strengthening implementation over time.  
 Overall, the approach combines institutional follow-up, voluntary 
reporting, technical monitoring, and international cooperation, 
ensuring that Summit outcomes translate into measurable and 
sustained impact. 
 
Pax Silica 
6.18 Pax Silica is a U.S.-led international initiative launched in December 
2025 aimed at building secure, resilient, and innovation-driven supply 
chains for technologies foundational to the artificial intelligence (AI) era, 

163 | P a g e 
 
especially silicon and critical minerals that underpin chips, advanced 
computing, and other high-tech systems. The Pax Silica Summit was 
held in Washington, D.C. on 12 December 2025, where partner nations 
signed the Pax Silica Declaration. The declaration lays out a shared 
vision of deep economic and technology cooperation across supply 
chains, from raw materials through semiconductors and AI 
infrastructure, and a commitment to mutual prosperity and security. 
6.19 On 20 February 2026, at a special event held on the margins of 
the AI Impact Summit in New Delhi, India joined the Pax Silica initiative 
by becoming a signatory to the Pax Silica Declaration. India also signed 
a Joint Statement on the "India-U.S. AI Opportunity Partnership” as a 
bilateral addendum to the Declaration. Pax Silica seeks to build secure, 
resilient, and innovation-driven supply chains for technologies 
foundational to the AI era, particularly silicon and critical minerals that 
underpin semiconductors, advanced computing, and other high-
technology systems. 
6.20 The documents were signed by Secretary, Ministry of Electronics 
and Information Technology, Shri S. Krishnan; U.S. Ambassador to India, 
Mr. Sergio Gor; and U.S. Under Secretary of State for Economic Growth, 
Energy and the Environment, Mr. Jacob Helberg. The signing took place 
in the presence of Hon’ble Minister for Railways, Information & 
Broadcasting, and Electronics & Information Technology, Shri Ashwini 
Vaishnaw, and Director of the U.S. Office of Science and Technology 
Policy, Mr. Michael Kratsios. In his keynote remarks, Minister of 
Electronics & Information Technology highlighted the strong potential for 
India and the United States to collaborate on supply chain security and 
emphasised that cooperation under Pax Silica would further deepen 
engagement on critical technologies and supply chain resilience under 
the India-U.S. Comprehensive Global Strategic Partnership. 
6.21 When asked about the implications of signing of Pax Silica, during 
the evidence held on 18 March 2026, Secretary MeitY made the 
following submission: 
“You mentioned Pax Silica. The agreement was duly signed, and 
on behalf of the Government of India, I signed the agreement. 

164 | P a g e 
 
Fundamentally, it will support us in both semiconductors and rare 
earths, which are two critical areas. It is also a continuation of the 
earlier support that we had received from previous administrations 
under the trust framework, and prior to that, the ISET framework. 
It is a continuation of the earlier support that we had from 
previous administrations under the trust framework, and prior to 
that the ISET framework. So, the possibility of us collaborating 
with the United States companies and more generally on 
advancing our advanced technologies, particularly in the 
semiconductor space, for which we also need access to critical 
minerals, is going to be significant there.” 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

165 | P a g e 
 
PART-II 
OBSERVATIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS BY THE COMMITTEE 
Recommendation No. 1 
 Technology Partnerships as a core component of Foreign Policy 
The Committee note that India has emerged as a 
significant technology power with tech diplomacy as a core 
priority. India stands at a significant juncture in the evolution 
of its technology diplomacy, as technology and foreign policy 
increasingly intersect. India possesses genuine and growing 
capability across digital Public Infrastructure, space technology 
and Artificial intelligence, and has demonstrated an ability to 
convene international coalitions, as seen in the G20 Digital 
Economy Working Group and the India AI Impact Summit. 
India's technology partnerships are spread across numerous 
initiatives such as India-US TRUST, the India-UK Technology 
Security Initiative, the India-EU Trade and Technology Council, 
and others, each significant in its own right. In addition, 
domestically, flagship initiatives such as Digital India, the 
IndiaAI Mission, the Semiconductor Mission, Indian Space 
Policy 2023 etc., provide the policy backbone for India’s 
external engagements in these domains. The Committee note 
from the Ministry's submission that India's technology 
diplomacy supports the Viksit Bharat 2047 vision by linking 
such domestic technology missions with international 
partnerships, with the Ministry facilitating these engagements 
and coordinating global cooperation. At the same time, the 

166 | P a g e 
 
global technology landscape remains intensely competitive. 
India's dependence on foreign partners for hardware, advanced 
components, and frontier AI models, exposes it to diplomatic 
and strategic risks, arising from the export control and 
regulatory decisions of other countries.  
Recognising that India stands at an inflection point in its 
technology diplomacy, the Committee commend the Ministry of 
External Affairs for being at the centre of India's response to 
this shift and spearheading India’s tech diplomacy alongside 
other line Ministries. The Committee recommend that the 
Ministry sustain this momentum and treat technology 
partnerships as a core, first-order component of its bilateral 
and multilateral engagement strategy, with technology 
considerations integrated into country and regional desk 
assessments rather than handled as a stand-alone technical 
track. This recognition should be reflected in the mandate, 
staffing and resource allocation of the Ministry, so that 
technology diplomacy is treated not as an auxiliary function but 
as central to the Ministry's core purpose. The Committee 
further recommend that MEA, in coordination with the Ministry 
of Electronics and Information Technology, Ministry of 
Commerce and Industry and the Department of Science and 
Technology ensure that India’s technology diplomacy is 
explicitly oriented towards reducing the country’s dependence 
on a narrow set of foreign countries for critical hardware, 
advanced components and frontier AI models, and not solely 
towards deepening engagement with existing partners.  

167 | P a g e 
 
Recommendation No. 2 
Need for a Vision Document and National Strategy 
The Committee note that technology has emerged as a 
central pillar of diplomacy, governance, national security and 
economic growth. India has consciously integrated technology 
into its foreign policy priorities with a particular emphasis on 
'three Ts' framework of Trade, Technology and Tourism. India's 
strategy in the global technology domain is defined by a 
commitment to harness strategic technologies while 
simultaneously building its role as a trusted global partner. This 
approach is institutionalised through bilateral, plurilateral, and 
multilateral frameworks focusing on the interface of 
technology, trade and security. In reply to a direct query on 
whether India has a clearly articulated strategy linking digital 
governance, AI, cyber issues and space diplomacy within its 
foreign policy, the Ministry submitted that “there is no single, 
stand-alone policy document that consolidates all these 
strands”, and that strategic direction is instead “reflected 
across sectoral missions, inter-ministerial coordination 
mechanisms and India's positions in key bilateral, plurilateral 
and multilateral engagements”. While the Committee take note 
of the Ministry’s submission that India is progressively 
developing an integrated approach towards tech diplomacy, the 
Committee are surprised to find that no such articulated 
strategy presently exists.   

168 | P a g e 
 
The Committee are of the considered view that this 
approach is fragmentary, piecemeal and ad hoc, and falls short 
of what an arena as significant and fast-evolving as technology 
diplomacy demands. A collection of well-conceived individual 
initiatives, however successful in isolation, cannot substitute 
for an articulated national vision, nor can tactical adaptability 
substitute for strategy. The Committee, therefore, recommend 
that the Ministry, in consultation with the concerned Ministries 
including MeitY, should actively reach out to all stakeholders, 
including academia, research institutions and industry, and 
initiate consultations on a Technology Vision and Strategy 
Document that brings together India's objectives, priorities, 
sequencing and institutional responsibilities across digital 
governance, artificial intelligence, cyber issues, space and 
emerging technologies within a coherent framework. In 
addition, the Ministry of External Affairs should also prepare a 
complementary Technology Diplomacy Strategy. The 
Committee are of the firm view that unless the Ministry moves 
in this direction, India’s claim to be regarded as a credible and 
consequential player in the global technological arena will fail 
to seize the opportunities that this moment presents. The 
Document as well as the Strategy should also be placed in the 
public domain and reviewed periodically to keep pace with 
technological change.  
 
 

169 | P a g e 
 
Recommendation No. 3 
Inter-Ministerial Coordination 
The Committee note that tech diplomacy is inherently a 
multi-stakeholder domain within the Government. While the 
Ministry of External Affairs acts as the nodal Ministry, MeitY, 
the Department of Science and Technology, the Department of 
Space, the Department of Telecommunications and the Ministry 
of Commerce and Industry, among others, each carry 
substantial responsibilities of their own. All these line Ministries 
are nodal for their sector specific technology in their domain 
with MEA participating as and when required. Within the 
Ministry of External Affairs itself, the New, Emerging and 
Strategic Technologies (NEST) Division, Cyber Diplomacy and 
Disarmament and International Security Affairs (DISA) Division 
deals with the issues concerning tech diplomacy. The 
Committee further note from the reply of the Ministry that 
inter-ministerial coordination is “carried out through existing 
mechanisms within the Government, with each Ministry having 
a sui generis process for coordination and consultation”. 
The Committee are of the view that reliance on 
improvised, Ministry-specific coordination arrangements will 
prove inadequate as technology's role in India's foreign policy 
continues to expand, and as the number of stakeholder 
Ministries grows correspondingly. The Committee, therefore, 
recommend that the Government should establish a formally 
constituted Inter-Ministerial Coordination Mechanism on 
Technology Diplomacy, chaired by MEA, with defined 

170 | P a g e 
 
participation from MeitY, DST, the Department of Space, and 
the Ministry of Commerce and Industry. This body should meet 
at a specified periodicity, so that coordination does not get 
limited to case-by-case arrangements but is institutionalised 
within a broader framework where activities can be monitored 
and followed up. 
Recommendation No. 4 
States Division and Sub-national Engagement 
The Committee note that a considerable share of 
technology related investment and activities occurs at the state 
level. The States Division within MEA has been assigned the 
additional responsibility of technology facilitation and 
coordination with the State Governments. The Division 
operates with certain officers placed on lien to State 
Governments through Branch Secretariat Offices and Regional 
Passport Offices. The Committee are constrained to observe 
that passport and consular work is a distinct function, calling 
for a different skill set from technology facilitation. The 
Committee further observe that the evidence placed before it 
on the States Division was general and descriptive in nature, 
and did not identify specific technology-related outcomes 
facilitated by the Division or the number of States actively 
covered.  
On the basis of the evidences and replies furnished by the 
Ministries, the Committee are constrained to observe that the 
States Division, in its current form, does not yet appear to be an 

171 | P a g e 
 
effective vehicle for coordination with State Governments on 
technology diplomacy. The Committee, therefore, recommend 
that the Ministry should undertake, as a matter of priority, a 
candid review of the functioning of the States Division. The 
Division should be resourced with officers possessing the 
relevant expertise, under a clearly defined mandate for 
technology facilitation, rather than absorbing this responsibility 
into an already-stretched Division staffed for an unrelated 
function. It should also engage actively and continuously with 
State Governments to identify and facilitate technology 
partnerships and investment opportunities, rather than 
functioning as a passive point of contact. The Ministry should 
report back to the Committee with the States currently and 
actively engaged, the specific technology outcomes achieved to 
date, an objective assessment of staffing adequacy, and a 
concrete, time-bound plan to render the Division pragmatically 
effective. 
Recommendation No. 5 
Engagement with the Private Sector, Industry and Research 
Institutions 
The Committee acknowledge the valuable evidence given 
by Nasscom and Krutrim on the contribution of India's 
technology services industry to the country's global standing. 
Nasscom 's initiatives, such as the Nasscom U.S. CEO Forum, its 
evidence-based advocacy on the industry's contribution to the 
US economy, and its India-U.S. startup partnership 

172 | P a g e 
 
programmes, illustrate the role industry bodies can play as 
active participants in technology diplomacy. The Committee 
also note the Ministry's acknowledgement, in the context of 
domestic reform, of "weak industry-academia links" as a 
continuing constraint on India's innovation ecosystem. 
The Committee are of the opinion that India's tech 
diplomacy stands to gain considerably from deeper and more 
sustained collaboration with private industry and research 
institutions, drawing on the example of developed countries 
where industry bodies play a leading role in shaping and 
advancing technology diplomacy. The private sector's 
contribution extends well beyond diplomacy: it fuels economic 
growth, generates employment, retains skilled talent that 
might otherwise seek opportunities abroad, builds skilling 
capacity, mobilizes capital, and supports startups and deep-
tech ventures at every stage of their growth. The Government 
should build on the good work already being done by industry 
bodies such as NASSCOM, rather than treating industry 
engagement as incidental to the process. The Committee, 
therefore, recommend that MEA along with MeitY should create 
an enabling environment for industry bodies and research 
institutions to participate more closely in technology diplomacy, 
including through structured, regular consultation and dialogue 
mechanisms that feed directly into the formulation of India's 
technology partnerships. Such collaboration should extend 
beyond the IT services industry to deep-tech, semiconductor, 
quantum, robotics and AI companies, as well as premier 

173 | P a g e 
 
research institutions and universities. The Committee further 
recommend strengthening of the existing mechanisms within 
MEA, including its NEST and Cyber Diplomacy Divisions, to 
facilitate structured and regular engagement with industry 
bodies and research institutions, in coordination with MeitY and 
other concerned Ministries. The need for a standing Technology 
Diplomacy Council, comprising senior representatives of 
Government, industry bodies and research institutions, should 
also be explored upon after assessment of the effectiveness 
and limitations of existing arrangements.  
Recommendation No. 6 
Fostering Industry, Academia, State Government and R&D 
Linkages 
The Ministry set out a range of domestic reforms it 
considers necessary to ensure that gains from technology 
diplomacy translate into concrete outcomes. These include the 
Anusandhan National Research Foundation's (ANRF) target of 
raising public and private R&D spending to 2% of GDP by 2030, 
the revitalization of the Technology Development Board to 
bridge laboratory research and commercial deployment, and 
the 'ease of doing research' package intended to reduce 
bureaucratic delay. The Ministry stated that these reforms are 
directed at overcoming domestic bottlenecks such as slow 
approvals, fragmented funding, weak industry-academia links, 
talent attrition and uncertain regulation, so that high-level 
technology diplomacy translates into industrial and 

174 | P a g e 
 
technological engagement on the ground. The Committee 
consider that these reforms, together with the engagement of 
State Governments, industry, and academic and research 
institutions as equal partners, together define the domestic 
ecosystem on which the ultimate success of India's technology 
diplomacy depends. 
Technology diplomacy and domestic innovation 
capacity are, in the Committee's assessment, two sides of the 
same coin. Diplomatic gains secured abroad will yield limited 
benefit if India's domestic R&D base, industry, academic 
institutions and States are not equally encouraged and 
strengthened. India's current R&D spending remains well below 
the levels sustained by leading technology nations, and the 
Committee are of the view that closing this gap cannot be left 
to incremental progress alone. The Committee, therefore, 
recommend that MEA take up the need of enhanced domestic 
R&D investment with the concerned Ministries and provide for 
a dedicated allocation within its budget for technology 
diplomacy. The Ministry may apprise the Committee about the 
concrete steps taken in this direction. The Committee further 
recommend that MEA work with the concerned Ministries and 
industry bodies to develop targeted incentives and career 
pathways for skilled Indians abroad in the United States and 
other countries who wish to return because of persistent visa 
insecurity or employment difficulties. This should go beyond 
diaspora engagement to offer concrete opportunities and 
relocation support for their return to India.  

175 | P a g e 
 
Recommendation No. 7 
Focus on Innovation and Skilling 
The Committee note that the Government is taking a 
suite of structural reforms in R&D, regulation and innovation 
governance to overcome domestic bottlenecks. However, 
unless an enabling environment is created through infusion of 
capital funding of the industry, the tech sector cannot grow and 
flourish. The Committee are of the view that India's talent in 
the field of information technology is globally recognised and 
the Government must focus intensely on creating an enabling 
environment for innovation and start-ups, so that this talent 
finds an industry and ecosystem within India in which it can be 
suitably utilised, rather than seeking opportunity abroad. 
Skilling must be treated as an integral part of this effort, with 
large-scale, industry-aligned skilling and reskilling programmes 
in critical and emerging technologies, developed jointly with 
industry and academic institutions. The Committee recommend 
that MEA, in coordination with, concerned Ministries, industry, 
academic/ research institutions, and State Governments should 
prioritise research collaborations, start-up linkages and skilling 
opportunities in technology partnerships with other countries 
so that international engagement and domestic capacity-
building reinforce one another.  
 
 
 

176 | P a g e 
 
Recommendation No. 8 
Capacity and Capacity-Building 
The Committee note the Ministry's candid submission 
about the capacity gaps within the diplomatic corps on AI and 
emerging technologies. It described the challenge not as a 
static shortfall but as a ‘dynamic need’ to continuously build, 
update and retain specialised expertise. The Committee also 
note that the Ministry has taken a range of measures to address 
this gap, such as the creation of the NEST and Cyber Diplomacy 
Divisions, incorporation of training modules on AI, technology 
diplomacy and cybersecurity within the Sushma Swaraj 
Institute of Foreign Service (SSIFS), and the deployment of 
dedicated technology officers in a few Indian Missions. This 
leaves the majority of India's diplomatic Missions, including in 
several partner countries with which India maintains active 
technology engagement, without dedicated technological 
expertise on the ground.  
The Committee are concerned to note that in spite of the 
above measures, there are critical gaps in the capacity of 
India’s diplomatic corps with respect to emerging technologies 
such as AI, cybersecurity etc. Capacity-building, in the 
Committee's assessment, is not a peripheral administrative 
matter but a strategic necessity, particularly in such a technical 
and niche area. Without adequate expertise at the negotiating 
table, India risks losing opportunities it could otherwise have 
seized. The Committee, therefore, exhort MEA to undertake a 
time-bound, needs-based expansion of dedicated technology-

177 | P a g e 
 
diplomacy positions across all Missions in India's principal 
current and prospective technology-partner countries. The 
Ministry should also substantially scale up specialised 
technology modules within SSIFS's foundational and mid-
career training. In addition, a mechanism should be instituted 
for periodic lateral exposure of diplomatic officers to industry 
and research institutions. The Committee further recommend 
that MEA should promote practical use of appropriate AI tools 
in its work to improve institutional effectiveness and deepen 
diplomat’s understanding of technology, with safeguards for 
sensitive information and human oversight.  
Recommendation No. 9 
Leveraging the Diaspora and PRABHAS 
The Committee are cognisant of the India's vast and well-
resourced Diaspora can play in advancing the country's 
technology diplomacy. In this regard, the Ministry has apprised 
the Committee of the PRABHAS (Pravasi Bhartiya Academic and 
Scientific Sampark) platform, developed jointly by DST, CSIR 
and MEA, which enables members of the Indian scientific and 
academic diaspora to register their expertise and contribute to 
India's education, science and technology programmes without 
needing to relocate. The Ministry characterised PRABHAS as ‘a 
very vibrant platform’ that has generated genuine interest. The 
Committee, however, observe that the evidence on outreach 
and engagement was confined almost entirely to the diaspora 
in the United States, with no comparable account offered of 

178 | P a g e 
 
outreach in other major technology hubs, where substantial 
Indian diaspora communities are also present. 
The Committee are of the view that India's diaspora is far 
too consequential an asset to be engaged through a single 
platform alone. Diaspora professionals in senior positions at 
global technology firms can act as informal bridges of trust 
between India and host countries, facilitating investment, 
technology transfer and venture capital flows into India. They 
can mentor Indian startups, join trade and innovation missions, 
and advocate for constructive positions within host-country 
policy and industry circles, much as Nasscom 's own evidence 
described. They can equally serve as a talent pipeline, drawing 
skilled professionals abroad into research collaboration with 
Indian institutions, even without relocation. More than a million 
diaspora in the United States alone, and comparably significant 
Indian technology diaspora communities in the United 
Kingdom, the European Union, Canada, Australia, Singapore 
and the Gulf, together represent a diplomatic and technological 
asset of considerable stature that remains substantially 
untapped and under-leveraged. Such standing warrants active 
acknowledgement and sustained outreach on the part of the 
Government, rather than a one-way, registration-based 
engagement. The Committee, therefore, recommend that the 
Ministry should undertake a structured, well-publicised 
outreach and awareness campaign for PRABHAS across all 
major overseas Indian technology and scientific communities, 
and not the United States alone. Such outreach should include 

179 | P a g e 
 
regular mechanisms to seek and incorporate the diaspora's own 
feedback and suggestions, so that engagement runs in both 
directions. The Ministry should also explore extending the 
diaspora engagement model beyond PRABHAS to cover the 
wider forms of contribution described above, set measurable 
registration and engagement targets, and report annually to 
the Committee on diaspora participation and its tangible 
contribution to India's technology, innovation and research 
programmes. 
Recommendation No. 10 
Diversification across Bilateral and Multilateral Partnerships 
The Committee note that there has been considerable 
range and diversity of India's technology partnerships. These 
include the Transforming Relationship Utilising Strategic 
Technology (TRUST) Initiative with the United States, the Trade 
and Technology Council (TTC) with the European Union, and the 
Technology Security Initiative (TSI) with the United Kingdom, 
alongside expanding engagement with Russia, Japan, Germany, 
the Netherlands, the Republic of Korea, Australia, Israel and 
numerous other countries. India is also an active multilateral 
participant, engaging through the Group of Twenty (G20), the 
grouping of Brazil, Russia, India, China and South Africa 
(BRICS), the Quadrilateral Security Dialogue (Quad), the Indo-
Pacific Economic Framework for Prosperity (IPEF), and the 
United Nations. The Committee also note NASSCOM's evidence 
that the United States and the United Kingdom together 

180 | P a g e 
 
account for 75 to 80 per cent of the Indian technology services 
industry's overall business, and that diversification into the 
Middle East, Sri Lanka and parts of Africa remains at a 
comparatively early, largely industry-led stage. 
The Committee concur with the view expressed by experts 
during evidence that continued over-dependence on a narrow 
set of partner countries represents a strategic vulnerability. 
External diversification should be treated as an explicit 
objective of technology diplomacy, and not left as a matter for 
industry initiative alone. The Committee, therefore, recommend 
that Missions in under-engaged regions, in coordination with 
the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, be directed to actively 
cultivate technology partnerships. The Government should also 
set measurable diversification targets. Together, these steps 
will ensure that the breadth of India's formal partnerships is 
matched by a genuine broadening of its technological and 
commercial dependencies. 
Recommendation No. 11 
Bilateral Engagement: From Signing to Delivery 
The Committee note that India is actively engaged with 
key partner, including the United States, the United Kingdom, 
the European Union, Japan, Singapore, etc to build trusted 
technology partnerships that serve strategic and economic 
objectives. The Committee take note of the substantial volume 
of bilateral activity placed on record. This includes considerable 
number of active MoUs, numerous Joint Working Groups, and a 

181 | P a g e 
 
wide array of work-streams under TRUST initiative, the Trade 
and Technology Council (TTC) and the Technology Security 
Initiative (TSI). These range from the AI infrastructure, the 
pharmaceutical supply chain, Strategic Mineral Recovery 
Initiative to the Advanced Materials R&D, commercial space, 
quantum, among others. The Committee, however, find that 
bilateral engagements consist predominantly of meetings 
convened, dialogues held and instruments signed. There is 
comparatively limited information on the concrete outcomes 
these engagements have actually delivered such as investments 
realised, technology transferred, or products and services 
delivered. 
The Committee note that the proliferation of technology 
dialogues and partnerships, while a welcome sign of diplomatic 
activity, carries a risk of announcements outpacing 
implementation. The Committee are of the view that MEA must 
institute a systematic mechanism to track deliverables agreed 
under each partnership and to report on their status, so that 
technology diplomacy translates into tangible outcomes for 
India's strategic and economic interests. The Committee 
recommend that the Ministry of External Affairs maintains and 
periodically update, for the Committee's review, a consolidated 
register of all bilateral and minilateral technology partnerships, 
including specific commitments made, their implementation 
status, and the diplomatic and strategic value derived from 
each. 

182 | P a g e 
 
The Committee sincerely desire that India's bilateral 
technology engagement be made explicitly outcome-oriented. 
Towards this end, the Committee recommend that each major 
MoU signed and workstream agreed should specify measurable 
deliverables and timelines at the point of signing. Outcomes 
should be tracked against defined indicators, such as 
technologies co-developed, joint ventures established, 
personnel trained or exchanged, and patents or publications 
resulting from the collaboration. 
Recommendation No. 12 
Technology Talent Mobility  
The Committee note Nasscom 's testimony before it 
that drew a sharp distinction between the perception and the 
reality of Indian technology talent mobility to the United 
States. Nasscom 's evidence showed that the Indian technology 
industry directly generated $103 billion of revenue and directly 
employed 207,000 people in the US in 2021, with its overall 
economic footprint contributing $198 billion to US GDP and 
supporting 1.6 million American jobs that year. Nasscom stated 
that this contribution is often overshadowed by a public 
narrative that frames Indian IT professionals and H-1B visa 
holders as displacing American workers, and that the industry 
has had to commission independent, data-backed research 
merely to correct this misperception. The Committee observe 
that the mobility of skilled technology professionals, 
particularly under the H-1B and equivalent visa categories, 

183 | P a g e 
 
remains vulnerable to being caught up in domestic immigration 
politics in partner countries, even though such mobility is, in 
substance, a technology and economic cooperation issue rather 
than a general immigration matter. 
The Committee are of the view that the continued 
conflation of skilled technology talent mobility with broader 
immigration policy debates works against India's interests, and 
indeed against the economic interests of host countries, as 
NASSCOM's own evidence demonstrates. Talent mobility of this 
kind underpins the very partnerships, such as TRUST, the TTC 
and the TSI, that the Ministry has invested considerable 
diplomatic effort in building, and cannot be treated as a 
peripheral irritant. The Committee, therefore, recommend that 
the Ministry of External Affairs should make systematic use of 
credible and updated evidence on Indian technology 
professionals and companies’ contributions to the economies 
US, UK and other partner countries in bilateral engagements. 
The Ministry may apprise the Committee of how findings by 
trade associations such as MASSCOM have informed its 
advocacy and the steps proposed to strengthen these efforts.  
Recommendation No. 13 
Multilateral Engagement: India as an Agenda Shaper 
The Committee note that in addition to bilateral 
engagements, India has been engaging with various 
multilateral organisations for promotion of technology. These 
include international organisations such as G20, United 

184 | P a g e 
 
Nations, World Trade Organisation (WTO), World Bank, BRICS, 
Quad etc. The Committee note with appreciation that during 
India's own G20 Presidency in 2023, India placed Digital Public 
Infrastructure at the heart of the global development agenda 
and secured its endorsement in the New Delhi Leaders' 
Declaration as a clear instance of India setting the multilateral 
agenda rather than merely responding to it. The Committee 
also note the Ministry's submission during evidence on India's 
continued engagement and India’s leadership role in various 
fora, including its advocacy for inclusive and development-
oriented AI governance at the Global Digital Compact process, 
its role as co-chair of the Paris AI Action Summit, and its 
hosting of the AI Impact Summit in February 2026. 
The Committee put on record their appreciation for all the 
multilateral and plurilateral engagements, where India has not 
only participated but has emphatically emerged as a 
technological powerhouse. At the same time, the Committee 
are of the view that India’s demonstrated capacity to shape the 
multilateral technology agenda, as shown during its G20 
Presidency, should, in the Committee's assessment, be built 
upon and reinforced through a more coherent and sustained 
diplomatic exercise across all fora in which India participates, 
and not treated as a singular achievement tied to a particular 
Presidency. India is well placed to play this role. As a large, 
democratic country with immense technological capabilities 
and a genuine record of building inclusive digital public goods, 
India carries a natural credibility that few other nations 

185 | P a g e 
 
command in these debates. The Committee, therefore, 
recommend that the Government should consolidate this 
credibility into a consistent, agenda-shaping presence in global 
technology governance, advancing India's own priorities across 
multilateral cycles rather than participating only where the 
agenda is set by others. In doing so, India should continue to 
be a voice for developing countries, championing a democratic, 
trusted and inclusive approach to technology that reflects the 
interests and aspirations of the Global South. The Committee 
further desire that India press for emerging global norms on AI, 
data governance and digital infrastructure to be shaped 
through the active engagement and participation of developing 
countries.  
Recommendation No. 14 
India as a Voice of the Global South 
The Committee note that India has consistently sought to 
position itself as the voice of the Global South, offering a model 
of technology partnership grounded in equity and shared 
benefit, in contrast to the more transactional or extractive 
engagement often associated with certain developed countries. 
India's standing as a leader of the Global South rests, to a great 
extent, on the credibility of its partnership with Africa, a 
continent that has emerged as a strategic space of growing 
consequence in global technology and resource competition, 
and one India cannot afford to cede to other powers. The 
Committee examined the Ministry's submission on India's 

186 | P a g e 
 
ongoing engagement with Africa, including the Framework for 
cooperation with Africa under the India-Africa Forum Summit 
(IAFS) and India's sharing of Digital Public Infrastructure and 
Digital Public Goods with African partners. During evidence, the 
Ministry highlighted that there exists a framework for 
cooperation with Africa in the context of the India-Africa Forum 
Summit and many MoUs have been signed on deployment of 
DPI and sharing of our experience on Digital Public Goods. 
However, when asked specifically when the next edition of the 
IAFS would be convened, the Ministry's response was that India 
"intend[s] to host the next edition of this Summit hopefully 
next year." The Committee observe that the date for the 
Summit has not been finalized, and the last edition of the IAFS 
was held more than a decade ago, representing a considerable 
and, in the Committee's view, unjustified delay in convening a 
Summit that is central to India's Africa technology engagement. 
The Committee also note the Ministry's acknowledgement that 
DPI-sharing and Digital Public Goods MoUs with African 
countries, while welcomed by partners such as Botswana and 
Angola, remain limited in number and scale. 
This positioning carries real weight only when it is 
matched by consistent, on-the-ground delivery, and Africa is 
where this test is most immediate. The Committee, in their 
Reports on Demands for Grants, have been repeatedly stressing 
the need to prioritise Africa in India's foreign policy. The 
Committee reiterate that view here. India's engagement with 
Africa must be rooted in genuine developmental diplomacy, 

187 | P a g e 
 
built on sustained partnership rather than episodic Summit-
level engagement, particularly in a region where India faces 
active and well-resourced strategic competition. The 
Committee, therefore, recommend that the Government should, 
without further delay, fix a date for the next India-Africa Forum 
Summit, and commit to a fixed, regular periodicity thereafter. 
Africa should be treated as a priority region for technology 
diplomacy, warranting sustained, high-level and proactive 
engagement between Summits, and not attention confined to 
Summit years alone. The Government should substantially scale 
up, with defined timelines, the number and funding of DPI-
sharing and Digital Public Goods MoUs with African partner 
countries, and report annually to the Committee on the 
implementation, and not merely the signing, of such 
commitments. 
Recommendation No 15 
India’s Digital Governance Model 
The Committee note that the Digital India programme, 
launched on 1 July 2015, was conceived with the vision of 
transforming India into a digitally empowered society and 
knowledge-based economy, through digital access, digital 
inclusion, digital empowerment and bridging the digital divide. 
Over the past decade, India has developed a suite of 
foundational digital systems: Aadhaar, the Unified Payments 
Interface, DigiLocker, MeghRaj Cloud and Direct Benefit 
Transfer, which together form the backbone of digital 
governance in the country. The Committee further note that 

188 | P a g e 
 
flagship platforms such as UMANG, DigiLocker, e-Sign, API Setu 
and the Common Services Centres have institutionalised 
citizen-centric, paperless and interoperable service delivery, 
while UPI, DBT and the Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission have 
ensured that benefits reach citizens directly and efficiently.  The 
Ministry has submitted that digital technology has emerged as 
a key enabler of transformative governance in India, 
significantly enhancing transparency, efficiency, accountability 
and inclusivity, underpinned by a regulatory framework 
comprising the Information Technology Act, 2000, and the 
Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023, among other 
instruments. The Government has leveraged digital platforms 
with DPI to improve governance, public service delivery and 
citizen welfare.  
The Committee place on record their appreciation of the 
manner in which the Digital India programme has, over the 
course of a decade, embedded technology at the core of 
governance and transformed the delivery of public services 
across education, health, finance and welfare.  The Committee 
recommend that the Government should continue to build upon 
this foundation, extending the reach of these platforms to 
regions and population segments where uptake remains 
limited, and ensuring that the regulatory framework continues 
to evolve in step with these systems as their scale and 
functions expand. 
 

189 | P a g e 
 
Recommendation No 16 
 India Stack Global and DPI Model 
The Committee note that India's Digital Public 
Infrastructure has come to be recognised internationally as a 
model for inclusive digital development, anchored in three 
foundational layers: a verifiable identity layer through Aadhaar, 
a real-time payments layer through UPI, and a consent-based 
data exchange layer through DigiLocker. This architecture has 
been catalysed by the JAM Trinity of Aadhaar, Jan Dhan bank 
accounts and mobile connectivity. The Committee note with 
appreciation that DPI became a permanent fixture of the global 
agenda through India's own G20 Presidency in 2023, carried 
forward through the India-Brazil-South Africa Troika Joint 
Communiqué. The Committee further note that the Government 
of India has signed MoUs with 24 countries for cooperation on 
India Stack and DPI as of February 2026. The Global DPI 
Repository, proposed by India and endorsed by the G20, 
features 54 DPIs from 16 countries. UPI is now live in eight 
countries: the UAE, Singapore, Bhutan, Nepal, Sri Lanka, 
France, Mauritius and Qatar. The Prime Minister has announced 
the creation of a Social Impact Fund for DPI acceleration in 
Global South nations, with an initial Indian contribution of USD 
25 million. 
The Committee are gratified to note the international 
standing that India's DPI model, and platforms such as Aadhaar 
and UPI, has come to command, and also commend India's 
leadership in embedding DPI within the G20 agenda. The 

190 | P a g e 
 
Committee are of the view, however, that this recognition and 
the resulting demand from partner countries must be matched 
by adequate delivery mechanisms. The Committee, therefore, 
exhort that the Government review whether the funding 
available through the Social Impact Fund, and the institutional 
capacity supporting India's DPI partnerships, are sufficient to 
meet the scale of demand, and report to the Committee on the 
outcome of this review exercise within three months. 
Recommendation No. 17 
DPI And India’s engagement with the Global South 
The Committee note that India's DPI model has garnered 
immense interest and demand from Global South countries 
seeking to strengthen their own governance and public service 
delivery. Countries across the Global South have evinced great 
interest in replicating India’s DPI model including Aadhaar, 
DigiLocker, and UPI that has enabled seamless, faceless and 
cashless service delivery. The Ministry of External Affairs has 
informed the Committee that India regularly facilitates study 
visits by foreign delegations to key institutions such as the 
UIDAI and the NPCI, with recent visits coming from Sri Lanka, 
Kenya, Rwanda, Nepal, Indonesia and South Africa, among 
others. MoUs on India Stack and DPI cooperation grew from 22 
countries in November 2025 to 24 by February 2026, with 
Africa alone accounting for six, evidently at these countries' 
own initiative.  The Committee further note the Ministry's 
submission that India advocates a digital sovereignty model 
that enables partner nations to draw on global innovation while 

191 | P a g e 
 
retaining strict sovereign oversight over their own data and 
critical digital infrastructure, intended to address partner 
countries' concerns on data security, digital sovereignty and 
standards alignment when adopting Indian DPI solutions. 
The Committee are of the view that this demand-led 
interest from the Global South is a considerable diplomatic 
asset that must be nurtured with care, particularly as it extends 
to sensitive domains such as data security and digital 
sovereignty. The Committee recommend that the Government 
treat the concerns of partner countries on digital sovereignty as 
a matter requiring continuous engagement and ensure that the 
safeguards are applied consistently across all partner countries. 
Recommendation No 18 
Publicising India's DPI Success Stories as an Instrument of Soft 
Power 
The Committee note the Ministry's submission that, guided 
by the principle of Vasudhaiva Kutumbakam, India is sharing its 
DPI as a global public good, through instruments including the 
India Stack MoUs, the Global DPI Repository and the Social 
Impact Fund. The Committee, however, note that the evidence 
placed before it, both by MEA and MeitY, did not point to any 
sustained, coordinated effort to publicise the outcomes of these 
partnerships, whether within India or internationally. 
The Committee are of the view that the success story of 
India's DPI model, both at home and in partner countries, is not 
yet being told or shared as effectively as it deserves to be, and 

192 | P a g e 
 
that this success is itself a valuable instrument of India's soft 
power. The Committee advise that the Ministry of External 
Affairs and the Ministry of Electronics and Information 
Technology jointly develop a sustained communication strategy 
to publicise India's DPI achievements and the outcomes 
realised in partner countries, making use of Indian Missions 
abroad, international media and multilateral platforms, so that 
India's standing as a trusted digital partner is more widely and 
consistently recognised. 
Recommendation No. 19 
Leveraging ITEC for Global South Technology Cooperation 
The Committee note that its Indian Technical and 
Economic Cooperation (ITEC) programme has served as a 
means of strengthening partnerships and building goodwill 
with developing countries. The Committee observe that ITEC, 
as India's flagship capacity-building programme, is well placed 
to serve as a vehicle for technology-specific developmental 
cooperation with the Global South, including Africa, yet the 
evidence before the Committee did not indicate that ITEC's 
training and capacity-building offerings have been deliberately 
expanded or reoriented to reflect India's growing technology 
diplomacy priorities, such as Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI), 
AI and cyber capacity-building. The Committee wonder how 
courses structured as two-week immersive residential 
programmes can cater to the growing skilling needs of the vast 
population across the Global South. 

193 | P a g e 
 
The Committee are of the view that ITEC represents an 
existing and underutilised instrument through which India can 
deepen its technology partnership with the Global South, 
without the need for new institutional architecture. There is a 
growing need to review the ITEC programme. The Committee, 
therefore, recommend that the Ministry should deliberately 
expand and reorient ITEC's course offerings to include 
dedicated modules on Digital Public Infrastructure, artificial 
intelligence, cybersecurity and other emerging technologies, 
and complement the existing residential format with hybrid, 
online and in-country delivery models, so as to genuinely 
extend ITEC’s reach to the scale of the skilling need across the 
Global South. This expansion should be undertaken in a phased 
manner, with funding and staffing requirements identified at 
each phase, and ITEC should be integrated more closely with 
India's technology diplomacy strategy, so that it acts as a direct 
instrument of technology-driven developmental cooperation 
and is not merely relegated to a general capacity-building 
programme. The Ministry should report to the Committee on 
the reoriented course structure and delivery model.  
Recommendation No. 20 
Strengthening ITEC and CEIT as Vehicles for Global South 
Capacity Building 
The Committee note the Ministry's submission that there 
is tremendous interest among India's African partners in 
learning from India's experience of rolling out DPI at a scale of 

194 | P a g e 
 
over one billion people, and that this interest is channelled 
principally through two structured verticals: the Centre for 
Excellence in IT (CEIT) model, and institution-based training 
under the Indian Technical and Economic Cooperation (ITEC) 
Scheme. The Committee are of the view that these two verticals 
form the backbone of India's capacity-building offer to the 
Global South, and that their scale must keep pace with the 
growing interest now on record, particularly from Africa. The 
Committee recommend that the Government strengthen and 
expand the CEIT and ITEC verticals in keeping with this 
demand, and report to the Committee on the steps taken in this 
regard. 
Recommendation No. 21 
India as a Responsible Space Power 
The Committee note that the growing number of actors 
engaged in space exploration, has correspondingly increased 
the potential for conflict in outer space. To guard against such 
confrontation, the United Nations has developed five treaties: 
the Treaty on Principles Governing the Activities of States in the 
Exploration and Use of Outer Space, including the Moon and 
Other Celestial Bodies, or the Outer Space Treaty (1967); the 
Agreement Governing the Activities of States on the Moon and 
Other Celestial Bodies, or the Moon Agreement (1979); the 
Agreement on the Rescue of Astronauts, the Return of 
Astronauts, and the Return of Objects Launched into Outer 
Space, or the Rescue Agreement (1968); the Convention on 

195 | P a g e 
 
International Liability for Damage Caused by Space Objects, or 
the Liability Convention (1972); and the Convention on 
Registration of Objects Launched into Outer Space, or the 
Registration Convention (1975). The Committee further note 
that India's own space journey has been distinctive, beginning 
with developmental objectives and now extending well beyond 
these, with the Chandrayaan missions, the Mars Orbiter 
Mission, the Aditya-L1 solar observatory, and the forthcoming 
Gaganyaan human spaceflight programme establishing India as 
a nation capable of executing sophisticated and reliable space 
missions. India navigates these developments through a 
balanced and forward-looking approach, advancing national 
capabilities through ISRO while promoting inclusive 
cooperation with developing countries, with the Indian Space 
Policy, 2023, enabling private sector participation in the space 
economy, and bilateral projects such as the South Asia Satellite 
and NISAR demonstrating both technological strength and 
sustained diplomatic outreach. 
The Committee are immensely proud to note that India is 
increasingly recognised as a trustworthy, responsible and cost-
effective spacefaring nation, well placed to convert its 
technological advances into enduring international 
partnerships. The Committee are of the view that this standing 
is itself a diplomatic asset, particularly at a time when the 
growing number of new entrants into space exploration raises 
the potential for conflict and misunderstanding among 
spacefaring nations. The Committee, therefore, recommend 

196 | P a g e 
 
that India draw upon its standing as a State party to the four 
space treaties and a signatory to the fifth, along with its record 
of responsible conduct, to actively encourage newer and 
aspiring spacefaring nations to accede to and comply with 
these treaties, including through bilateral outreach, capacity-
building programmes, and India's voice in COPUOS. The 
Committee further recommend that this record of responsible 
conduct be woven more deliberately into India's broader 
diplomatic messaging and public diplomacy, so that India's 
example serves as an active instrument of persuasion in 
encouraging responsible behaviour among both established and 
emerging spacefaring nations. 
Recommendation No. 22 
Space Diplomacy Architecture 
The Committee note that India's space diplomacy is 
conducted through a distinctive institutional arrangement in 
which the Department of Space, including the Indian Space 
Research Organisation (ISRO) and the Indian National Space 
Promotion and Authorisation Centre (In-SPACe) exercise a 
degree of direct global engagement. ISRO's international 
cooperation functions, together with the commercial and 
international marketing activities now undertaken through 
NewSpace India Limited, give the space establishment a 
standing capacity to negotiate technical and commercial 
agreements, situated within India's wider bilateral and 
multilateral foreign policy. The Committee note that this 

197 | P a g e 
 
architecture is anchored within the Ministry of External Affairs 
by a single body, the Disarmament and International Security 
Affairs (DISA) Division, which deals with issues pertaining to 
space safety and space security and facilitates bilateral and 
multilateral space cooperation, working closely with the 
Department of Space, ISRO and IN-SPACe. Indian Missions and 
Posts abroad, as the first point of contact for foreign partners, 
receive requests for space cooperation through diplomatic 
channels and convey them to ISRO and the Department of 
Space, with the Division playing a facilitating role in setting up 
interactions between the two sides, negotiating agreements 
and MoUs, and conveying the Ministry's clearance and the 
clearance of its Legal and Treaties Division before the 
conclusion of any such document. 
The Committee are of the view that a division carrying 
responsibility for so consequential and fast-growing a domain 
as space diplomacy would benefit from institutional strength 
commensurate with that responsibility, particularly as the 
volume of cooperation requests, agreements and multilateral 
engagements continues to expand. The Committee, therefore, 
suggest that the Ministry should undertake a review of the 
staffing and resourcing of the Disarmament and International 
Security Affairs Division, with a particular attention to whether 
its current strength is adequate to handle the growing pipeline 
of space cooperation requests from partner countries, the 
negotiation of an increasing number of agreements and MoUs, 
and India's active participation across multiple multilateral 

198 | P a g e 
 
space forums simultaneously. The Committee also recommend 
that the Ministry consider whether closer, more structured 
coordination mechanisms between the Division, the 
Department of Space, ISRO, IN-SPACe and NSIL would help 
streamline the processing of cooperation requests, and report 
to the Committee on the outcome of this review and any 
measures taken in consequence.  
Recommendation No. 23 
Bilateral and Multilateral Cooperation on Space 
The Committee note that India has launched more than 
400 foreign satellites for over 30 countries through its cost-
effective and reliable space launch services. This record rests 
on measures including reusability, standardisation, 
indigenisation, and the involvement of Indian industry in 
manufacturing operational launch vehicles. The Ministry of 
External Affairs has also informed the Committee that a number 
of initiatives are being implemented to promote start-ups in the 
space sector, including the Antariksh Venture Capital Fund, the 
Technology Adoption Fund, and technical assistance provided 
through IN-SPACe. The Committee draw attention to the 
collaborative missions realised with international partners: 
Megha-Tropiques (2011) and SARAL (2013) with France, both 
of which outlived their planned life and provided valuable 
scientific data; and NISAR with the United States, launched in 
July 2025 as the world's first dual-frequency Synthetic Aperture 
Radar mission. Further missions are in the pipeline, including 

199 | P a g e 
 
TRISHNA with France and the joint lunar exploration mission 
LUPEX with Japan. The Committee note that India signed the 
Artemis Accords on 21 June 2023, becoming the 27th signatory, 
and that these joint missions have collectively helped build 
trust among partners and position India as a global hub for 
space activities. The Ministry has submitted that space sector 
reforms have enabled the growth of a commercial space 
ecosystem that will provide launch services to global 
customers. 
The Committee commend India's demonstrated record as 
a reliable and cost-effective launch partner, and the trust that 
these collaborative missions have built with major spacefaring 
partners across both the developed and developing world. The 
Committee are of the view that, as competition from private 
global players in the commercial launch market continues to 
intensify, India's commercial space ambitions would benefit 
from being underpinned by clearly articulated milestones. The 
Committee, therefore, recommend that the Government should 
set out a clear, time-bound roadmap for the expansion of 
NSIL's and those of Indian space start-ups commercial launch 
services to global customers. MEA should work with the 
Department of Space and IN-SPACe to facilitate regular 
international showcases and business matchmaking for Indian 
space start-ups through Indian Missions and relevant bilateral 
and multilateral platforms. These engagements should connect 
start-ups with prospective customers, investors and technology 

200 | P a g e 
 
partners, with structure follow-up to translate interest into 
commercial opportunities.  
Recommendation No. 24 
Strengthening the India-US Civil Space Joint Working Group 
The Committee note that India-US space cooperation is 
anchored by the interaction between the two countries through 
the Civil Space Joint Working Group. The space agencies of both 
countries, namely the Indian Space Research Organisation 
(ISRO) and the National Aeronautics and Space Administration 
(NASA), have expressed interest in continuing the strong 
cooperation built under NISAR, with new proposals discussed 
at the ninth meeting of the Group held in August 2026. The 
Committee observe that India-US space cooperation has gained 
substantial momentum, including the launch of NISAR in July 
2025 as a landmark joint Earth observation mission, the Axiom-
4 mission in June 2025, in which Group Captain Shubhanshu 
Shukla became India's first astronaut to visit the International 
Space Station, and the wider India-US TRUST initiative, 
encompassing cooperation across artificial intelligence, 
semiconductors, quantum technology, space and defence co-
production. 
The Committee extend their felicitations to Group Captain 
Shubhanshu Shukla on this historic achievement, which stands 
as a tall milestone in India's space journey. The Committee are 
of the view that a bilateral relationship generating milestones 
of this frequency and strategic consequence stands to benefit 

201 | P a g e 
 
from a working-group mechanism whose cadence keeps pace 
with the underlying cooperation. The Committee, therefore, 
suggest that the Ministry of External Affairs and the 
Department of Space explore increasing the frequency of the 
Civil Space Joint Working Group's meetings. This could be 
achieved through more frequent plenary sessions, or through 
the establishment of standing sub-groups on specific work-
streams that can meet independently between full meetings of 
the Group. 
Recommendation No. 25 
India's participation in the Artemis Accords 
The Committee note that India signed the Artemis Accords 
on 21 June 2023, becoming the 27 th signatory to a framework 
intended to shape the emerging rules of lunar exploration. 
Presently, India is not a participant in the NASA-led Artemis 
Programme itself. India has instead outlined its own ambitious 
vision for space exploration under its Space Vision 2047, 
including the continuation of the Chandrayaan series and the 
Gaganyaan programme leading toward the Bharatiya 
Antariksha Station. The Ministry has stated that ISRO will 
explore the possibility of deeper participation in future. 
The Committee welcome India's early and considered 
engagement with the Artemis Accords framework, and are of 
the view that India, having established its credentials through 
missions such as Chandrayaan-3's landing near the lunar south 
pole, is well placed to build further on this foundation as the 

202 | P a g e 
 
international governance architecture for lunar and cislunar 
activity continues to take shape. The Committee are of the view 
that clarity on India's intended trajectory within this 
framework, would strengthen India's position in shaping the 
emerging rules of lunar exploration and resource utilization.  
Recommendation No. 26 
Building on India's standing in Multilateral Space Governance 
The Committee are aware of India's long-standing and 
consistent advocacy for the preservation of outer space as an 
ever-expanding frontier for cooperative endeavours, and its 
opposition to the weaponisation of outer space. India has 
supported a legally binding instrument on the Prevention of an 
Arms Race in Outer Space (PAROS) since the issue was placed 
on the agenda of the Conference on Disarmament in 1982, a 
position it continues to reaffirm in the First Committee, the 
Conference on Disarmament, and the Open-Ended Working 
Group for PAROS. The Committee further note India's 
substantial technical contributions to international space debris 
mitigation efforts, through its engagement with the 
International Academy of Astronautics Space Debris Working 
Group, the International Astronautical Federation's Space 
Traffic Management Technical Committee, and the 
International Organisation for Standardisation's Working Group 
7, and its own ambitious domestic commitment, through the 
Debris Free Space Mission, to achieving debris-free space 
missions by all Indian space actors, governmental and non-

203 | P a g e 
 
governmental, by 2030. India presently chairs the COPUOS 
Working Group on the Long-Term Sustainability of Outer Space 
Activities. The Ministry has itself identified continuing gaps in 
global debris governance, including the absence of universally 
agreed rules for space traffic management and collision
