avoidance, the absence of mandatory satellite registration with 
contactable owner details, and the absence of any standardised 
mechanism for sharing orbital data. 
In the considered opinion of the Committee, India's 
standing across both these domains, as a founding member of 
COPUOS, a consistent voice for the peaceful use of outer space, 
the chair of a key COPUOS Working Group, and a nation with 
genuine and demonstrated technical credibility in debris 
mitigation, positions it exceptionally well to play an 
increasingly active role in shaping international consensus on 
these issues. The Committee, therefore, recommend that India 
should build purposefully on this standing. India should bring 
forward its own proposals or substantive contributions toward 
a legally binding instrument on PAROS. India should use its 
chairmanship of the Working Group on the Long-Term 
Sustainability of Outer Space Activities to advance stronger 
international cooperation on satellite registration and orbital 
data-sharing, building on the gaps the Ministry has itself 
identified. MEA in consultation with the Ministry of Defence 
should consider offering tracking and collision-avoidance 
capabilities developed under IS4OM in support of shared 
international space situational awareness efforts, and not only 

204 | P a g e 
 
for the protection of Indian assets. India should also extend its 
debris mitigation expertise and training to newer and aspiring 
spacefaring nations, particularly in the Global South, as a 
further dimension of its capacity-building diplomacy. The 
Committee would like to be informed about the progress made 
in this regard. 
Recommendation No. 27 
Network for Space Object Tracking and Analysis (NETRA) 
The Committee note that NETRA is an indigenous capacity-
building project envisaging the establishment of a network and 
systems for space situational awareness, consisting of three 
major elements: a Radar Observation System, an Optical 
Telescope Observation System, and an Space Situation 
Awareness (SSA) Control Centre. The Committee note that the 
establishment of an optical telescope at Hanle, Ladakh, is 
underway, and that efforts to establish an indigenously 
developed radar in north-eastern India are in progress. The 
Committee further note that IS4OM is operational and 
functions as the nodal entity coordinating India's space 
situational awareness activities, and that the Multi-Object 
Tracking Radar at Sriharikota currently tracks Indian rocket 
bodies and satellites, including POEM-4, during their last few 
orbits before atmospheric re-entry, wherever feasible. 
The Committee recognise NETRA as a foundational 
capability for India's independent space situational awareness, 
with a direct bearing on India's ability both to safeguard its 

205 | P a g e 
 
own space assets and to contribute meaningfully to the 
international debris and space traffic management efforts 
discussed in Recommendation No. 26 above. The Committee 
are of the view that the timely completion of NETRA's 
remaining components would strengthen India's international 
space situational awareness cooperation. The Committee would 
like to be apprised about the schedule for the completion and 
full operationalisation of the Hanle optical telescope and the 
north-eastern radar facility. 
Recommendation No. 28 
NavIC and the Sharing of Space-Related Data and Technology 
The Committee note that India is one of only six countries in 
the world with an independent satellite navigation system, and 
that Navigation with Indian Constellation (NavIC) is regarded 
as a tool of soft power showcasing India's indigenous capability 
in space. NavIC presently operates on three satellites, with a 
seven-satellite constellation under development. The 
Committee further note that discussions are underway with 
France, Russia, South Africa, Japan, the Republic of Korea, 
Australia and Nigeria on the establishment of ground stations 
that would extend NavIC's accuracy and reach beyond India. 
On the wider sharing of space-related data, the Ministry has 
informed the Committee that data from India's RESOURCESAT 
series and other satellites is widely used in the United States 
and Europe, including for applications such as weather 
forecasting. India's data-sharing portals, including Bhoonidhi, 

206 | P a g e 
 
Bhuvan, the Meteorological and Oceanographic Satellite Data 
Archival Centre (MOSDAC) and the National Information 
System for Climate and Environment Studies (NICES), make 
Indian satellite data available to the global community, in many 
cases free and open. 
The Committee commend the progress made on the NavIC 
constellation and the openness of India's data-sharing 
platforms, which the Committee regard as a genuine and 
under-recognised strength of India's space diplomacy. The 
Committee are of the opinion that the diplomatic value of 
NavIC will be further enhanced as the ground station network 
extending its reach is completed. The Committee, therefore, 
exhort the Ministry to set indicative timelines for concluding 
the ground station discussions presently underway with each of 
the countries concerned. The Committee further recommend 
that the Government should invest in more actively publicising 
the availability of India's open satellite data platforms to 
potential partner countries, including through Indian Missions 
abroad and international scientific and technical forums, so that 
this strength becomes as widely recognised internationally as 
the underlying data is freely accessible. 
Recommendation No. 29 
Deepening Space Diplomacy with the Global South 
The Global South is emerging as an increasingly contested 
arena of space diplomacy, with a growing number of 
established and rising spacefaring powers competing to build 

207 | P a g e 
 
partnerships across the region. The Committee note India's 
active space cooperation with African and Global South 
countries, including South Africa, Nigeria, Kenya, Tanzania, 
Mauritius and Egypt. This cooperation is being undertaken in 
support of India's Space Vision 2047 and forms part of  its 
wider Neighbourhood First and Global South-oriented foreign 
policy. The Committee observe that substantial capacity-
building is being extended through the Centre for Space 
Science and Technology Education for Asia and the Pacific and 
the Indian Institute of Remote Sensing. These two institutions  
have together trained more than 3,000 officials from over 100 
countries. Moreover, ISRO's UNNATI programme has trained 90 
participants from 45 countries in satellite building and testing. 
The Committee also note initiatives such as the India-Bhutan 
satellite, the South Asia Satellite, and the ongoing 
establishment of a ground station in Vietnam to provide remote 
sensing data to ASEAN countries. 
In line with India's wider positioning as a development-
oriented and trusted partner for the Global South, the 
Committee feel that such an extensive record of capacity-
building offers a strong foundation on which India's satellite 
launch cooperation and broader space partnership with the 
Global South can be further expanded. Given the growing 
presence of competing spacefaring powers in the region, the 
Committee are of the view that India cannot afford to rest on 
this foundation alone. The Committee, therefore, recommend 
that the Ministry, in consultation with the Department of Space, 

208 | P a g e 
 
should set concrete, time-bound targets for scaling up NSIL's 
Global South-oriented satellite launch pipeline and ISRO's 
capacity-building programmes, so that India's satellite 
cooperation with the region grows in step with its broader 
diplomatic and developmental partnership with the Global 
South.  
Recommendation No. 30 
Data and Technology Sovereignty in Foreign Satellite 
Authorisations 
The Committee note the Ministry's submission that IN-
SPACe has authorised Starlink to provide broadband services 
through its satellite system in India, following an inter-
ministerial process, and that considerations of data and 
technology sovereignty are being addressed by the Department 
of Telecommunications through service licence conditions. The 
Committee recognise that India's satellite communications 
sector is likely to see increasing participation from foreign 
operators in the years ahead, as the broader ecosystem for 
satellite-based connectivity continues to expand. The 
Committee are of the view that, as this sector opens further to 
foreign participation, it would be prudent and forward-looking 
for data and technology sovereignty safeguards to be placed on 
a clear, consolidated and published footing at the earliest stage 
of the authorisation process itself. The Committee, therefore, 
recommend that the Government should establish and publish a 
consolidated framework of data and technology sovereignty 
conditions applicable to the authorisation of foreign satellite 

209 | P a g e 
 
communication operators, in consultation with MEA, covering 
data localisation, lawful access, and technology transfer, as 
applicable. 
Recommendation No. 31 
Seizing the AI Momentum 
The Committee note that AI presents a transformative 
opportunity for a developing country such as India. AI is a key 
driver of India's digital economy, with strong potential to 
enhance productivity, innovation and service delivery, and is 
projected to contribute hundreds of billions of dollars, with 
greater long-term potential. India ranks third globally on the 
Stanford Global AI Vibrancy Tool, up from seventh the previous 
year, hosts approximately 16% of the world's AI talent, and 
ranks first globally in AI skill penetration. The Ministry has 
repeatedly emphasised AI's potential as a critical enabler of 
inclusive growth, capable of improving service delivery and 
expanding access to opportunity at scale, particularly when 
combined with India's Digital Public Infrastructure. Against 
this, the Ministries have themselves placed on record a 
substantial set of challenges: continuing gaps in high-end 
compute and advanced chip access relative to the United States 
and China; comparatively lower private investment in frontier 
technology and large-scale model development; AI 
infrastructure and data centre capacity that remain 
inadequately measured; the absence of a clear domestic 
adoption pathway for India's own foundation models; and the 

210 | P a g e 
 
absence of a national AI strategy that reflects the realities of 
the large-model era. 
While acknowledging that India's underlying potential in 
AI is considerable, the Committee are equally of the view that 
this potential has not translated into realised opportunity to the 
extent that it should have been. Technological transitions of 
this kind unfold quickly, and demand an equally quick 
institutional response. The Committee are concerned that India 
still lacks several of the basic foundations that would make it a 
genuine AI power: reliable, centrally measured data on its own 
infrastructure; adequate data centre and compute capacity; 
timely access to advanced chips; and a level of private 
investment in frontier technology and large-scale model 
development that matches the scale of India's ambition. The 
Committee are of the view that the gap between India's 
demonstrated capability and its realised leadership is not 
primarily a gap of resources or talent, but of follow-through, 
adoption pathways, strategic clarity, and the pace at which 
these foundations are being built. The Government should 
ensure that the opportunity AI presents for India's 
development is not left to be realised by default, but is pursued 
as deliberately. The Committee, therefore, recommend that the 
Government should respond to the situation as the moment 
demands and give utmost priority to closing the gaps in India’s 
AI journey. The Committee also recommend that sector-specific 
challenges be identified in coordination with other line 

211 | P a g e 
 
Ministries, private players and industry bodies and appropriate 
steps are taken to address them in a time-bound manner.  
Recommendation No. 32 
AI Infrastructure: Sustaining India's Distinctive Compute Model 
The Committee note the Ministry's submission that a 
robust AI ecosystem requires a combination of compute 
infrastructure, high-quality and diverse datasets, a skilled 
talent base, a research and innovation ecosystem, sound policy 
and governance frameworks, and adoption across sectors. 
Assessing the current status of India's AI ecosystem against 
these criteria, the Ministry stated that India ranks third globally 
in AI competitiveness, with AI skill penetration at two and a 
half times the global average, enterprise adoption at 
approximately 87%, and a tech and AI workforce of 
approximately six million professionals, underpinned by India's 
Digital Public Infrastructure and its large-scale datasets. MeitY 
acknowledged, at the same time, that challenges remain in 
advanced compute capacity, frontier research, private 
investment, and scaling adoption across industry. The 
Committee note that the Government has responded to these 
gaps through the IndiaAI Mission, the expansion of national AI 
compute capacity, the AIKosh datasets platform, the 
Application Development Initiative, Centres of Excellence for 
AI, support for the start-up ecosystem, and the promotion of 
indigenous AI models and language technologies, including the 
Vikram family of models, BharatGen and Bhashini. AI is now 

212 | P a g e 
 
being deployed across healthcare, agriculture, governance, 
finance, education and smart mobility. The Committee further 
note the Ministry's description of India's distinct approach to 
providing this infrastructure, which rests on the private sector 
building out data centres and GPU capacity while the 
Government contracts to make compute available as a 
subsidised service, at approximately Rs. 65 per GPU-hour 
against a global rate of two-and-a-half to three US dollars, a 
model the Ministry stated has been recognized by the United 
Nations, the World Bank and the Gates Foundation. The 
Ministry stated that India is not seeking to dominate the more 
expensive domains of infrastructure or model-building, and is 
focusing instead on the comparatively cheaper domain of 
building applications. 
The Committee commend the ingenuity of this compute 
model and the genuine cost advantage it has secured for India's 
innovators, researchers and start-ups. The Committee note that 
the Government is, in practice, pursuing all three layers of the 
AI value chain at once: building shared compute infrastructure, 
investing in sovereign foundation models through Sarvam AI, 
BharatGen and Gnani, and encouraging application 
development on top of both. The Committee are of the view 
that pursuing all three simultaneously is a reasonable strategy 
for a country at India's stage of development, but that it makes 
clarity on relative priority and resourcing all the more 
important, so that no single layer is under-resourced as a result 
of effort being spread across all three. The Committee, 

213 | P a g e 
 
therefore, recommend MEA work with MeitY and other line 
Ministries to develop a time bound plan for international 
partnerships that improve India’s access to advanced compute, 
support frontier AI research and attract investment into 
domestic AI infrastructure. The plan should identify priority 
partners and measurable outcomes while addressing export 
restrictions and risks arising from dependence on any foreign 
suppliers.  
Recommendation No. 33 
Regulatory Mechanism for AI: Balancing Domestic Growth with 
Global Responsibility 
The Committee observe that India's domestic regulatory 
posture on AI presently rests on the latitude available under 
existing laws, principally the Information Technology Act, the 
Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS), and the Digital Personal Data 
Protection Act, supplemented by the voluntary India AI 
Governance Guidelines, 2025. The Ministry has taken the view 
that these existing instruments should be used to the fullest 
extent possible before any AI-specific legislation is considered. 
Internationally, however, the Committee note that India has 
taken a considerably more assertive position. India has 
advocated for safe, secure, trustworthy and responsible AI at 
the G20, through the ‘Responsible Human-Centric AI 
Governance’ framework introduced during its 2023 Presidency. 
India has also accepted the UNESCO Recommendation on the 
Ethics of Artificial Intelligence, and supported the BRICS 

214 | P a g e 
 
Leaders' Statement on the Global Governance of Artificial 
Intelligence in 2025, while cautioning against prescriptive, one-
size-fits-all global rules that could disadvantage developing 
economies such as its own. The Committee are of the view that 
India's assertive advocacy abroad for safe and responsible AI 
governance should be matched by an equally credible domestic 
regulatory architecture with a view to ensuring that India's 
domestic regulatory credibility keeps pace with the global 
responsibility it has taken upon itself. 
Recommendation No 34 
 National AI Strategy 
The Committee note that NITI Aayog's national strategy 
for AI predates the emergence of large language and 
multimodal models, and that India's ambitions in this field have 
not been correspondingly revisited since. The Committee are of 
the view that a national strategy formulated before the large-
model era cannot adequately guide India's choices in it, 
however strong India's underlying position in talent and 
infrastructure may be. The Committee, therefore, recommend 
that NITI Aayog and MeitY, in consultation with MEA, 
undertake a revision of India's national AI strategy to reflect 
India’s domestic AI priorities as well as its international 
positions on AI governance, standards and technology 
partnerships.  
 
 

215 | P a g e 
 
Recommendation No. 35 
AI Literacy  
During the evidence, one of the experts drew a direct 
comparison between India's earlier digital literacy campaigns, 
such as the Pradhan Mantri Gramin Digital Saksharta Abhiyan, 
and the need for an equivalent effort on AI literacy. Agreeing 
with this view, the Committee observe that the risks associated 
with generative AI differ in kind from the risks that earlier 
digital literacy efforts were designed to address. While digital 
literacy chiefly concerned citizens' ability to use new tools 
safely, AI literacy requires citizens to exercise judgement about 
content itself, distinguishing genuine communication from 
convincingly fabricated audio, video and text, a skill that is 
harder to acquire and to catch. 
The Committee are of the view that the risks associated 
with generative AI, including deepfakes, voice cloning and AI-
generated misinformation, will fall most heavily on all citizens, 
and that India's considerable achievements in digital literacy 
should not be regarded as a substitute for a distinct and 
deliberate AI literacy effort. In the considered view of the 
Committee, the Government of India should explore the 
feasibility of launching a dedicated AI literacy campaign in 
manner of India's earlier digital literacy programmes. The 
Committee recommend that MEA should work with the 
concerned Ministries to facilitate global cooperation on AI 
literacy, including the exchange of educational resources and 
best practices for addressing deepfakes and AI-generated 

216 | P a g e 
 
misinformation and fraud. These partnerships should support 
domestic efforts to reach vulnerable groups and citizens with 
limited digital literacy. 
Recommendation No. 36 
Absence of Verified Data on India's Data Centre Capacity 
The Committee note that, when asked whether India hosts 
nearly 20% of the world's data while accounting for only 3% of 
global data centre capacity, MeitY candidly acknowledged that 
neither figure is officially verified, and that no official data on 
India's data centre capacity is centrally maintained by the 
Ministry, with the figures placed before the Committee drawn 
instead from industry estimates. The Committee note that this 
uncertainty exists notwithstanding the acknowledged scale of 
the challenge, with industry estimates suggesting that capacity 
may need to expand several-fold, to somewhere between 5 and 
10 GW, by 2030. 
The Committee are of the view that a domain as 
consequential to India's AI ambitions, and as capital and 
power-intensive, as data centre infrastructure ought not to be 
planned on the basis of industry estimates alone. The 
Committee, therefore, recommend that the MEA take up with 
MeitY and other concerned Ministries the need for systematic 
collection and publication of verified data on India’s data centre 
capacity, utilisation and growth and use this evidence to 
prioritise international investment and technology partnerships 
addressing identified capacity gaps.  

217 | P a g e 
 
Recommendation No. 37 
India's Data Centre Capacity Gap 
The Committee note that India hosts nearly 20% of the 
world's data, yet accounts for only 3% of global data centre 
capacity. This is a significant gap between the scale of data 
India generates and the domestic infrastructure available to 
store and process it. The Committee have also taken note of the 
steps being taken by the Government to address this gap, 
including the IndiaAI Mission's efforts to build AI-ready 
compute infrastructure, the facilitation of large-scale 
investment in data centre capacity, and the accordance of 
infrastructure status to data centres to enable long-term 
financing. The Committee note that this capacity has grown 
rapidly, from around 520 MW in 2020 to approximately 1.5 GW 
in 2025. It is projected to reach approximately 2 GW by 2026 
and over 8 GW by 2030. The Committee, however, observe that 
even this rate of growth requires capacity to more than 
quadruple within four years to meet the Government's own 
2030 projection. Major hubs are concentrated in Mumbai, 
Bengaluru, Hyderabad, Chennai and Delhi NCR, driven by 
connectivity, demand and policy support.  
The Committee are concerned that the present gap results 
in a portion of India's data being stored or processed outside 
the country, raising concerns of latency, cost and, most 
significantly, data sovereignty. The Committee are of the view 
that a gap of this scale, and the sovereignty concerns that flow 
directly from it, cannot be left to close on its own through 

218 | P a g e 
 
incremental, market-led growth. A well-coordinated planning is 
required to close it. The Committee also urge the Government 
to assess the extent of Indian data presently stored or 
processed outside the country as a consequence of this gap and 
its implications on our security and sovereignty. The Committee 
therefore recommend that MEA work with MeitY and other line 
Ministries to assess the strategic implications of overseas 
storage and processing of Indian data and technology 
partnerships that address verified domestic capacity gaps. 
Recommendation No. 38 
Data Protection and Security 
While cognizant of the fact that AI systems rely on large 
volumes of personal and sensitive data, the Committee observe 
that this raises the risk of data breaches, data privacy 
unauthorised access and misuse. MeitY has informed the 
Committee that these risks are being addressed through the 
India AI Governance Guidelines, 2025, and through an overall 
approach directed at building end-to-end safeguards, including 
secure data architectures, privacy-preserving technologies and 
strong cyber-security measures. The Committee suggest MEA to 
work with MeitY to advance India’s priorities on AI-related data 
protection in bilateral and multilateral engagements, including 
safeguards for cross border use of personal data and 
cooperation on breaches involving overseas entities should be 
informed by the application of its domestic data protection 
framework to AI systems.  

219 | P a g e 
 
Recommendation No. 39 
Sustaining Private Sector and Research Community's Role in AI 
Innovation 
The Committee recognise that India's progress in AI has 
been driven substantially by the initiative of its private sector 
and research institutions. MeitY has submitted that more than 
500 proposals were received from start-ups, industry and 
academic consortia for the development of indigenous 
foundation models. Academic institutions, including the IITs, 
IIITs and NITs, are independently running more than 13 
projects on AI safety, bias mitigation and explainable AI. More 
than 820 start-ups have been onboarded under the Technology 
Incubation and Development of Entrepreneurs (TIDE) 2.0 
scheme, with over 150 collectively raising more than 300 crore 
in investment. NASSCOM and industry bodies have, on their 
own initiative, built platforms such as the NASSCOM U.S. CEO 
Forum and evidence-based advocacy efforts to support India's 
technology industry internationally. The Committee are of the 
view that this is a private sector and research community that 
has, in substantial measure, been leading and driving India's AI 
efforts, rather than merely responding to Government direction. 
The Committee are of the view that the Government's task 
in respect of this ecosystem is not principally one of oversight, 
but of sustained enablement, and that its support should grow 
in step with the ambition this community has already shown. 
The Committee feel that the Government should treat the 

220 | P a g e 
 
private sector and research institutions as genuine partners in 
shaping India's AI momentum. The Committee recommend that 
MEA involve private industry and research institutions as 
partners in shaping India’s AI engagements, MEA should work 
with the concerned Ministries and Indian Missions to facilitate 
access to international research collaborations, investments 
and markets.  
Recommendation No. 40 
India-Specific Large Language Models: Learning from Asian 
Peers 
The Committee note that developing India-specific Large 
Language Models (LLMs) tailored to Indian languages, datasets 
and socio-economic contexts, presents significant strategic, 
economic and technological opportunities, particularly in 
strengthening domestic capabilities and enabling inclusive AI 
deployment. India has already launched sovereign AI models, 
including Sarvam AI's LLMs (30B and 105B), BharatGen's 
Param2, a multilingual model, and Gnani.ai's Vachana, a speech 
AI model, demonstrating growing domestic capability. The 
IndiaAI Foundation Models pillar aims to develop indigenous 
large and small language models, including multimodal 
systems, trained on Indian datasets and languages to support 
diverse national use cases. The Committee, however, note that 
MeitY has itself identified significant challenges to this effort, 
including limited access to advanced compute and high-end 
chips, comparatively lower private investment in frontier model 
development relative to global players, and continuing gaps in 

221 | P a g e 
 
large-scale, high-quality Indian-language datasets. The 
Committee note with interest the evidence before it regarding 
Singapore's SEA-LION model, built specifically for the 
languages and public-service needs of South-East Asia rather 
than to compete with global frontier benchmarks, and now 
adopted as the official language model of several countries in 
that region 
While appreciating the progress made, the Committee are 
unable to reconcile India's acknowledged strength in AI talent, 
and its early recognition of the strategic case for sovereign 
models, with the fact that India continues to lag behind global 
frontier developers in the scale and capability of the models it 
has so far produced. The Committee are of the considered view 
that India need not measure its achievement solely against 
parity with the largest global models and SEA-LION model 
offers an instructive alternative benchmark. The Committee 
strongly feel that domestic adoption and regional utility, 
including across South Asia, would be an equally valid measure 
of success, given that India's own languages, such as Bengali, 
Tamil and Hindi, already carry natural reach into Bangladesh, 
Sri Lanka, Nepal and Bhutan respectively. The Committee, 
therefore, suggest that India's success in this domain be 
measured against a clearly articulated standard of domestic 
adoption and regional utility, rather than against frontier model 
benchmarks alone. The Committee further urge the Ministry of 
External Affairs, in coordination with MeitY, to identify priority 
countries, particularly in the neighbourhood and the Global 

222 | P a g e 
 
South, for partnerships on Indian language and multilingual AI 
large language models, and AI capacity-building, with a view to 
strengthening India’s regional Technology cooperation.  
Recommendation No. 41 
Matching the scale of India's AI Skilling Effort to the Scale of 
the Transition 
The Committee note MeitY's assessment that AI is 
expected to reshape India's workforce through job 
reconfiguration rather than wholesale displacement, with an 
estimated 24% of tasks automated and 42% augmented, and 
with global estimates suggesting 92 million jobs displaced 
against 170 million created by 2030. NITI Aayog's analysis, 
cited by the Ministry, estimates that approximately two million 
jobs in India's IT sector may be lost to this transition, against 
four million new roles created in the development and 
deployment of AI applications and solutions. The Government 
has responded through IndiaAI FutureSkills, supporting 13,500 
scholars across undergraduate, postgraduate and PhD levels; a 
planned network of 570 Data and AI Labs, of which 30-plus are 
presently operational; the FutureSkills PRIME reskilling 
programme; and YUVAi for school-age engagement. The 
Committee note, by contrast, NASSCOM's evidence that India 
needs to train 8 to 10 million people for the AI era, since 
competence in AI will be required not only by those working 
directly in AI, but across most professions. 

223 | P a g e 
 
The Committee are of the view that there is a significant 
gap in scale between the workforce the Government's flagship 
skilling programmes presently reach and the scale of the 
training task that industry itself has identified. The Committee 
consider this gap especially pressing given that India's 
demographic strength, its large and youthful workforce, is 
precisely the asset that should allow it to lead the world in AI 
skilling rather than lag behind the scale of the challenge. The 
Committee, therefore, recommend that the Government set out 
a clear plan, developed jointly with industry bodies such as 
NASSCOM, for closing this gap within a defined timeframe. The 
Committee further recommend that the Government accelerate 
the rollout of the planned 570 Data and AI Labs, given the fact 
that only a small fraction is presently operational. 
Recommendation No. 42 
India's Comparative Standing in the Global AI Landscape 
The Committee note MeitY's evidence that the global AI 
landscape is shaped by a group of economies with 
complementary strengths: the United States in research, 
innovation and advanced AI systems; China in large-scale 
deployment and data-driven applications; and the United 
Kingdom, Canada, Germany, France, Israel, Singapore and the 
UAE advancing through targeted investment in research, 
governance and sectoral adoption, with India emerging as a 
key player on the strength of its talent, its Digital Public 
Infrastructure, and its capacity for scalable, inclusive 

224 | P a g e 
 
deployment. The Committee note MeitY's own account of where 
India's disadvantages lie relative to the leaders of this field: 
continuing gaps in high-end compute infrastructure, advanced 
chip access, and private investment in frontier technology and 
large-scale model development, set against the far greater 
resources and years of accumulated capability commanded by 
the United States and China. The Committee further note that, 
as per the Stanford Global AI Vibrancy Tool 2025, India has 
risen to third position globally in AI competitiveness, up from 
seventh the previous year. Alongside this material position, the 
Committee note MeitY's evidence describing India's distinct 
approach to AI governance, resting on a human-centric, "AI for 
All" philosophy, and the Ministry of External Affairs' evidence 
that India has consistently advocated, in forums including the 
G20, BRICS and the United Nations, for AI governance that is 
inclusive of the Global South and does not concentrate the 
benefits of AI among a small number of advanced economies 
and corporations. 
The Committee acknowledge India's rise in ranking as a 
genuine achievement, but are of the firm view that it should not 
be read as evidence that India is closing in on global leadership 
in AI. The distance separating India from the United States and 
China is considerable. It is a difference of kind and not merely 
of degree. At the same time, the Committee are of the view that 
India, as a responsible global player, has been a steadfast 
advocate of inclusive, democratic and ethical AI, consistently 
placing these principles at the centre of its engagement in 

225 | P a g e 
 
forums including the G20, BRICS and the United Nations. The 
Committee, therefore, recommend that the Government 
continue and strengthen India's advocacy for inclusive, 
democratic and ethical AI in international forums, as a distinct 
and permanent pillar of India's position, and pursue, with equal 
and urgent thrust, a substantially better-resourced national 
effort to close the material gap in compute, investment and 
frontier research that presently separates India from the United 
States and China.  
Recommendation No. 43 
India's Multilateral and Bilateral Engagement: From 
Participation to Agenda-Setting 
The Committee note the considerable range of India's 
multilateral and bilateral engagement on AI. India has 
consistently advanced a human-centric, trustworthy and 
responsible approach to AI at the UN, GPAI, the G20 and 
BRICS, and has accepted UNESCO's Global Recommendation on 
the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence. During its 2023 G20 
Presidency, India introduced a framework for "Responsible 
Human-Centric AI Governance," and, during BRICS in 2025, its 
advocacy for an inclusive approach was reflected in the BRICS 
Leaders' Statement on the Global Governance of Artificial 
Intelligence. Bilaterally, India has established dedicated 
mechanisms on digital and emerging technologies with partner 
countries covering AI standards, risk-based governance and 
capacity-building. On standards specifically, the Committee 

226 | P a g e 
 
note that India has become an active contributor to global AI 
standard-setting through the Bureau of Indian Standards, and 
hosted the 15th plenary and subgroup meetings of ISO/IEC JTC 
1/SC 42, the principal international standards committee on 
artificial intelligence, in New Delhi, bringing together more than 
350 experts from 70 countries. The Committee further note that 
India has secured a seat on the UN's Independent International 
Scientific Panel on AI, as one of 37 countries represented, and 
has offered IIT Madras as a Centre of Excellence in the UN-
supported network for AI capacity development. 
The Committee are of the view that this record shows 
India moving deliberately from being a participant in 
international AI governance to being, in specific instances, an 
author of its rules and standards, and that this shift deserves to 
be recognised and consolidated as a matter of policy, rather 
than allowed to remain the sum of several separate, well-
executed engagements. The Committee are of the view that 
India's hosting of the ISO/IEC standards plenary, its seat on 
the UN Scientific Panel, and its introduction of a governance 
framework during its own G20 Presidency are each, 
individually, instances of India shaping rather than merely 
accepting the rules that will govern AI globally, and that this 
pattern should be treated as a deliberate strategy rather than a 
series of discrete achievements. The Committee, therefore, 
recommend that the Government should adopt an explicit 
strategy for India's continued transition from a participant to 
an agenda-setter in international AI governance and standard-

227 | P a g e 
 
setting, identifying the specific forums, standards processes 
and negotiations in which India intends to lead rather than 
follow, and maintaining continuous Indian representation and 
technical contribution in bodies such as ISO/IEC JTC 1/SC 42 
and the UN Scientific Panel between major meetings, rather 
than concentrating engagement around individual high-profile 
events. The Committee further recommend that the 
Government report to the Committee on the specific outcomes, 
in the form of adopted standards, frameworks or governance 
principles, that this engagement has produced. 
Recommendation No. 44 
Safeguarding AI Inputs from Tariff related disruptions 
The Committee note that India’s AI ambition rest on 
continued, affordable access to critical AI inputs, including 
semiconductors, GPUs and related hardware, the bulk of which 
are imported. Given the growing use of tariffs as an instrument 
of technology diplomacy by major economies, the Committee 
recommend that the Ministry of External Affairs, in coordination 
with the Ministry of Commerce and Industry and MeitY 
proactively engage bilateral and plurilateral partners to secure 
tariff stability or preferential access for AI- critical inputs, and 
factor this risk into ongoing and future trade and technology 
dialogues. 
 
 
 

228 | P a g e 
 
Recommendation No. 45 
Global AI Divide 
The Committee note that global AI capability remains 
highly concentrated, with private AI investment in leading 
markets exceeding USD 100 billion in 2024, and high-end 
compute, cloud infrastructure and foundation models 
concentrated among a small number of advanced economies 
and large technology companies. The Ministry of External 
Affairs has informed the Committee that this concentration 
risks leaving developing countries with limited access to data, 
compute and technology, and correspondingly limited 
representation in global AI governance. The Committee note 
that India has consistently positioned itself not merely as an 
intermediary between advanced AI economies and the Global 
South, but as a member of the Global South in its own right, 
sharing the interests and priorities of the countries it seeks to 
represent. 
The Committee are of the view that India's standing as a 
genuine voice of the Global South carries real diplomatic 
weight, but that this weight must be exercised through 
concrete action and not advocacy alone. The Committee note 
that India has already demonstrated, through its DPI-sharing 
MoUs with 24 countries, that it is capable of translating this 
solidarity into a structured, resourced programme. The 
Committee further note that the United Nations system entities 
such as the United Nations International Computing Centre 
(UNICC) and the Office for Digital and Emerging Technologies 

229 | P a g e 
 
(ODET) deploy digital and technology solutions across sectors 
such as health, agriculture, education and payments well over a 
hundred countries, and that this multilateral channel offers 
India an additional route alongside its bilateral DPI sharing 
MOUs, through which Indian AI and digital solutions can reach 
Global South partner countries at scale. The Committee 
recommend that the Government should explore the possibility 
of extending a comparable AI resource-sharing programme to 
Global South partner countries, modelled on this DPI-sharing 
precedent, so that India's advocacy for the Global South on AI 
is matched by the same verifiable record of delivery as its DPI 
diplomacy. 
 
Recommendation No. 46 
Commending the Government for successful India AI Impact 
Summit 
The Committee note that the India AI Impact Summit 
2026, built on the momentum of previous multilateral 
initiatives, including the Bletchley Park, Seoul, Paris and Kigali 
Summits, marked a shift from aspiration to impact, 
demonstrating how AI can deliver tangible outcomes for 
People, Planet and Progress. The Committee note that this was 
the first summit of its series to be held in the Global South, and 
that the Ministry of External Affairs described its hosting as 
holding both political and symbolic significance from a foreign 
policy perspective. The Committee note the extensive 
preparatory process behind the Summit, beginning nine months 

230 | P a g e 
 
in advance with more than 550 pre-summit events conducted 
across 30-plus countries, and the scale ultimately achieved: 
participation from 118 countries, 22 Heads of State or 
Government, and more than 100 CEOs and CTOs of leading 
global AI companies, an India AI Impact Expo of over 900 
exhibitor booths against 35 in Paris, and a visitor footfall 
exceeding six lakh against approximately 1,500 in Paris. The 
Committee further note that the Ministry of External Affairs set 
out four specific strategic objectives ahead of the Summit: 
translating existing AI regulatory principles into actionable 
policy frameworks adoptable by countries at different levels of 
digital maturity; advancing safe, trusted and responsible AI 
with equitable access to computing power, datasets and skills; 
amplifying Global South perspectives so that developing 
countries feature as co-creators rather than mere adopters; and 
showcasing real-world AI use cases with measurable impact. 
The Committee note that 92 countries signed the Summit 
Declaration, against 58 in Paris, the United States and the 
United Kingdom, which had not signed the Declaration in Paris, 
did so here, and China, which had not been represented at the 
Paris summit at all, was represented at this Summit. 
The Committee commend the Government of India for 
the scale and ambition with which the Summit was conceived 
and delivered, and regard its successful hosting as a milestone 
in India's technology diplomacy, particularly given that this was 
the first summit in its series to be held in the Global South. The 
Committee are of the view that the insights gained and the 

231 | P a g e 
 
momentum generated from organising a summit of this scale 
should not be allowed to go in vain, but should instead be 
capitalised upon appropriately. The Government should 
continue to engage actively with the outcomes and institutional 
follow-through of this Summit, including the working groups 
and deliverables it produced, so that India's influence over this 
series continues well beyond the edition it hosted.  
 
Recommendation No. 47 
Outcomes of the Summit 
The Committee are pleased to observe that the India AI 
Impact Summit produced a wide-ranging set of outcomes 
spanning a global declaration, twelve technical deliverables, 
private sector commitments, and substantial investment 
pledges. The centrepiece was the adoption of the New Delhi 
Declaration on AI Impact Summit, endorsed by more than 92 
countries and guided by the principle of "Sarvajan Hitaya, 
Sarvajan Sukhaya." Alongside the Declaration, the seven 
working groups produced twelve further deliverables, including 
the Equitable AI Transitions Playbook, the Guidance Note on AI 
Governance, the New Deal for Data, the Charter for the 
Democratic Diffusion of AI, and the Framework for the Trusted 
AI Commons. Each of these deliverables was endorsed by 
between 19 and 25 countries and international organisations. 
The Committee also note that leading AI companies separately 
committed to advance real-world AI usage analysis and to 

232 | P a g e 
 
strengthen multilingual evaluation for low-resource languages, 
and the AI Impact Expo showcased more than 300 real-world 
AI solutions across healthcare, agriculture, logistics and 
climate. The Ministry further stated that the Summit catalyzed 
over USD 200 billion in AI-related investment commitments 
across compute, data centres and AI innovation ecosystems, 
and strengthened India's position in global AI supply chains, 
including initiatives aimed at securing semiconductors and 
critical minerals.  
Appreciating the breadth and ambition of these 
deliverables, and the scale of country endorsement as proof of 
India's immense capability of convening Summits of this scale 
in this domain. The Committee are of the view that the true test 
of the Summit lies in translating this diplomatic capital into 
durable gains, both abroad and at home. The Committee are of 
the view that, diplomatically, the Government should use 
India's standing as convenor and lead endorser of these 
deliverables to remain an active and visible participant in the 
working groups, review mechanisms and follow-up meetings 
through which each deliverable will be taken forward. In the 
considered view of the Committee, India's ability to fulfil USD 
200 billion in investment commitments, and on its stated 
ambition to secure semiconductor and critical mineral supply 
chains, will depend on the pace at which India's own compute 
capacity, data centre infrastructure and semiconductor base are 
built. The Committee, therefore, urge the Government to treat 
the domestic build-out of this capacity as inseparable from the 

233 | P a g e 
 
diplomatic ambition and ensure that India's domestic AI 
infrastructure keeps pace with the scale of the commitments it 
has secured globally. The Committee also desire to be apprised 
of outcomes and follow up on each of the deliverables to the 
India AI Impact Summit. 
 
Recommendation No. 48 
Sustaining the Gains from AI by HER and YUVAi 
The Committee note that, among the flagship events 
held at the main Summit, the Ministry of Electronics and 
Technology (MeitY) specifically identified ‘AI by HER,’ which 
focussed primarily on women, and ‘YUVAi,’ for participants in 
the age group of 13 to 20, as having attracted extensive 
participation. The Ministry has, however, described these 
events only in terms of the participation they generated, with 
no corresponding account of what has followed since, whether 
in the form of mentorship, funding, training pathways, or 
continued engagement with the participants involved. 
The Committee are of the view that flagship events of 
this kind ought to leave a lasting legacy beyond the Summit 
itself, particularly given the gender gap presently evident in 
India's AI workforce and the corresponding need to nurture 
young AI talent from an early stage. Keeping in view the fact 
that ‘AI by HER’ and ‘YUVAi’ cater to two groups of real and 
continuing importance to India's AI ecosystem, women and 
young people, the Committee are of the view that the 

234 | P a g e 
 
engagement and enthusiasm generated by these events should 
not be allowed to fade away with the end of the Summit itself. 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Government 
formally incorporate ‘AI by HER’ and ‘YUVAi’ into India's 
ongoing AI skilling framework. MEA should facilitate 
international global showcases and innovation exchanges 
through Indian Missions and global technology forums to create 
opportunities for Indian participants and attract overseas 
innovators, mentors, research institutions and investors to 
these initiatives. 
 
Recommendation No. 49 
Outcomes for the Global South 
The Committee note the Ministry's assessment that the 
Summit enabled the Global South to move from being rule-
takers to active contributors in global AI governance. From the 
perspective of the Global South, the Committee observe that 
the Summit delivered outcomes across governance, access to 
resources, capacity building and inclusive participation. These 
included practical policy tools such as the Equitable AI 
Transitions Playbook, the Guidance Note on AI Governance and 
the New Deal for Data. They also included private sector 
commitments to develop multilingual AI systems and improved 
benchmarking for low-resource languages. The Summit further 
showcased more than 300 real-world applications and 170 use 

235 | P a g e 
 
cases across healthcare, agriculture, education and financial 
inclusion. 
The Committee are of the view that this shift in framing 
reflects well on India's diplomacy, and is consistent with India's 
wider positioning as a voice of the Global South.  This standing, 
in the Committee's assessment, places a corresponding 
obligation on India to ensure that these outcomes translate 
into democratic sharing of AI governance, in which developing 
countries participate as equal partners rather than as recipients 
of frameworks designed by a few.  The Committee, therefore, 
urge the Government to treat broader and more equitable 
participation in AI governance as an ongoing obligation flowing 
from the Summit, by ensuring that countries of the Global 
South are given a substantive role in the continued 
development and revision of these frameworks going forward. 
The Committee may be apprised of the action taken and 
outcomes of such engagement with the Global South countries. 
 
Recommendation No. 50 
Monitoring Mechanisms 
In view of the numerous deliverables, commitments 
and outcomes emanating from the successful hosting of the 
Summit, the Committee note that India is adopting a 
structured, multi-layered approach to monitor and track their 
implementation. This approach rests on the Working Groups 
and Expert Engagement Groups continuing as implementation 

236 | P a g e 
 
and review platforms, on built-in monitoring approaches within 
individual playbooks and guiding principles, and on the IndiaAI 
Mission being leveraged to monitor domestic implementation. 
The Committee are of the view that a list of achievements is 
only as meaningful as the mechanism put in place to verify that 
those achievements endure. The Committee, however, observe 
that no specific monitoring mechanism has actually been put in 
place. The Ministry's replies merely make reference to periodic 
progress reviews and data-driven tracking mechanisms. 
The Committee are of the view that the Summit's 
considerable achievements ought to be matched by an equally 
stronger commitment to tracking their delivery, and that this 
commitment cannot rest on a monitoring architecture described 
only in prospective terms. The Committee, therefore, urge MEA 
to put in place a monitory framework to track the Summit's 
deliverables in terms of diplomatic outcomes.  
 
Recommendation No. 51 
Pax Silica 
The Committee note that, on the sidelines of the 
Summit, India became a signatory to the Pax Silica Declaration. 
Pax Silica is a US-led initiative launched in December 2025 to 
build secure and resilient supply chains for silicon and critical 
minerals underpinning semiconductors and AI infrastructure. 
India separately signed a Joint Statement on the India-US AI 
Opportunity Partnership as a bilateral addendum to it. The 

237 | P a g e 
 
Committee note the evidence of the Secretary, MeitY, that Pax 
Silica continues the cooperation India has received from 
previous US administrations, first under the iCET framework 
and more recently under TRUST. This cooperation is expected 
to support India's access to advanced semiconductor 
technology and critical minerals. The Committee further note 
the Ministry's evidence expressing interest in supply chain 
security arrangements that are not led by the OECD grouping of 
advanced economies alone. 
The Committee welcome India's accession to Pax Silica 
as a further deepening of the India-US technology partnership, 
and as a natural continuation of the iCET to TRUST trajectory. 
The Committee are hopeful that India's joining of Pax Silica will 
provide an opportunity to strengthen India’s supply chains, 
particularly in semiconductors and rare earths. In line with the 
Ministry's own assertion, the Committee also strongly believe 
that India, given its standing among developing economies, is 
well placed to pursue a supply chain security framework 
beyond the OECD grouping. The Committee will keep a close 
watch on the further progress of this technology alliance. The 
Committee also urge the Government to actively explore the 
feasibility of a complementary supply chain security initiative 
involving non-OECD and Global South partner countries, and to 
place before the Committee an account of the steps taken in 
this direction. The Committee recommend that MEA, in 
consultation with the concerned Ministries place before them a 
detailed assessment of India’s Commitments and expected 

238 | P a g e 
 
benefits under Pax Silica, including its implementation and 
monitoring mechanisms. The assessment should explain how 
participation will advance India’s technology and supply chain 
objectives, identify measurable outcomes and examine any 
conditions or dependencies that may affect India’s strategic 
autonomy.  
 
 
 
 
New Delhi                        Dr. Shashi Tharoor,               
22 September, 2026                                      Chairperson,                                      
31 Bhadrapada 1948 (Saka)          Committee on External Affairs

Ministry Of External Affairs 
 
2. At the outset, the Chairperson welcomed the Members and 
Officials to the sitting of the Committee, convened to receive a briefing 
by the representatives of the Ministry of External Affairs on the subject 
‘India’s role in Global Tech Diplomacy, Digital Governance, Outer Space 
Diplomacy, AI, and emerging innovations’. In his opening remarks, the 
Chairperson highlighted the importance of the subject in the context of 
India’s evolving foreign policy and its aspirations for global leadership in 
the digital and technological domains. The Chairperson also drew the 
attention of the representatives of the Ministry of External Affairs to 
Direction (55) of the Directions by the Speaker, Lok Sabha. 
3. In his opening remarks, Secretary (ER) informed the Committee that 
technology is a central pillar of diplomacy, governance, national security, 
and economic growth. India’s approach remains anchored in ensuring 
democratization of technology, capacity-building for the Global South, 
and safeguarding strategic interest through robust regulatory framework 
and partnership.  
4. Thereafter, the representative of the Ministry of External Affairs 
gave a comprehensive overview of India’s growing engagement in the 
technology domain. The presentation was structured around five key 
S. 
No. 
Name of the officer Designation 
1. Shri Sudhakar Dalela  Secretary (ER) [Leader of 
Delegation] 
2. Shri K.Nagaraj Additional Secretary (AMS)

themes. First, India’s bilateral engagements with partners, including 
initiatives such as the Transforming Relations Utilising Strategic 
Technology Initiative (USA), India-EU Trade and Technology Council, and 
India-UK Technology Security Initiatives. Second, India’s multilateral and 
plurilateral engagements, including its active participation in forums such 
as the United Nations (UN), G20, BRICS, and Association of Southeast 
Asian Nations (ASEAN). Third, India’s role as host of the AI Impact 
Summit in February 2026, including its pillars, themes, priorities, and 
seven working groups. Fourth, India’s space diplomacy, covering 
achievements such as Chandrayaan-3, the Axiom- 4 mission, and 
upcoming initiatives like the Gaganyaan Programme, Bharatiya Antariksh 
Station, and sustainable, debris-free space exploration. Fifth, India’s 
partnerships with the Global South through the India Stack Global 
Initiative, where countries are adopting frameworks like Aadhaar, UPI, 
DigiLocker , and CoWIN, demonstrating how India’s digital transformation 
can inspire global cooperation.  
5. Subsequently, Members of the Committee raised queries on a wide 
range of issues. These included the integration of technology into 
foreign policy priorities; promotion of an open, secure, and inclusive 
digital ecosystem; 2026 AI Summit for the Future; engagement with the 
UN Committee on the Peaceful Uses of Outer Space; investment in R&D; 
collaboration with the key AI players like USA, China, EU and public-
private partnerships to strengthen India’s AI ecosystem. Members also 
discussed digital public infrastructure, data sovereignty, collaboration 
with other countries on space technology, private sector participation in 
space sector, investment in research and development, and capacity 
building. Other issues raised included space debris mitigation, prevention 
of weaponization of space, India’s negotiating strategies for AI and 
digital governance, inter-ministerial coordination mechanisms, 
collaboration with the private sector while aligning technology with 
national security, competition with global technology leaders, 
coordination with State Governments, and balancing domestic 
technological growth with international responsibilities. 
6. In response, the representatives of MEA provided a detailed 
explanations and insights into all the issues raised. In conclusion, the 

242 | P a g e 
 
Chairperson directed that the Ministry furnish written replies on the 
points raised by Members during the discussion to the Secretariat at the 
earliest. 
The Committee then adjourned 
(The witnesses then withdrew)

2. At the outset, Chairperson welcomed the representatives of the 
MeitY, MEA, and NASSCOM to the sitting of the Committee convened to 
hear their views on the subject ‘India’s role in global tech diplomacy, 
digital governance, outer space diplomacy, AI and emerging innovations’ 
and drew their attention to Direction 58 of the Directions by the Speaker, 
Lok Sabha.  
 
3. Thereafter, the representative of the MeitY made a power point 
presentation before the Committee highlighting key multilateral and 
plurilateral platforms engaged by MeitY, such as India’s G20 engagement 
on AI, Global partnership on Artificial Intelligence (GPAI), AI Action 
Summit (Paris 2023), and India AI Impact Summit 2026. The India AI 
Impact Summit 2026 in India aims to democratize AI and shape the 
global discourse.  The Committee were also informed of the existing 
laws, such as Digital Personal Data Protection Act which can regulate 
many of the potentially harmful areas of AI, Right to Know, scale of the 
AI impact Summit, etc. On DPI, the Committee were informed that India 
has agreements with nearly 23 countries globally for DPI exchanges, 48 
bilateral agreements on sharing of cyber security on both DPI and other 
elements of digital governance.  
4. Thereafter, the representative of the MEA informed the Committee 
about the strategic objective of India AI Impact summit.  With regard to 
regulatory framework for AI, India is open to discussing global principles 
and cooperative mechanism of AI, however, India is employing a 
cautious approach against premature, rigid, treaty like obligations that 
could freeze innovation and startups.  The Committee were also 
informed of the role of India during upcoming BRICs presidency; 
position of India at plurilateral and multilateral settings such as UN, G20, 
BRICs, etc; maintaining strategic autonomy; deepening critical mineral 
cooperation with EU, US, Australia and Japan, etc.   
5. The representative of NASSCOM briefed the Committee on the AI 
Impact Summit, role of NASSCOM as knowledge partners to the Working 
Group on Human Capital, India’s positioning in the AI space, India as the 
talent hub of AI, building India Models, India as a trusted player in the 
whole AI space, etc. The representative of NASSCOM also informed the 

246 | P a g e 
 
Committee on the creation of NASSCOM U.S. CEO Forum which is a 
platform to discuss tech collaboration between both markets, particularly 
in areas of emerging technology; evidence-based advocacy in U.S. to 
showcase the contribution of Indian IT industry to U.S. economy, etc.  
With regard to expansion in developing countries, NASSCOM is 
encouraging companies to focus on Middle East countries, such as UAE, 
Saudia Arabia, Bahrain, Kuwait, etc. 
6.  The Members of the Committee, then, raised various queries 
related to  Pax Silica, effort to increase export of technology services to 
developing countries of Global South, inputs provided by NASSCOM to 
Government Ministries, outcomes of various cyber policy dialogues, 
impact of unilateral actions on India’s technology service exports, AI 
voice cloning, deepfake enabled financial crimes, opportunities and 
challenges posed by AI, AI model India contemplate, challenges to 
human values and instincts,  impact of AI on employment, environment 
impact of EV battery, debris in the space, post-MoU outcomes, gender 
bias in existing AI, AI in Africa, inclusive and human centric AI, limited 
influence of Global South in AI forums, national AI policy, Grok 
controversy, safeguards to ensure responsible innovation, risk of tariffs 
on AI inputs, AI patents, bringing back AI skills to India, utilisation of 
funds for developing AI ecosystem, collaboration with academic 
institutions, etc. 
7. The representatives of the MeitY, MEA and NASSCOM responded to 
the queries of the Members. The Chairperson directed that replies to the 
points raised by the members of the Committee which remained 
unanswered during the meeting may be furnished at the earliest. The 
Chairperson then thanked the representatives of the two Ministries and 
NASSCOM for valuable and insightful inputs on the subject. 
The Committee then adjourned 
(The witnesses then withdrew) 
A verbatim proceeding of the sitting has been kept on record.

2. At the outset, Chairperson welcomed the representatives of the 
Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology, and the Ministry of 
External Affairs to the sitting of the Committee convened to hear their 
views on the Outcomes of the recent AI Summit.   
 
3. Thereafter, the representative of MeitY made a Power Point 
Presentation before the Committee on the outcome of the recent AI 
Summit and informed the Committee about the strategic objectives of 
India AI Summit,  various pre-summit events held across the globe, 
Regional events and Working Group meetings, consultative & participating 
approach to Summit planning, demonstrating of the cutting edge of AI 
innovation, Research symposium on AI & its impact, participation of 
leading global voices on AI & Technology, key outcomes of Working 
Group meetings,  investment announced at Summit, etc.   
4. The representative of MeitY also informed the Committee of some of 
the key achievements of the Summit,  such as it was organised as the 
largest global summit on AI attended by about 118 countries and 6 lakh 
attendees, India AI Impact Summit Declaration signed by 92 countries 
and international organisations which is the highest number of signatures 
to any political declaration on AI, commitments from 13 leading global 

249 | P a g e 
 
and Indian frontier model developers to promote greater understanding 
of AI usage and its impact, investments commitments exceeding USD 250 
billion across the AI value chain,  Guinness World Record for the AI 
Responsibility Campaign, etc.  To demystify AI and provide hands on 
exposure a large number of students participated in the Summit.  Nearly 
2,000 rural women from Self-Help Groups attended and explored how AI 
could benefit them. 
5. Thereafter, the representatives of the Ministry of External Affairs (MEA) 
briefed the Committee covering various issues, such as Global South 
anchored agenda, emphasised on democratic and human Centric AI, 
leadership position of India in AI, approach of US & EU to AI, moral and 
ethical AI, enhanced voice of India in setting norms and standards, etc. 
6. Members of the Committee then raised various queries on issues 
related to implications of the Summit for the country, steps being taken to 
ensure India’s leadership in AI, data sovereignty,  building AI 
infrastructure in India, cooperation among developing countries, 
structured mobility frameworks  to facilitate the movement of Indian 
professionals, H1-B Visa, integration of the outcome of the Summit with 
India’s greater tech diplomacy,  India’s participation in Pax Silica, global 
AI governance, lessons learned from the AI Summit, impact of AI on 
employment, etc. 
7. The representatives of the MeitY, and MEA responded to the 
queries of the Members. The Chairperson directed that replies to the 
points raised by the members of the Committee which remained 
unanswered during the meeting may be furnished at the earliest. The 
Chairperson then thanked the representatives of the two Ministries for 
valuable and insightful inputs on the subject. 
The Committee then adjourned 
(The witnesses then withdrew) 
A verbatim proceeding of the sitting has been kept on record.

2. At the outset, the Chairperson extended a warm welcome to the 
Non-official Witnesses/Experts to the Sitting of the Committee convened 
to hear their views on the subject ‘India's role in global tech diplomacy, 
Digital Governance, Outer Space diplomacy, AI and emerging 
innovations’ in accordance with Rule 331L of the Rules of Procedure and 
Conduct of Business in Lok Sabha. He clarified that the Committee 
would confine itself to the international and diplomatic implications of 
developments in the field technology, rather than sector-specific policy 
recommendations. The Chairperson also drew their attention to Direction 
(55)1 of Directions by the Speaker, Lok Sabha pertaining to maintaining 
the confidentiality of the proceedings.  
3. Thereafter, the Committee heard the oral submissions of the 
witnesses.  The experts apprised the Committee of their insights and 
perspectives on a wide range of topics and dimensions concerning the 
subject. These inter alia  included national AI strategy; commitments 
made at the India AI Impact Summit; India’s Digital Public 
Infrastructure; growing concentration of AI stack in few countries; H 1B 
related challenges; AI literacy campaign; NASSCOM’s country-specific 
strategies for market expansion beyond the United States; Singapore’s 
SEA-LION model, India’s compute infrastructure; India’s space diplomacy 
and so on. 
4. The members of the Committee then raised various queries, 
including: whether AI investment constitutes a bubble comparable to the 
dot-com era; the need for an ' Indian model of AI ' on the lines of 
Aadhaar and UPI; the likely impact of AI on employment; the proposed 
US FCC rulemaking on offshoring and its potential impact on India's IT 
and BPM exports; India's relative standing vis-à-vis countries such as 
Japan, Korea and Taiwan in global AI calculations, etc. The experts 

252 | P a g e 
 
provided detailed responses to the queries raised by the Committee 
members, contributing to a robust and insightful discussion.  
5. Before the Sitting concluded, the Chairperson, on behalf of the 
Committee, expressed profound gratitude to the witnesses for their 
invaluable contributions to the deliberations on this subject matter. The 
experts were requested to submit any additional written submissions, 
that they may wish to place before the Committee, to the Secretariat, 
for the Committee’s consideration. 
The Committee then adjourned 
(The witnesses then withdrew) 
A verbatim proceeding of the Sitting has been kept on record.
